Cyber Insurers See New Divide: AI Defense 'Haves' &amp; 'Have Nots'

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Dark Reading Confidential Episode 23: Cyber insurers share their view of the AI-driven threat landscape and how mounting challenges are putting pressure on both sides of the policy, leaving the current model unsustainable.

Cyber insurance providers are approaching 2027 facing a fundamental challenge. Claims rose 40% in 2024 and another 20% in 2025, even as premiums have stayed largely flat. This trend is something both Coalition and Travelers say isn't sustainable; and at the center of that shift is AI, which is reshaping the threat landscape not by inventing new attack types, but by making existing ones — ransomware, business email compromise, and phishing — dramatically easier to execute at scale.

In this episode of Dark Reading Confidential, Dark Reading media and projects director Becky Bracken and Dark Reading senior news director Rob Wright sit down with Lauren Winchester, vice president of cyber-risk services at Travelers; and Daniel Woods, head of emerging risk at Coalition, to unpack what the data actually shows. The conversation moves past the headline-grabbing surge in disclosed vulnerabilities to a more nuanced picture: exploitation is rising, but not as sharply as disclosure volume, while AI-powered phishing kits are bring sold SaaS-style to criminals with little technical skill, driving a measurable spike in BEC and ransomware activity.

Related:US Cyber Director Pick Signals Shift Toward Expertise

The two insurers also agree on where the real exposure lies: not necessarily with large enterprises, which have the resources to track threat intelligence and patch quickly, but with small and mid-sized businesses that often lack an in-house IT team altogether. That awareness gap, they argue, is where insurers now have to step in — not just to underwrite risk, but to actively help policyholders defend their organizations against it.

This transcript has been edited for clarity and length by Informa TechTarget's internal AI assistant. For the full experience, please listen to the podcast or watch the video.

Dark Reading's Becky Bracken: Hello, everyone, and welcome to Dark Reading Confidential, a podcast from the editors of Dark Reading, where we bring you real-world stories straight from the cyber trenches. My name is Becky Bracken. I'm an editor with Dark Reading, and I am thrilled today to do just that: deliver as promised, along with my colleague Rob Wright. Today we are going to be talking about all things cyber insurance and AI. In addition to my very smart colleague Rob Wright, who I mentioned, we are thrilled to welcome Lauren Winchester, who is vice president of cyber risk services at Travelers Insurance, and of course Daniel Woods, who is the head of emerging risk at Coalition. Welcome to both of you. Thank you.

Related:Behind the Alias: How One Researcher Unmasked a Decade of Ransomware Actors

DR's Becky Bracken: This conversation got started actually in one of our editorial meetings, and Rob had the smartest take, and then won the dubious distinction of getting to lead the conversation, which is always a problem around here when you have a smart idea. So, I am going to get out of the way and let Rob explain some of his very smart thinking around this topic, and get you guys in on it.

Dark Reading's Rob Wright: It wasn't a smart idea, but it was a question about what is happening with cyber insurance now that we have all this AI stuff going on. Thank you for joining me on this. We'll start with a basic question about where the cyber insurance industry is right now today in 2026, going into 2027. Just broad strokes. I know it's still somewhat of a new market, but where do you feel like it is in terms of overall health and growth? Daniel, why don't you kick us off?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: So I think the best data source on the state of the market comes from the National Association of Insurance Commissioners, who publish an annual report about the US market, and that shows this paradoxical situation where the number of policies being issued are growing, but the number or the amount of premium being collected is pretty much stable.

Related:In Security, Words Matter as Much as Tools — Maybe More

We also see claims are increasing; they rose 40% in 2024, 20% in 2025. So I think the cyber-insurance market is beginning to ask whether prices are sustainable, especially given some of the changes we'll discuss today related to AI.

DR's Rob Wright: And Lauren, what's your take on what the industry is today?

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: I think we're at a period of uncertainty right now, as most industries are. AI is likely to change a lot of the ways that we work, a lot of the ways that threat actors engage with companies and attack companies, and ultimately that uncertainty is something the industry is grappling with.

DR's Rob Wright: So I know I'm a bit of a pessimist among my Dark Reading colleagues. I try not to be pessimistic, but obviously AI has had a huge effect on the cybersecurity landscape this year. Ever since Project Glasswing, Mythos, and the tsunami of reported vulnerabilities, disclosed vulnerabilities, increases in zero-days. That's really put a lot of companies, a lot of vendors, and enterprises on the defensive. So, what effect is that having?

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: I think it's important not to conflate the surge in vulnerabilities being disclosed with a surge in attacks that drive cyber claims, at least not just yet. As many of your listeners will know, not all vulnerabilities are created equal, and so certainly there are thousands more vulnerabilities being disclosed in the months since Mythos preview came out, as compared to earlier in the year, and that's going to have an impact. And you have to look at the high-severity and the critical bugs within that group, and then you have to look at what's actually being exploited as well.

If we want to look at what's a good stand-in for exploitation, you can look at CISA's known exploited vulnerability list. There's a little lag there, but ultimately, if you look at the KEVs, you can see an increase that's happening, but it's not to the same order of magnitude as the overall CVE and vulnerability disclosure. But certainly, there is an increase. I think we just looked at this recently through Sept. 30, and CISA had added 246 KEVs in 2026, which exceeded the 245 added in total for 2025 when we looked at it. So naturally, we are on a trend to exceed last year.

We think maybe it'll be about a third more than last year, when we get to the end of the year, not a trend you want to see, but it's not in the same almost-exponential curve you're seeing with the total vulnerabilities. And I don't mean to be like everything's fine, right?

We also put out a report yesterday, and we look at ransomware leak site data as well as potential indicators of activity, and that's where ransomware threat actors are posting on their Dark Web leak sites who their victims are, and ultimately for Q3 2026, that activity was up. We had 111 distinct groups posting victims. We had a record number of 2,882 victims on leak sites, which is 26% up from the prior quarter and our highest quarter that we've ever seen. So certainly, the activity with the financially motivated threat actors is picking up, and we've also seen that in our business email compromises (BECs) as well, so it's painting a picture of more activity for sure. But just good to note that increased volumes don't necessarily mean increased tax immediately.

DR's Rob Wright: Gotcha. And I'll just say you are allowed to be positive on this podcast. I am going to actually implore you guys to give me some good news as we get deeper into this conversation because I always try to leave these discussions on a positive note, on a high note, rather than doom and gloom. So, we'll see if Daniel brings the doom and gloom now.

Coalition's Daniel Woods: So to bring a bit of gloom, I'd say the conversation also needs to look beyond just vulnerabilities and exploits. AI is a general-purpose technology, and it impacts not just the exploitation of vulnerabilities, but other mechanisms.

So Lauren mentioned an increase in business email compromise, which we've also seen, and we attribute this to in February of this year, a new AI phishing kit was released, EvilTokens, which was basically like a SaaS business model, and they weren't exploiting vulnerabilities. They were using AI to create convincing phishing lures, either by email or often replica websites. They would manipulate the user into letting the attacker in, even if MFA was enforced through either adversary in the middle and phishing, or device code phishing, and then the platform would get access to the mailbox, create mailbox rules, and start to pass through and identify sensitive data.

So this is a way that AI was used at multiple steps. It caused an increase in claims, but it's not actually related to the vulnerabilities that Lauren and you were just talking about. A perfect example. We also attribute that increase in BEC to those tools being made available to threat actors. They are truly too easy to use, unfortunately. EvilTokens was sold as a SaaS business model. So one sophisticated group of criminals built the technology, and they allowed tens, hundreds, thousands of criminals without those skills to use it. And a blockchain tracking company found that they earned $1.1 million in revenue from other criminals buying their services. So, I think this is what Coalition is really worried about when cybercriminals adopt AI into their business models.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: We see that in the ransomware ecosystem, where there's ransomware-as-a-service (RaaS), and so part of what we're looking for is when will the tools become even further identified, and to what extent, who will be doing that? Will it be the ransomware developers? How will that actually get disseminated into the ecosystem? There's a little bit more separation between the original, the victims, and who actually ends up doing the attack at the end, because of the access broker economy and what have you. So, I agree with Daniel. You have to track how these different tools are getting used, and to what extent it has changed the ecosystem.

DR's Rob Wright: So I feel like we're trending in a direction that's less positive. That's OK. But going back to the vulnerabilities, do you feel like customers, like the enterprises out there that are buying insurance, are they aware of what's going on with the threat landscape? Do they know about the surge in reported vulnerabilities, and that the patching windows have decreased? That stuff is getting exploited a lot faster these days. That the risk is generally higher, and that there's a much bigger importance to promptly patching systems. Do they understand that, and do you see them acting on it?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: I would say there's a big range of customers. So, of course, at the high end, you have in the enterprise market customers with a team of threat intelligence specialists who don't really need an insurer to tell them about vulnerabilities being exploited. I think where you really see an awareness gap is small businesses. Many of them don't even have an IT team; they have a website some third-party developer built, and then you see something like the PHP vulnerability in that system, and that's where I think the onus is on the insurer to actively notify those customers that there is a new exposure for them. And that's where, in particular, what Coalition offers is support to actually help them patch those systems, because often for the very smallest customers, they don't have the capabilities to do it.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: That's very well put. I think we're definitely doing our darndest to get the news out to our policyholders at Travelers because that is so important. We similarly have a book that runs the gamut from small businesses and mid-sized businesses, and then large enterprises, and the large enterprises are very aware. With middle market and small companies, I do think the work that we've done and other insurers have done to start that engagement before all of this began will hopefully pay off to some extent.

With our cyber-threat alerts, we're letting them know about any of these new emerging exploits, and then certainly in all of our monthly newsletters and quarterly webinars. This is all we can talk about, is how much faster companies need to be patching, and that their 30-day patch windows or SLAs with their vendors are not going to cut it in this new paradigm. They need to be looking to patch within three days, one day, even for certain types of exploits.

But it is interesting you mentioned PHP, not to have a side here, Daniel. But I find that one so fascinating, because the prevalence on the Web. A full 70% of websites could have PHP as a language within them. What do you do with that? You let everybody know about this vulnerability. You encourage everyone to patch, and I actually totally encourage all the press and the news around it because people were patching more quickly on this vulnerability. But turns out it's been pretty hard to exploit so far, right? And so that's a perfect situation for everyone. Something well-known, pervasive, get it fixed, and fortunately beat that exploitation.

DR's Rob Wright: In addition to shrinking patching windows, faster exploitation, and threat actors using AI to build more effective phishing and BEC attacks … what other risks or major threats do you see your clients facing today, as it pertains to AI?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: So, I would start with the rise of open source pen-testing frameworks like Artex. This was developed by a Chinese researcher, and it was actually later taken down because the researcher realized how much it was being abused. But what we've seen is this gives, very unsophisticated actors the ability to draw on more sophisticated pen testing tools that have many agents working in parallel to exploit publicly facing systems. What this pen-testing framework is able to do is test systems, test plug-ins, use a range of tools, and basically throw everything at the system and try every avenue, and the costs are so cheap.

For me, the really worrying thing here is often it's just people playing around. They don't even have the rationale on how to make money from this, and yet from a cyber-insurance perspective, you still have to respond to the claim if they steal data, which often happens. You still have to notify those customers. You'll face a lawsuit. So you have just chaos at the moment with these agentic hacking tools being used to just compromise so many different organizations.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: And it's that experimentation that's happening right now. Once the financially motivated threat actors figure out how they want to harness that type of tool, that's when it becomes more concerning. I think right now we're in the early stages of it. That's translated into not seeing much examples of Artex used for ransomware yet, and they tend to be a little noisier right now, so to the point of the large enterprises who are really on top of things, they're going to identify that very quickly within their systems because it's so noisy.

But these tools are only going to get better, and it's something that looks for the existing vulnerabilities that are already out there, the existing exploits, and that's something that every corporation struggles with already. So again, back to the things we've been preaching for so long: needing to make sure you have good vulnerability management and patch management. That's how you combat these new tools. They're not doing net new ways to attack necessarily. They're using existing methods. They're just agentified and moving fast.

But the other risk, if we're talking about other risks from AI, I look at the inside threat of AI as well. here's a real gap between those that know they're adopting AI and those that have AI governance in place, and I think that's really huge. Whenever you have a technological step-change like AI is, adoption is going to get out ahead of governance, and that creates this risk. There's a lack of visibility for a lot of small businesses and middle-market businesses as to how employees are using these tools. There's a lack of accountability. There's a lack of governance. And we're not yet seeing claims and droves that result from this governance gap. But it's happening, and it's creating this risk, and so you can see how companies are going to start to have employees who put sensitive data into an unsanctioned AI tool.

We know that's happening, and that's going to create a potential data breach, depending on what type of information they're putting into the tool. So that's something that when we're engaging with policyholders right now, we're talking to them about vulnerability management, but we're turning around and trying to assess their actual governance risk as well.

DR's Rob Wright: Daniel, are you seeing similar trends in terms of just not necessarily shadow AI usage, but lack of AI governance?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: So, I for sure agree with Lauren. There's a bunch of organizations out there using unsanctioned tools. I think to Lauren's point, there's a bunch of data breaches happening that just will never be reported. They will never end up with a breach attorney who says your notification requirements are X, Y, and Z. I think at the moment, Coalition is more sanguine about that risk just because it's remaining in the shadows for now. I think we're really focused on the business email problem because that's causing the spiking claims at the moment, and also how organizations are going to deal with these open source pen-testing frameworks.

DR's Rob Wright: So a question about agentic AI. I've talked with some companies who are trying to find ways to use AI for good, trying to find vulnerabilities within their products, and develop patches in some cases. I spoke with Ivanti about this recently. Do you guys think that that's a realistic path? Have you, or is that a possibility? Like that we may be able to use some of this technology to remedy vulnerabilities, find vulnerabilities, and fix them before the attackers get them, or is that a long way off?

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: I think that's been happening. There are a lot of tech companies that are out ahead of the attackers because they've been running frontier models against their code bases, and then there are also companies that have been running against open source code, right? So there's a plethora of work happening right now to shore up code to ship new patches.

I think one of the problems, though, is for the companies that don't have massive teams who can work on establishing patches. You're told about a new vulnerability. How do you turn around and action that and ship a patch that doesn't have more bugs, right? And so I think that's the spiral we're in right now. There's a bit of a bottleneck, and then companies who are consumers of those tech products then have to do the patches in a lot of cases if it's not a simple cloud update. So, I see this bottleneck happening right now, but it is certainly out ahead of attackers.

That said, some of the models that are out there and in use by attackers now are more sophisticated. So we're at that crux point now, where there are models out there that threat actors can use to identify vulnerabilities and create exploits. And the clock's been ticking, and we're at that intersection.

Coalition's Daniel Woods: Exactly to Lauren's point, there will always be another model that can find new vulnerabilities. Something we're concerned by is, there's been a recent spate of outages. Of course, everyone heard about Microsoft last week. The FAA had an outage, and there's noise that potentially the speed at which organizations are now having to develop and deploy patches is leading to some of these outages. Just because you have to test all of them, and eventually something goes wrong. You test one configuration, then it's pushed to a different server, and that's what causes the outage.

So, although potentially defenders will be able to stay ahead in terms of patching the vulnerabilities, the big concern for cyber insurance is that leads to reliability issues that cause systems failure and business interruptions, which we also cover. So you might solve the security risk, and increase the outage risk.

DR's Rob Wright: I guess the thing I worry about again, and I am a pessimist, so take this with a grain of salt, but is the number of organizations out there that generate the code with AI tools, and push it live without not a lot of human review or really any human review.

And I worry about a future where we're going to have Patch Tuesdays with not just 900 or 1,000 vulnerabilities, but 2,000 or more, and we're just going to be running the code through these AI tools and pushing them out because the sheer number is just too big, and Daniel to your point, not having the quality in place to effectively fix the vulnerability, and also stuff that might screw things up, and you might have outages. That's the panic mode in my brain. Is that something that you guys are worried about, or do you think that we might have the human capability, the human workforce to actually review these patches and the code, and get them in a place that they should be, rather than just pushing them out willy-nilly?

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: I think it's important to not talk about it too broadly, right? Tech companies creating tech have an obligation, a responsibility to have software development lifecycles, so it's one thing to be running AI against the code to test it for vulnerabilities and identify them. You need to make sure you have the proper harnesses in place and the proper engineers and humans overseeing that process, and then you want to run it still before it ships to look for vulnerabilities. You need that life cycle. If they're not doing that, that's creating a real problem. But responsible companies who are creating tech responsibly still have their rigorous software development life cycles in place.

Coalition's Daniel Woods: And I think where I would take that is a similar direction. The cyber insurance industry has seen a huge increase in claims related to SSL VPNs provided by a handful of providers who built them in legacy, proprietary operating systems, they never rewrote those that codebase with modern secure software engineering models. And honestly, I think that business model will always be stuck in this reactive cycle. I think what the insurance industry needs to move toward, and it's something Coalition is doing, is shifting organizations toward a more modern approach to developing software, where you build security in early in the process.

So, for instance, a huge amount of vulnerabilities are related to how memory is managed. There are by now completely memory-safe programming languages that vendors can use to build their systems. So, I think the insurance industry needs to move toward rewarding policyholders who are using more modern technology sets. So, we escape the patch cycle because it's just not sustainable to be telling people at 5 p.m. on a Friday you need to patch your systems. Our staff get exhausted from this because they stay up all night notifying people, and then also the people receiving those emails are just as exhausted.

DR's Rob Wright: You mentioned VPNs. I wanted to ask you guys: so obviously, edge devices have been under attack now for a long time, and there have been specific companies and products that have been favorite targets of threat actors. Lots of vulnerabilities, zero-days, etc. Are carriers or the insurance companies approaching a point where you guys might say to a customer, you know what? If you have this in your environment, the risk is too high for us, and just walk away or say that it's a no-go because of certain edge devices or edge products in that environment? Or are we not there yet?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: So I would say, for the vast majority, you can build compensating controls in. So we know SSL VPNs are not great. What we would do is work with the customer to update the firmware to the latest version to enforce multifactor authentication or MFA, and then what we would also ask is whether they have running behind that, a modern managed detection and response solution. So if something gets through, you at least detect it and contain it quickly. It's quite rare that we say there's no way we can insure you. It's more about working with the customer, so they're secure enough to offer terms to.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: We take a similar approach as well. We're certainly identifying at the point of underwriting some of the more risky technologies, so that those conversations can be had, those questions can be asked. I'm reaching out to help them understand what a proper patching cadence is going to look like with that edge device that's constantly under attack. What are their plans to move toward something that, in the future, will be more secure? It's not always realistic for a company to move off their VPN quickly, and we recognize that. So it's for those that lean in and say how can I do this securely, and then how can I roadmap to get to where we need to be in the future.

DR's Rob Wright: Last question on AI for you guys. So, we've covered threat actors using AI tools. We've covered the pen testing tools that are available, frameworks, and what it has done to the influx of vulnerabilities. But what about rogue models, agents escaping containment, agents getting into other networks; is that something that you guys have gamed out plans for? Like, if a client is breached by way of an escaped agent, and there's damage or there's data exposed, does it matter who's behind a breach for your customers, or does it become a different calculus when AI is involved?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: So, from a coverage perspective, the way cyber insurance policies are worded, the coverage trigger is a security failure, and it's about the victim organization and some unauthorized action being taken on their network.

We never say, we'll cover these kinds of attacks, but not these kinds of attackers. It's always just been about the victim, what did the security incident look like? I don't think this is a coverage conversation.

I think historically the media, and I know we're on a podcast at the moment, has focused on certain kinds of attacks that make great stories, and I think this message that OpenAI was developing a model and it escaped: it makes a great story, but the reality is, for the vast majority of businesses, they're not going to face that kind of threat. I think it's incumbent on cyber insurance to use our data to bring a more sober conversation and focus on how they will actually be compromised: business email compromise, funds transfer fraud, ransomware, and I think that's what we should be helping businesses prevent.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: I agree. I think when we talk about the motivations of a rogue agent, it's different than our financially motivated threat actors potentially who are looking to capitalize on that attack. So what that agent does within the environment dictates the response. So, like Daniel said, it doesn't matter if it's a human or an agent. What happened within the environment? What access was had? What data was accessed or exfiltrated or locked up?

I do think, though, there are concerns about agentic use within an organization and when there's agentic drift as well, and that's certainly a known risk, especially among companies that are developing tech. And there are good solutions starting to be built and put into market to help with that monitoring of agents.

But there again, right now in this moment, a lot of companies are lacking that insight and that visibility and that monitoring of agentic behavior, so it's a risk but not something that we're necessarily seeing pronounced in claims yet.

DR's Rob Wright: OK, so let's take a beat and look at 2027. Let's close it out with a look down the road. What do you guys see happening in the next year for the cybersecurity industry, just broadly, Daniel?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: I would expect the increase in frequency of the attacks we've spoken about: business email compromise, phone transfer fraud. The latest evaluations of the very frontier models are finding they can't consistently go from the perimeter of a network to full domain compromise. So I don't think we're going to see automated end-to-end ransomware in 2027. That said, I think there's going to be a lot of chaos, a lot of Web-facing systems breached. They often contain credit-card data, email addresses. So I think it's going to look a lot like the 2010s, more like data breaches, business email compromise.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: If we think about the types of claims that can really drive costs, to what extent are those attacks becoming more and more identified as agentic? Because that necessarily allows threat actors who are financially motivated to have more efficiency and to go after more companies potentially.

Unfortunately, the ransomware threat actors are very successful right now with what they have. And if it ain't broke, don't fix it. So we fully anticipate to see more agentification of different aspects of the attack chain, but that full chaining would be a significant change in this landscape.

DR's Rob Wright: Right. I feel a little bit better about where we are based on what you guys are telling me. Sobering news about how this is going to look more like the 2010s, and we're not going to be having Skynet attacks of rogue agents and models running amok, Daniel. I think you're probably right about that. It's reassuring to hear it. But thank you guys for weighing in on this and giving us a look at what's happening in your industry. Appreciate it.

DR's Becky Bracken: I was going to say, the 2010s don't sound really bad at this point, frankly.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: We had some pretty massive data breaches back then, so I wouldn't seem too eager to go back to that pattern.

DR's Becky Bracken: Oh, something about a BlackBerry though just brought me a sense of comfort. I wanted just to ask, because I am infinitely smarter after this conversation than I was on this topic. So thank you all so much, Rob, for your smart questions and Daniel and Lauren. Your wealth of expertise is invaluable. But I want to ask the question that we started off with, which is: are premiums sustainable? Is the cost of cybersecurity insurance in its current stratosphere, is it sustainable? And what is going to happen with costs in your estimation?

Coalition's Daniel Woods: I don't think the current trend is sustainable, and I think premiums will have to go up. If there are more claims, there are more costs. Insurers need to collect the premiums to cover it.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: And I think that statement was true prior to the frontier AI models. So a lot of insurers within the industry are definitely feeling that that struggle with the current levels of premiums and the current view of risk.

DR's Rob Wright: Although I gotta say, you would think they'd be much higher than they are right now. So I guess that's good. I can't believe I'm being an optimist right now.

Travelers' Lauren Winchester: There are other market conditions at play. There are a lot of markets that are selling cyber, and there are new entrants, and that has an impact.

DR's Becky Bracken: Again, you guys have left us all with a lot to think about, from the governance gap to the speed up of patching introducing configuration problems. I think that we're going to be thinking a lot about all of this in the future.

Daniel Woods, head of emerging risk at Coalition; Lauren Winchester, head of cyber risk services at Travelers Insurance; and Rob Wright, senior news director at Dark Reading: thank you all so much for your time and your expertise today, we appreciate it. This has been Dark Reading Confidential, a podcast from the editors of Dark Reading, bringing you real-world stories straight from the cyber trenches. I'm Becky Bracken, on behalf of all of us at Dark Reading. Thank you so much for tuning in, and we will see you or hear you next time. Bye.

Nicolò Barella ha parlato ieri alla vigilia della partita di Champions contro lo Stoccarda. Il capitano, fermo ai box per un mese dopo un infortunio che lo ha messo ko, è rientrato tra gli uomini a disposizione nella gara contro l'Atalanta, ha giocato uno spezzone e si vedrà questa sera cosa deciderà Chivu sul suo impiego con lo Stoccarda.

Il calcio è fatto anche di infortuni e a volte serve tempo per recuperare. Però chi ha giocato al posto suo, Pio, Thuram, Angy Bonny prima dell'infortunio, hanno fatto molto bene. E ma ora dato che è tornato, molto volentieri lo aspettiamo in campo.

-Per conoscere un po' di te, chi era il tuo idolo da ragazzo, c'è qualcuno che ti piace nel calcio di oggi?

Mi piace guardare tutti i calciatori, c'è sempre da imparare da qualcuno, anche chi viene esaltato meno può fare cose eccezionali e io ho sempre da imparare da tutti. Probabilmente in questo momento il mio preferito di adesso è Bellingham perché è bravo a fare tutto, è un centrocampista completo, è fortissimo anche da fuori. Ma quando ci giochi contro sicuramente capisci quanto è forte e riesce a dare ad una squadra nei momenti importanti di una partita. Il giocatore a cui mi sono sempre ispirato è Dejan Stankovic e l'ho sempre detto.

From Files to Cited Answers in One API Call: Managed Ingestion and SearchDocuments in Agent Retrieval

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A guide to the new managed ingestion and SearchDocuments features in Agent Retrieval, for searching PDFs, Office files, and videos without writing an ingestion pipeline.

Before an agent can answer a question about your PDFs, someone has to build the pipeline: parse every file, split it into chunks, embed each chunk, write the chunks to a vector database, and then learn that database's schema to query it. For video it gets harder: transcode the files, cut them into clips, embed the frames, and keep track of which second of which video each clip came from.

Most of that code has nothing to do with your application. Two features recently added to Agent Retrieval (formerly Vector Search 2.0), now in Public Preview, take that work off your hands. Managed ingestion builds the pipeline for you: point a data store at files in Cloud Storage, and the service parses, segments, and embeds them with Gemini Embedding 2. SearchDocuments, the second new feature, then searches the data store with one API call and returns the matching passages with their page numbers or timestamps. In our tests, 18 Alphabet investor PDFs went from a public Cloud Storage bucket to searchable, page cited results in about 9 minutes, and 19 minutes of video became searchable by both visuals and spoken audio in 0.3 to 0.5 seconds per query, with no parsing, chunking, transcription, or embedding code.

This guide covers how to create a data store, import documents and videos, and search them with filters, reranking, and audio-aware video search.

Pairing managed ingestion with SearchDocuments gives you several immediate benefits:

Agent Retrieval Collections already give you semantic, text, and hybrid search over your own data objects. But you have to produce those data objects yourself, and to query them you need to know how they are stored. Here is what changes when you let a data store do the work:

The backing Collection is still a regular Agent Retrieval Collection, so you can query it directly whenever you need full control.

A data store holds your files and the configuration that controls how they are parsed and segmented. The data store and SearchDocuments APIs are currently served over REST at v1beta, so the examples use an authorized session from google-auth:

Two segmentation modes are available. Chunk segmentation (the default) splits text into chunks of 100 to 500 tokens and works with layout parsing, which also detects tables and headings. Page segmentation produces one segment per page. For paginated documents such as PDFs, both modes record page numbers (startPage and endPage) so you can cite pages or filter by page range: page segmentation returns a single page per segment, while chunk segmentation returns the page range each chunk spans.

Point an import at your files with URI patterns such as gs://my-bucket/reports/*.pdf, or with a JSONL manifest when you want to set your own document IDs and attach metadata:

The import runs as a long-running operation. Re-importing the same files updates existing documents instead of duplicating them, and files whose content didn't change are skipped.

SearchDocuments needs only the data store and a text query:

Each result is a segment of a document, with the text, the page, and the source file:

Pass filter to search only the segments that match, using the same paths you read back in results:

You can also pass document_ids to search only inside specific files, and output_fields.metadata to return each document's metadata with its results. In our test, searching "What did the CEO say about AI?" with the page-1 filter returned the CEO quotes from the headline page of five different earnings releases.

For "How many employees does Alphabet have?", plain hybrid search returned two balance sheet pages among the top five, which don't mention employees at all. With reranking on, every top result reported the headcount: the annual report's "Alphabet had 190,234 employees" and the "Number of employees" row of the quarterly supplemental tables.

Video works the same way: create a data store with document_type: VIDEO, import the files, and call SearchDocuments. The service transcodes each video, splits it into fixed windows, and embeds each window:

Each result carries the moment it matched:

We imported five Google sample videos (about 19 minutes in total) and searched them with scene descriptions. "Cucumbers being sorted by a machine" returned 03:20-03:40 of the machine learning video, where a farm's sorting machine grades cucumbers, without any tags or captions. Searches returned in 0.3 to 0.5 seconds.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

By default (audio_mode: DISABLED), only the frames are embedded, so a query matches what is shown. With ENABLED, the first audio track is embedded together with the frames. We imported the same videos into two data stores and asked both about statements from a keynote, spoken while the picture shows the speaker:

Only the audio-enabled data store found the right moments.

The rule of thumb: If your data is files, let a data store ingest them and search with SearchDocuments. Drop down to the Collection only when you need control the data store doesn't give you.

Managed ingestion doesn't change what good retrieval needs: well-segmented content, good embeddings, and the right filters. What changes is who builds it. Instead of maintaining parsers, chunkers, embedding jobs, and a schema, you point a data store at a bucket and get back segments that are ready to cite.

For agents, that means a retrieval tool that returns where each answer came from, a page in a PDF or a moment in a video, with one API call.

Try it yourself: the document notebook ingests 18 Alphabet investor PDFs and walks through filters and reranking, and the video notebook searches five sample videos with an inline player and compares audio modes.

Building Self-Checkout at Keells: Challenges Beyond the Checkout Screen

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

During my time at Keells, one of Sri Lanka’s leading supermarket chains, I had the opportunity to work on the company’s early self-checkout implementation.

Having worked extensively with our existing Point-of-Sale (POS) system, I was familiar with many of the requirements of supermarket operations. However, moving from a cashier-operated checkout to a self-service environment brought a different set of challenges.

We already had an established POS system that supported a wide range of business functions. Introducing self-checkout meant finding ways to retain those functions while making the shopping experience simpler for customers.

I wanted to share a few of the challenges we faced during this project, particularly those that required us to look beyond the technical implementation.

One of the more interesting problems I worked on was product pricing.

In Sri Lanka, it is possible to have two batches of the same product on a supermarket shelf with different marked prices, even though both have the same barcode.

At a traditional checkout, the cashier can check the price marked on the product and handle the situation accordingly. With self-checkout, the system cannot determine which marked price is printed on the individual item simply by scanning its barcode.

We needed a way to handle this without making the checkout process difficult for customers.

Introducing another screen or asking customers to request assistance was an option, but it would have defeated part of the purpose of introducing self-checkout in the first place.

I looked at the problem from a business perspective as well as a technical one. Building a complicated solution would involve additional development, testing and ongoing maintenance. I wanted to understand whether that level of complexity was actually necessary.

The approach I proposed was to automate the handling of these pricing situations and avoid unnecessary customer intervention.

There were commercial considerations behind that decision, but the main objective was to keep the checkout process straightforward.

It was a good example of a problem where understanding retail operations helped me make a better engineering decision.

Promotions were another area that required considerable attention.

Our existing POS system supported various promotional campaigns. Some required customers to provide their mobile numbers for raffle entries, while others involved free items or additional interactions during a transaction.

These were already familiar processes for our cashiers, but supporting them through self-checkout was not as straightforward.

We were working with an externally managed checkout interface, which meant our development team had limited control over certain customer-facing interactions.

We couldn’t simply reproduce everything from our existing POS application.

One of our internal developers came up with a clever solution using the integration capabilities available to us. This allowed us to support certain promotional requirements without directly modifying the externally managed interface.

My involvement was mainly in understanding the existing POS behaviour, identifying what needed to be supported, discussing the integration options and coordinating with the relevant technical teams.

Knowing our existing POS system was particularly useful here. I could explain not only what a particular function did, but also why the business needed it.

Keeping the checkout running during network interruptions

One of my responsibilities across both POS and self-checkout was to help keep supermarket checkout operations running smoothly.

Network interruptions were something we had to consider. We couldn’t assume that connectivity would always be available during trading hours.

As part of the implementation, we supported offline operation so that temporary connectivity issues would not necessarily bring checkout operations to a complete stop.

However, I always preferred the systems to operate online whenever possible, particularly because of price changes.

Retail prices can change, and a system operating offline may not have the most recent information. The longer it remains offline, the greater the possibility of working with outdated data.

The purpose of offline operation was to help the store continue operating while the connectivity issue was being resolved. It was never intended to replace normal online operation.

From my perspective, the priority was to minimise disruption to the business without overlooking the importance of accurate retail information.

The project also involved working with people outside the IT department.

In particular, I worked with the Finance team because they needed sales information from the self-checkout system for their reporting requirements.

Introducing another checkout channel meant considering what happened to the transaction information after the customer had completed their purchase.

We had to understand the reports and sales information required by Finance and take those requirements into account during the integration.

This was an important part of the project, even though it was not something customers would ever see.

A customer might have a perfectly good experience using a self-checkout machine, but if the business cannot properly account for the sale afterwards, there is still a problem that needs to be addressed.

My previous experience working with POS and business reporting helped me understand these requirements and communicate them to the technical teams involved.

What I enjoyed about this project was that it involved much more than application development.

There were discussions with internal developers, external technology partners, Finance and other business stakeholders. Each team had different requirements and priorities.

Having worked on the existing POS system gave me a useful understanding of how these requirements were connected.

It also helped me identify areas where we could reuse existing functionality instead of developing everything again for self-checkout.

Not every solution came from me. Some came from our developers, while others came from discussions with the people responsible for different areas of the business.

My role was to contribute my knowledge of the existing systems, work through the technical challenges and help bring those requirements together.

By the time I left Keells in 2022, self-checkout had already been introduced across several supermarket locations.

Looking back, the pricing challenge remains one of my favourite examples from the project. It showed me how a relatively simple engineering decision could address a problem that might otherwise have required a much more complicated implementation.

The project also changed the way I looked at enterprise software development. A decision made within an application can affect store employees, customers, Finance and several other areas of the business.

Understanding those dependencies is something I continue to value in my work as a software engineer.

There were several other technical challenges during the implementation, including an interesting issue involving a third-party payment integration. I hope to share that experience in a separate article.

Keells’ early self-checkout and Scan & Go initiatives are documented in the Ceylon Cold Stores PLC Annual Report 2021/22.

https://keells.lk/resource/reports/group-annual-reports/Ceylon-Cold-Stores-PLC-21-22.pdf

This article is based on my personal professional experience. The views expressed are my own. Internal architecture, proprietary implementation details and confidential business information have intentionally been omitted.

Plants can grow elaborate leaves, so why are most of them so simple?

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A study suggests that the way leaves grow helps explain why most flowering plants have simple leaves, even though complex shapes have evolved many times.

The leaves of a maple tree, a rose bush and a magnolia bear very little resemblance to one another. Deep cuts are seen in some of them. Others are made up of several small leaflets or one unbroken blade.

Complex leaf shapes have arisen many times during the evolution of flowering plants. Simple leaves have nevertheless remained by far the most common form.

A new study suggests that the way leaves grow helps explain why leaves with a single blade and no deep cuts are so common.

Random changes were introduced by the researchers into a computer model that simulates a growing leaf.

No particular shape was favored in those runs but simple leaves were still produced far more often than other kinds.

The research was led by James Malone, a graduate student in the Department of Physics at the University of Oxford.

He was joined by Ard Louis, a professor of theoretical physics at Oxford. Researchers in Barcelona and at Stony Brook University in New York also took part.

“What we found is that you can largely explain which leaf shapes dominate in evolution simply by looking at how leaves develop and how mutations affect that process, without needing to invoke natural selection,” said Malone.

“The bias towards simplicity is built into the developmental system itself.”

For the purposes of the study, every leaf was assigned to one of four broad groups based on its outline.

An unlobed leaf consists of a single blade with no deep cuts along its edge. In a lobed leaf the blade is divided by cuts into distinct sections.

Those cuts extend much further toward the center in a dissected leaf. Separate leaflets attached to one shared stalk are what make up a compound leaf.

Large differences in leaf form can be found even among closely related plants. All four leaf types are represented among species of the geranium group Pelargonium.

Both lobed and unlobed leaves may even be produced on a single mulberry tree, as the authors pointed out.

Different leaf shapes do not necessarily require different genes. Much of the variety depends on the timing and location of gene activity inside a developing leaf.

Comparable genetic processes determine whether single leaves or leaves divided into three parts are grown by strawberry plants.

For centuries, botanists have collected, pressed and dried plants for long-term storage. Such specimens are held in collections known as herbariums that now serve as a record of plant diversity.

The team looked at 8,911 digital images of specimens from the Naturalis Biodiversity Center in Leiden, the Netherlands. The shape of each leaf was identified by hand.

The identified shapes were then placed onto five large family trees of flowering plants. One of these trees included 9,506 species and was based on information from 353 genes.

In 2012 an earlier study of 561 plants had reported that about 76% of them carried unlobed leaves. That share rose to between 86% and nearly 89% in the much larger trees used for the new study.

The frequency of leaf shape changes during evolution was then estimated from those labeled trees. Complex leaves were found to give way to unlobed ones far more readily than unlobed leaves became complex.

All five family trees showed this pattern. No disagreement was seen among the trees on the direction of any clearly established shift.

It seems natural to assume that simple leaves give plants some kind of edge over complex ones.

The shape of a leaf is known to influence the way heat and water are managed by the plant. Sunlight capture and protection from plant-eating animals may also be affected by it.

In one experiment with paper leaves, symmetrical unlobed shapes sank the fastest. Such behavior could allow the nutrients in fallen leaves to stay closer to the parent tree.

When earlier research was reviewed by the authors, the evidence for selection proved much stronger for leaf size. Far weaker support was found for selection on these four broad shapes.

In many of those studies it was shown that shape changes how a leaf works. Far fewer showed that a given shape actually helps a plant survive and reproduce. Many also looked only at certain groups of plants.

“For many biological traits, such as the camera eye with its lens and retina, complexity clearly accumulates over time through natural selection,” Louis said.

“But leaf shape shows the opposite pattern. Complexity evolves – and then evolution frequently abandons it.”

A different explanation was therefore explored using a model built by Adam Runions and colleagues in 2017.

Leaf growth in this model is driven by the veins and edge along with chemical signals acting on that edge.

Before the main experiment began the model was compared with results that had been obtained in real plants.

Earlier work on thale cress and hairy bittercress had shown that compound leaves need two genes active together.

Lobes alone were formed in thale cress when only one of those two genes was switched on.

The model behaved in the same way once its two most comparable settings had been adjusted.

Twenty of the 25 tested combinations of those settings went on to produce simple unlobed leaves.

The main experiment was started from 48 virtual leaves that represented all four shapes between them.

Each setting in turn received a small random change and this went on for 320 steps. Five runs were made from every starting leaf and 76,800 virtual leaves came out of the experiment.

Rejection was limited to physically unrealistic leaves such as those in which veins filled too little of the blade.

No leaf was rewarded for its shape and yet about three-quarters of the virtual leaves were found to be unlobed.

Within the margins of error the model’s rates of change toward simple shapes matched those of the family trees.

The model’s settings can be imagined as a long list of instructions for growing one leaf.

Random edits to that list would mostly still end in a leaf with a simple outline.

A far more specific combination of settings is needed to produce a more elaborate leaf, as the researchers found.

The settings for unlobed leaves were grouped into only three separate regions within the model.

Lobed leaves by contrast were spread over 131 regions while dissected leaves occupied 96 and compound leaves 235.

Many of the small regions that gave complex leaves were located inside the much larger region that gave simple ones.

The arrangement can be compared to a mostly green map on which hundreds of tiny blue patches have been scattered.

A few random steps taken from a blue patch would often lead a walker onto green ground.

Someone who started out in the green area might travel a long way before reaching any blue at all. Leaf development appears to work in a comparable way according to the study’s results.

Complex leaves are easily turned into simple ones by random genetic change while the opposite outcome is less likely.

Switches from one complex shape to another such as from lobed to compound were handled less well by the model.

The real family trees were also more likely to disagree with one another about these particular changes.

The authors suggested that natural selection may have had a larger influence on those shifts. It is also possible that some of the finer details of complex leaf growth are not captured by the model.

Closed net-like vein patterns cannot be recreated by it and the parallel veins common in grasses are also absent.

Much of what is known about leaf growth has been learned from a handful of laboratory plants.

Every group of flowering plants may therefore not be represented equally well by a single model.

The small teeth found along the edges of some leaves were not included in the analysis. Hand classification of thousands of herbarium images also put a limit on the amount of usable data.

Whether similar patterns exist elsewhere in nature is the question the researchers hope to answer next.

Tree and root shapes will be examined next along with tooth complexity and the fins of African cichlid fish.

“By continuing this approach – combining large-scale analysis of evolutionary trees with computer simulations – we will gain a much greater understanding of how the complexity of a trait evolves over time across different kingdoms of life,” said Louis.

Folded RNA molecules and grouped proteins had already shown a similar preference for simple forms in earlier work.

Evolution can certainly produce complicated structures but greater complexity is not always the direction it takes.

One reason simple leaves may be so common is that plants have more ways to grow them.

The full study was published in the journal eLife.

App icon size cheat sheet for the App Store and Google Play

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 25 • Originally published at appranker.net

Getting the app icon size wrong is one of the most common reasons a build bounces at upload time, and it is completely avoidable. This post gives you the exact dimensions for every Apple platform and Google Play, the two upload errors worth knowing, and a small script you can run before you ship.

Apple and Google both want one large master image and generate everything else themselves. You no longer need to export a dozen PNGs per platform.

On color: Apple accepts sRGB, Gray Gamma 2.2 and Display P3. Google Play asks for sRGB, so export your Play asset in sRGB even if your iOS master is P3.

Apple's icons are now built for the Liquid Glass look, which means the system adds the depth and lighting for you. A few rules follow from that:

The typical workflow: draw your layers, import them into Icon Composer, set the background, annotate the appearances, drop the .icon file into Xcode, and export a flattened PNG for your website and press kit.

The upload error people hit most is ITMS-90717: the large App Store icon may not contain transparency or an alpha channel. You can check a file on macOS in one line:

If it reports yes, flatten the icon onto a solid background and re-export without alpha. Also remember that on the App Store, changing your icon means shipping a new app version.

Google Play behaves almost the opposite way:

For the launcher icon on devices, use an adaptive icon. Each layer is 108 × 108 dp; keep the logo inside the central 48 to 66 dp and leave the outer 18 dp clear, because launchers crop and animate that edge. Add a monochrome layer so your icon supports themed icons on Android 13 and later. The wiring lives in mipmap-anydpi-v26/ic_launcher.xml:

Here's a small Pillow script that catches the store-asset mistakes above before they reach review:

Run it as python check_icon.py AppIcon-1024.png appstore or python check_icon.py play-icon.png play.

Size is only half the job. Both stores reject icons that carry ranking claims, prices, "New" labels, award claims, store badges or imagery from other platforms, and the artwork has to suit a 4+ age rating.

The icon doesn't rank your app directly, but it drives the taps and installs that do. So design for the thumbnail: test it at small sizes, make it stand out next to competitors in search results, avoid photos and UI screenshots, check the dark, clear and tinted variants, and A/B test before you commit.

The original post goes further, with an Icon Composer walkthrough, an FAQ and a full pre-launch checklist: read the complete app icon size guide for the App Store and Google Play. If you want more visibility once the icon is sorted, you can list your iOS, Android or browser extension app on AppRanker.

What's the icon rejection or redesign that taught you the most? Share it in the comments.

Blizzard is making a lot of the Diablo 4's event-specific Diablo 2 anniversary content permanent because everyone loved it in Season 15

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Blizzard has officially unveiled Diablo 4's next live-service season: Season of Tyrael's Gambit. This is the ARPG's 16th season, and it follows on from the popular and well-received Season of Hell's Legacy.

In an unexpected move, Blizzard announced it's keeping some of the nostalgia-laiden content from the previous season, Season of Hell's Legacy, as a permanent addition. This is unusual, as the ephemeral nature of live seasons means new content goes away at the end of each season.

Season of Hell's Legacy paid tribute to Diablo's legacy and its 30th anniversary by introducing a lot of content inspired by previous Diablo games, chiefly Diablo 2. The list includes the return of iconic character Deckard Cain, a recreation of Old Tristram, and a big fight against the three Prime Evils - Diablo, Baal, and Mephisto - in their classic forms.

"We heard overwhelming requests for features from Season of Hell's Legacy to carry forward as permanent game features," Blizzard wrote in Season 16's announcement post. "While the entire season couldn't come along for the ride, we are implementing the following features with the launch of Season of Tyrael's Gambit that will carry forward in both Eternal and Seasonal realms."

Season of Tyrael's Gambit kicks off 1st December, so you still have time to access all of that in Diablo 4 until Season 15 ends. Certain things, such as craftable items, won't return, so you may as well try them while you can. That said, Blizzard is aware of the love some of those items have, and the developer said it may bring them back in the future.

Photographers Protest Australia’s Plans to Weaken Copyright Laws for AI

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Photographers Protest Australia’s Plans to Weaken Copyright Laws for AI

Photographers have signed a statement protesting the Australian government’s plans to weaken copyright law to benefit AI companies.

According to multiple reports, the Australian government is considering weakening copyright law in order to attract investment from overseas AI companies. Under the proposals, AI companies would no longer be required to get permission from rights holders before training commercial AI models on their work.

Most of the world’s leading AI companies are known to build their models by using copyrighted work without permission or payment. The changes proposed by the Australian government would legalize this unlicensed training on copyrighted work, as long as it happened on Australian soil.

Now more than 7,000 photographers, writers, and musicians have come together to publish a statement opposing proposed changes to Australian copyright law that would favor AI companies and weaken copyright law.

“The Australian government must not legalise the theft of the work of the world’s creators on their soil,” the statement reads. “Our work must only be used to train AI models in Australia with our explicit permission.”

Isabelle Doran, CEO of the Association of Photographers and Vice Chair of Creators’ Rights Alliance, argues that photographers are already at a significant disadvantage when dealing with technology companies, and an opt-out system would place further responsibility on those whose work has been used without permission.

“Creators, such as photographers and image-makers, have little bargaining power against the might of big tech,” Doran says in a statement. “We have been absolutely clear, creators’ work has been exploited without consent, and any Opt-Outs proposal, which has proven to be burdensome, should not be forced onto creators.

She warns that Australia’s ambitions to become more competitive in the AI sector should not come at the expense of creators’ rights.

“It’s imperative that the Australian Government doesn’t backtrack on its initial commitment to protect creators, both in Australia and across the globe,” Doran explains. “Copyright is a human right and critically the lifeblood of creators; this basic human right cannot and should not be used as an economic bargaining chip for national AI investment.”

The widespread use of copyrighted material by leading AI companies without permission or compensation has prompted around 150 major lawsuits in the U.S., with rights holders alleging unauthorized use of their work.

What Running a Free HTML5 Games Site Taught Me About SEO

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 25 • Originally published at tongport.com

I did not start Tongport to learn about search engines. I started it because I wanted a simple place where anyone could open a browser tab and play a game, with no download, no install, and no account standing in the way. The site is Tongport, at https://tongport.com/ and it is a collection of free HTML5 games that run right in the browser. Search came later, and it taught me more than I expected.

The first lesson was that speed is part of the content. A games site lives or dies on how fast a game starts. If a visitor clicks a game and waits, they leave, and they do not come back to try another one. That is true on a desktop, and it is even more true on a phone, where most of my visitors arrive. So I learned to care about loading, about keeping pages light, and about making sure a game is playable on a small screen with touch controls, not just on a keyboard.

The second lesson was about categories. Tongport groups games into .io games, 2 player games, action, racing, shooting, puzzle, arcade, adventure, sports, and cooking. At first I thought of categories as a menu, a way to tidy things up. In practice they do much more. They tell a visitor what kind of fun is on offer before they click anything, and they tell a search engine what each page is about. A clear category page, with games that really belong in it, is easier to find and easier to use than a long mixed list. When a category is vague, both people and search engines get confused, so I try to keep each one honest.

The third lesson was that every game page has to stand on its own. A title and a short, plain description matter more than a clever name. If I cannot say in one or two sentences what a game is and how it plays, the page is not ready. I also learned to avoid copying the same description across many pages. It is tempting when you have a lot of games, but identical text helps nobody, and it makes each page harder to tell apart.

The fourth lesson came from writing guides. On the Tongport blog I write short guides about playing games in the browser, like soccer games, two player games on one phone, and how to get better at racing games. Those guides do something a game list cannot do. They answer the question a person actually typed into a search box. Someone looking for how to play a game with a friend on one screen does not want a wall of thumbnails. They want a clear answer, and a few games that fit it. Writing those guides taught me to think in questions, not just in keywords.

The fifth lesson is the simplest one, and the one I keep relearning. Keep the site easy to use. No forced sign up, no maze of popups, and a game that starts when you click it. People remember how a site made them feel. If it was quick and friendly, they return. If it was slow and pushy, they do not, and no amount of clever search work will fix that.

Running Tongport is still a work in progress for me. I am learning as I go, one category and one guide at a time. But if I had to sum up what a free games site taught me about search, it would be this. Make pages fast, make them clear, group them honestly, and answer real questions. Search engines notice the same things visitors notice, just a little later.

Why RGB breaks skin-undertone checks (and how CIE Lab fixes it in the browser)

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you have ever tried to measure whether a garment color or a selfie leans warm or cool in JavaScript, you probably noticed how fast sRGB falls apart. Two shades can sit 30 units apart in RGB space and look nearly identical to the eye, while two muted earth tones 12 RGB units apart read as completely different temperatures under window light.

While building a browser-only color matcher for the 12-season CIE Lab color palettes, I hit three practical snags that any frontend developer working with real-world photos or garment swatches runs into.

Browser canvas getImageData() gives you gamma-encoded 8-bit sRGB channels (0–255). Averaging raw RGB pixels or computing Euclidean distance directly in sRGB skews dark shadows and muted mid-tones.

You need to linearize each channel first before converting through D65 XYZ into CIE L*a*b*:

Once a hex swatch or sampled pixel is in L*a*b*, you immediately get the three dimensions stylists and colorists actually care about:

Bright primary colors are easy to classify. The hard boundary sits between low-chroma palettes—specifically Soft Autumn versus Soft Summer. Both live around medium lightness (L* ~50–68) and low chroma (C* ~18–26).

In our live Soft Autumn color palette hex & Lab reference, olive (#808A4E), warm taupe (#8C7B6B), and dusty peach (#E0A98C) stay firmly on the warm b* > 0 side while keeping C* low enough that high-contrast black (#111111, L* 5.1) creates an obvious ΔE jump. Comparing an unknown shirt's hex code by computing nearest CIE76 ΔE against both the 12 target swatches and the 4 clash swatches catches mismatches in under a millisecond—zero backend, zero image upload.

Most online color tools send selfies to a server for segmentation. Running the sampling loop, warm-cast lighting check (b* > 14 across the frame mean), and ΔE matching locally inside means:

CIE76 ΔE is fast and transparent, yet it over-penalizes saturated hues compared to CIEDE2000. Even more importantly, phone camera auto-white-balance will happily neutralize a yellow tungsten lamp on one shot and push a north-window selfie cool on the next. Showing the user a lighting-cast warning—and pairing photo sampling with deterministic daylight questions—beats pretending a single pixel read is ground truth.

If you're building any browser-side color picker or wardrobe matcher, start in L*a*b* from day one. What color-space edge cases have bitten you in production?

Get Paid Sooner – Invoice the Day the Job Ends, With a Pay-Now Link

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

A customer can’t pay a bill they haven’t seen, and the days they have to pay don’t start until your invoice goes out. Send each invoice the day you finish the job, with an exact due date and a link to pay online by card or bank transfer, and call the customer the day it’s late.

Billing in one batch at the end of the month feels efficient. It also leaves a job finished on the 3rd unbilled until the 31st, four weeks in which your customer couldn’t pay you even if they wanted to.

That gap between finishing the work and sending the bill is the one delay in getting paid that you control completely. A pay-now link, which lets the customer pay from the invoice email, shortens the rest of the wait.

Picture a cleaning company that mails its invoices at month’s end and gets paid by check, an average of 38 days after each job. Say it switches to emailing each invoice, with a pay-now link, the day the job is done. The typical bill no longer waits two weeks for the month to end or days in the mail, and customers pay without writing a check, so the average wait drops to 12 days.

Send each invoice the same day you finish, from your phone if you’re still on site. Give it an invoice number, an itemized list of the work, a due date, and payment instructions, so the customer has nothing to ask before paying. Write the due date as a date, “due March 14,” rather than “net 30,” which leaves the customer to count 30 days from the invoice date.

Add a pay-now link that accepts cards and bank transfers through ACH, the network that moves money directly between bank accounts. Cards cost you a processing fee on every payment, so ask customers with big invoices to pay by bank transfer, which costs far less.

Then follow up on a schedule. Email a short reminder three days before the due date, while the customer can still pay on time. Call the day after it’s due, not a week later, so they know you track every invoice. Most invoicing apps can send the reminders automatically, so the call is the one step you have to remember.

Put that call on your calendar as you send each invoice, for the day after it’s due.

To find an app that emails pay-now links and sends the reminders for you, compare the best accounting and invoicing software.

Order volume exceeds shipments across core markets as design wins accelerate. Analog Devices relies on proprietary signal processing and hybrid manufacturing while tracking inventory.

Capital allocation prioritizes internal research and shareholder returns. Free cash flow (cash remaining after operations and capital spending) is projected to grow.

"Maybe They Hate Me, But I Love Nintendo" - Kamiya Shares His Collaboration Bucket List

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nintendo, Konami, Square Enix... you name it!

It has been a long old time since we've heard anything from Hideki Kamiya's YouTube channel. The legendary designer kept us fed with gossip, grievances, and game dev stories a couple of years back, but things have been quiet for almost a year now. Until this week, that is!

Yes, Kamiya has uploaded a new video, and it's just as packed with interesting tidbits as you would hope. One part that particularly caught our attention, however, was when the dev started talking about studios he'd love to collaborate with in the future, including a certain Big N.

"Maybe they hate me, but I love Nintendo," Kamiya jokes in his latest video (translated by Automaton), "so I think we may be able to do something together".

He doesn't go into any detail about what a Clovers X Nintendo collab might look like, but hey, there's nothing wrong with trying to manifest something by speaking it into existence.

Kamiya's collaboration bucket list doesn't end there, mind you. He goes on to say that he'd be game to work with Sony or Microsoft, Koei Tecmo on an Omega Force sequel, Square Enix on a King's Knight sequel, and Bandai Namco on a Tag Team Match: M.U.S.C.L.E. game. Dream big, eh!

They are some lofty goals, but we have to respect the ambition. Kamiya founded his new studio, Clovers, after leaving PlatinumGames back in 2023. He is currently working on the aforementioned Okami sequel with Capcom, where he will be back in the Director's seat.

EU plans €50 billion sovereign tech equity fund to boost quantum technology

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The European Commission proposed a tech fund with a target size of €50 billion to boost quantum computing efforts. The bloc aims to keep up with the US and China in the global race.

The European Commission proposed a €50 billion ($54.73 billion) equity financing fund on Wednesday to boost artificial intelligence and quantum computing in Europe.

The draft "Quantum Act" aims to help Europe compete in a race against the United States and China.

"Quantum technologies will shape the future ‌of industry, security and innovation, and they will have a ‌profound ​impact on our societies. Europe needs to accelerate now," EU tech chief Henna Virkkunen said in a statement.

"We want to build a fund and our target scale is now €50 billion," she said, calling it a "sufficient starting point."

Virkkunen introduced the officially named "Sovereign Tech Equity Fund" while presenting new rules to support Europe's quantum technology sector.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The new rules will push public authorities to favor European companies when giving public contracts and funding.

The European Union (EU) also wants to reduce its dependence on third countries amid concerns that US companies could use so-called "kill switches” to disable their products in Europe.

Quantum computing technology is still at the early stages of development, with both the US and China competing to achieve dominance in the field.

While classical computers process data using bits — which are either 0 or 1 — quantum computers use qubits (quantum bits), which can exist in a blend of both states at once.

By using qubits, these machines could eventually solve complex problems much faster than today's supercomputers.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The EU expects the quantum computing to lead to breakthroughs in several areas, including drug development, earlier medical diagnoses or super-efficient batteries and even AI development.

Although quantum technology offers significant potential, it also poses cybersecurity risks, as it could undermine encryption and other safeguards for digital data at a time when governments are battling AI-driven cyberattacks.

Around one-third of the world's quantum companies ⁠are ​based in the European Union, according to ​the Commission. Last year, public funding for quantum technologies reached €8.6 billion in the bloc, compared with €5.3 billion in the United States and €13.6 billion in China.

However, European quantum start-ups have attracted €1.1 billion in private funding so far, compared with €7 billion in the United States.

The EU launched its "quantum Europe" strategy in July 2025, aiming to be a global leader in quantum technologies by 2030.

The bloc is already a global leader in quantum research but it falls short when it comes to transforming this into viable commercial opportunities.

The draft legislation must be negotiated with EU member states and the European Parliament in the upcoming months before it can be law.
