Microsoft's 24-hour outage had no hacker behind it, and 10 lessons follow for business

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A dormant Windows flaw, not a hacker, caused Microsoft's 24-hour outage. Here are 10 risk lessons for businesses that rely on one cloud vendor.

Most corporate risk plans imagine a cloud outage that begins with an intruder, such as a stolen password, a ransomware gang or a hostile state. A global Microsoft $MSFT outage began with no intruder at all, and no hacker caused it. Even so, people around the world filed more than 7.4 million reports of trouble during the failure, according to ABC News. A fault inside one company was enough to disrupt work across the globe. The 2024 Microsoft IT outage was linked to CrowdStrike $CRWD, a cybersecurity firm, according to TIME. This time the fault sat inside Microsoft's own systems, in what CBC News reports was the company's second disruption of 2026. For a business, the question is less about who might break in and more about what happens when its provider breaks its own systems.

When a provider of this size fails, whole institutions stop working, not just their email. The Next Web reports that trading firms were delayed and airline communications were disrupted. Finance and air travel usually break down for different reasons, but this time they failed together because they rent the same software, and the weak point sat in a layer that no customer controls.

News18 laid out what went wrong behind Microsoft's "Code Red" and what enterprises should take from it. Its lessons cover a dormant flaw that no hacker planted, the backup channels a company needs when Teams and Outlook stop working, and continuity plans built for days rather than minutes. Together, they move the job of staying online from the vendor's promises to the customer's own preparations.

Microsoft $MSFT caused its own day-long outage with a change it had made months earlier. The root of the failure was a hidden flaw that Microsoft added to its gateway servers in July, not an attack by hackers, according to News18. The flaw was a scalability bug that nobody detected for months, The Next Web reports, and if Microsoft's own engineers missed it, no customer could have found it. A bug of this kind stays hidden until the load on a system grows large enough, so a clean record means only that nobody has triggered it yet.

The cost of a dormant flaw is nothing for months and then everything on one day. Once the flaw surfaced, users lost Exchange Online, Outlook, Teams and Copilot together, which left staff with no fallback app inside the same suite. The component that broke had seen "roughly ten years' worth of acceptable failures," Charles Lamanna, Microsoft's executive vice-president for Copilot, Agents and Platform, said in a Code Red review, as relayed in a memo by Amanda Silver and reported by The Next Web. Customers absorbed that cost during the 24-hour outage rather than in small amounts spread across years.

Vendor reviews built on uptime history and incident counts cannot detect this kind of risk, because nothing about a dormant flaw appears in the record until the day it fails. A company that rents its email and collaboration tools should assume that a flaw already exists somewhere in the stack and plan for the day it surfaces.

No customer could have reached the defect itself, but what triggers a hidden flaw and how fast Microsoft can fix it decide how much damage it does.

Microsoft $MSFT's day-long outage began on an ordinary day of operations, with no launch, attack or unusual event behind it. News18 reports that a routine traffic shift, combined with reduced capacity, set off the dormant flaw. The result was a full stop rather than a slowdown, with Microsoft's servers left "overwhelmed and unresponsive," according to ABC News.

An ordinary trigger makes this kind of failure harder to see coming, and it makes the failure no less serious. Engineers tend to flag rare events, such as a major product launch, for extra caution. A traffic shift belongs to the normal running of a service, and nothing about it marks it as a moment of risk. Walayat Hussain, an associate professor at Australian Catholic University, sees that ordinariness as the cause for concern. "This looks like Microsoft's own systems tripping over themselves, and that is the real warning," Hussain told ABC News.

For a customer, the trigger sits inside operations it does not run and cannot schedule around. A company gets no notice of routine traffic shifts inside its provider's systems and has no say in how much spare capacity the provider has on hand.

A company's defense has to begin the moment service stops, whatever the cause turns out to be.

A failure like this is only partly about the broken code. The rest depends on whether the people fixing it can agree on what is broken. Microsoft $MSFT's engineers exchanged more than 11,000 messages during the outage response, News18 reports, split across 11 different Teams chats rather than gathered in one. The weakness this exposed was coordination, not manpower.

Every one of those chats ran on Teams, Microsoft's own chat product. Teams was itself degraded during the outage, and the responders were coordinating through a tool damaged by the same failure they were trying to fix. A diagnosis scattered across many threads, on a tool that is failing along with everything else, is a risk any company could face in its own outage. For an enterprise, the lesson is to decide before a crisis where the diagnosis gets assembled. A company needs one named record that brings the separate threads together, with a person responsible for keeping it, and that record matters more than the number of people typing. It also helps to keep that record somewhere other than the chat tool the company depends on every day.

Responders can trade 11,000 messages and still lack a clear answer if nobody owns the full picture.

An outage ends when engineers have delivered a repair to every affected system, not when they find the bug. Microsoft $MSFT's services were out for 24 hours, according to Microsoft's internal memos reported by News18, and customers had no part in the repair for that whole time. No company can shorten a schedule that another company sets, and its staff could only wait for Microsoft to finish.

The obvious lesson is that Microsoft should push fixes out faster, but its chief executive points to the growing number of patches as a source of risk. Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella sees patching speeding up rather than lagging, CNBC reports, and he credits artificial intelligence with finding more bugs for engineers to fix. "The thing that AI has done is it allowed us to find these bugs and fix these bugs. But that means you've got to then patch them, and that creates instability," Nadella said. Every fix is also a change to systems that are already running, and each change carries risk of its own. Patching is therefore a trade-off rather than a race. Leaving flaws unpatched keeps them open, while a steady stream of patches can unsettle the systems that receive them.

Neither faster nor slower patching addresses what made this failure global, which was the lack of a limit on how many systems one bad change could affect. Nadella acknowledges that Microsoft should have tested the change before releasing it and should have built barriers between its systems so that a single bug could not cause a worldwide outage, according to CNBC. For enterprises, the useful questions are whether the vendor releases updates in stages and keeps regions separate. They should also ask whether their own IT staff patch in-house systems in stages, so that nobody installs one update on every machine at once.

The measure that matters is not how fast a vendor delivers a good fix but how many systems it lets one bad change reach.

Microsoft $MSFT's promise to recover from an outage within an hour is a commitment about the next failure, not a record of this one. News18 reports that the company's Code Red plan sets a one-hour recovery target. Traffic management, the way Microsoft moves traffic inside its own systems, is one of the areas the company is overhauling to reach that target. Even if Microsoft meets it, the hour measures how quickly Microsoft resolves a disruption. It does not measure when a customer's courtroom, clinic or trading desk gets back to work.

The accounts of this outage's length do not agree. It lasted 24 hours across Nov. 12–13, 2026, yet The Associated Press reports that Microsoft did not announce full restoration until Saturday, Nov. 14. By either account, Microsoft's actual recovery took far longer than an hour, and a business that writes the hour into its own plans is counting on a performance Microsoft has not yet delivered.

Microsoft restored its services in stages, and customers lost work the whole time. Outlook problems piled up into Friday even as Microsoft kept restoring service, according to The Associated Press, and in Manchester, England, a trial was halted and the jurors were sent home. The recovery time a customer actually faces is Microsoft's gradual restoration plus whatever fallbacks the customer has built for itself.

Plan against the record, not the pledge, and test the hour the next time Microsoft's services go down.

Most companies picture a single point of failure as a target, a system that an attacker aims at and the security budget pays to defend. Microsoft $MSFT's systems stayed out of service for 24 hours without any attacker involved, according to News18. The company said it had found nothing to suggest that anyone acted with malicious intent, according to TIME. A routine update or a hidden design bug can knock out core systems as thoroughly as an attack, and defenses built to stop intruders are not designed to catch either one. The harm a company suffers depends on how much of its work rests on the failed component, not on whether anyone meant to break it.

While systems are down, nobody outside the vendor can tell an accident from an attack, and a dead inbox looks the same either way. In Australia, the outage cut off the Australian Federal Police and even ASIO, the country's domestic spy agency, according to TIME. No cyberattack was behind their losses. Their skill at spotting enemies gave them no protection against a fault with no enemy behind it.

Ruling out an attack became public work of its own. Australia's Home Affairs and Cyber Security Minister Tony Burke passed on Microsoft's early view of the cause. "They believe they are dealing in some ways similar to what we saw with respect to CrowdStrike $CRWD, where there is an update that has gone awry," Burke said, as reported by TIME. Microsoft gave that account while its recovery was still under way, so customers were weighing the failed company's own explanation at the moment they were least able to check it. A company's response therefore has to start from the damage, not wait for a verdict on the cause.

The more useful audit asks which single components, at the vendor or in-house, could stop the company's work for a day, whoever or whatever breaks them. Under that test, an accident at a vendor counts as a threat as serious as a break-in and should get the same contingency planning.

Most companies picture a backup channel as a list of phone numbers kept for emergencies, a way to keep talking rather than a way to keep working. Microsoft $MSFT had an outside channel of that kind during the outage, and it posted its public updates on X $TWTR, according to ABC News. Its engineers, though, ran the repair on Teams, the product that was failing. Microsoft had a separate line for speaking to the public and none for fixing the problem.

A fallback needs a trigger that the company controls as much as it needs a place to go. Microsoft's first update described a problem "in the Americas," ABC News reports, while Immigration New Zealand had stopped processing every visa application. A company that waits for the vendor to confirm the scope may lose hours it could have spent working. An SMS bridge runs entirely outside Microsoft's systems, and a company can switch it on by its own decision. The plan should state the trigger in plain terms: Staff move to the bridge when they lose mail or chat, not when the vendor's status update agrees. A bridge that nobody has practiced on is just a contact list again, and employees need to rehearse using it before a crisis.

Small companies have the least room to absorb a lost day. Exchange Online, Outlook, Teams and Copilot failed together, News18 reports, so a small firm that kept all of its mail and chat in that suite had to find a workaround somewhere else or go without. Some small businesses lost up to $30,000 in a single day, according to ABC News.

The time to set up and test a route outside the suite is now, while every channel still works.

Buying an AI assistant from the company that already runs a firm's email and meetings ties more of the working day to one provider and spreads none of the risk. Companies tend to budget for Copilot as a new capability with risks of its own. During the outage, it failed alongside the rest of the suite. Exchange Online, Outlook, Teams and Copilot all went down at the same time, according to News18. Microsoft $MSFT said AI agents had nothing to do with the cause, according to CBC News. Copilot went dark because it runs on the same foundation as the mail and the meetings, not because of a flaw in the assistant. Each product a company adds from the same provider rests on that foundation too, so it deepens a single dependency instead of adding a separate system.

The remedy is a second set of tools, but building one is a long project. "Ultimately, it has to be diversification. We have to make sure that when a system goes down, we have a backup," Francis Syms, associate dean of applied sciences and technology at Humber Polytechnic, told CBC News. Bill Laboon, vice-president of technical operations at the Web3 Foundation, says companies don't see the value of spreading their systems across providers until one of them fails.

The first step is knowing which work stops when the vendor does. A map of the processes that would halt in a vendor outage gives a company that knowledge before the next failure, and it can also show which critical work already sits outside the shared stack. Santé Québec does not handle patient communications by email, according to CBC News, so those exchanges did not depend on the email service that failed.

The useful question today is not which vendor to switch to, but which work stops when this one goes down.

A continuity plan prepares a company only for the outage it imagines, and a short outage is the easiest one to imagine. News18 reports that a company drafting a formal Continuity of Operations Plan should not count on a quick failover of 15 minutes. CBC News reports that Microsoft $MSFT's outage was in its second day on Nov. 13, so a plan built around a 15-minute switch would have been measuring the wrong scale. A plan that expects service back within minutes has no instructions for the second business morning, when staff, customers and deadlines are still waiting. Companies should size their plans to the failure that just happened, counted in days, rather than to the failure the plan was written for.

The organizations that held up did not recover sooner than anyone else. They had a way to keep working that did not depend on the failed service. Aviva kept its calls going on alternative numbers and moved recordings by Bluetooth, according to CBC News. Its staff could use those low-tech routes because they did not run through Microsoft's cloud. Where a company runs its software is also a continuity decision. Companies that hosted Microsoft software in-house came through somewhat better, and they had made that choice well before the failure, not in the middle of it.

Planning for a multi-day outage means deciding now which work must continue without the cloud, then building that route through manual processes or self-run systems before a company needs it for a second day.

The Government Tested How Dangerous AI Could Get—Then Four ChatBots Helped Plan a Terrorist Attack

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Government Accountability Office secretly tested ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, and Grok and found that all four would help make plans when prompted.

The dawn of artificial intelligence brings with it both rewards and risks. With the ability to sift through unfathomably large data sets, AI could help detect cancers in their infancy, unlock some of the most enduring mysteries of mathematics, and even help contain the beating nuclear heart of future fusion reactors.

Unfortunately, the risks of this technology are far from minuscule, including its impact on human education, the environment, and potentially the survival of the human race (an all-too-plausible scenario put forward by AI tech CEOs). Even the everyday LLMs that now litter our digital lives—Claude, Gemini, ChatGPT, and Grok—can aid and abet some of the most dangerous actors on the planet.

At least, that’s the latest conclusion by the Government Accountability Office (GAO), a nonpartisan watchdog housed in the legislative branch of the U.S. government. Typically, GAO turns its investigative gaze on efforts to limit fraud and wasteful spending in the government itself, but in a new tactic at the urging of U.S. Senator Maggie Hassan (D-NH), the office instead performed real-world tests on the platform to see if these four popular AI models would aid in planning a terrorist attack.

“A terrorist can log onto an AI platform and in less than 12 hours walk away with a concrete playbook for how to carry out a deadly attack,” Hassan, incoming Chair of the Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs Committee, said in a press statement. “Today’s new report makes it clear that the AI public safety threat is no longer theoretical.”

GAO conducted this test in the spring this year, and earlier this week reported some disturbing results. Two of the platforms, according to an official GAO release, provided guidance on building and detonating an improvised explosion device, or IED, and two other AI models showing differing methods for targeting the electrical grid. Within 12 hours, all four models also gamed through potential locations and methods for carrying out such an attack.

Although these violent lines of questioning triggered each platform’s safeguards, GAO got around them using common safeguard-bypassing techniques. Additionally, they confirmed the validity of the information provided with experts. The results of this investigation were published in a 61-page report that will be central to future senate hearings on AI safety.

“We set out to do two different types of evil,” GAO director Kevin Walsh said in an interview posted on YouTube. “The first type is we wanted to create a bomb. And we didn’t just want to know how to create a bomb, we wanted it to help pick out a target, a location, and a time, and to give us instructions all the way…Evil scenario two, we wanted to do a bunch of economic damage, and in both cases that ended up being targeting the electrical grid.”

In one case, Walsh reports that one AI model would refuse to answer questions or divert the user to more safe behaviors but said that “it wasn’t always successful.” Walsh says that companies theoretically call law enforcement when suspicious activity is detected, but whether that occurs and at what frequency is uncertain.

“As AI capabilities evolve, AI providers will continue to refine their safeguards,” the report concludes. “However, in its current state, this technology can potentially be dangerous and its misuse represents a growing risk to national and economic security.”

The GAO officially released these terrorist-abetting findings on November 18, 2026, the same day Anthropic, creators of Claude, went public on the stock exchange. Despite the company’s own admission of AI being an existential threat to humanity, its IPO at one point reached a meteoric a $2 trillion valuation before dipping back down to a (measly) $1.6 trillion.

Darren lives in Portland, has a cat, and writes/edits about sci-fi and how our world works. You can find his previous stuff at Gizmodo and Paste if you look hard enough.

It's time for Marvel to replace the Avengers, and Avengers: Doomsday is the perfect way to do it

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the GamesRadar community for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

The latest Avengers: Doomsday trailer has me convinced the Young Avengers are on their way

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The latest trailer for Avengers: Doomsday gives us our best look yet at the Avengers in their fight against Doctor Doom. But it's not just the Avengers showing up to fight. With the entire multiverse at stake, pretty much everyone is throwing their hat or pointy helmet into the ring at this point. As well as the main Avengers team, the former Thunderbolts, now known as the New Avengerz, will also suit up alongside the Fantastic Four and the X-Men from the early Fox movies.

It's no wonder that Thor seems a bit confused by all these newcomers. "I don't even know what half of you do," he says in the trailer. "I don't even know who half of you are..." He's probably not the only one either. If you haven't been keeping up with the MCU post-Endgame, a lot has changed since the Infinity Stone saga came to an end in 2019.

While that's par for the course if you're familiar with the comics, casual viewers might be struggling to understand or even care about this vast roster of heroes now. That's especially true given just how long some of these Avengers have been hanging around. Endgame was supposed to bring Steve Rogers' story to a close, yet here he is hogging all the attention again, 15 years after Chris Evans first picked up the shield.

With Chris Hemsworth's Thor and Robert Downey Jr. also returning, albeit in a different role for the latter, this old guard are still pulling focus, which isn't ideal for all the new heroes Marvel has tried to win us over with in Phases Four, Five and Six especially. Endgame was sold as… well, as an endgame, yet that couldn't have been further from the truth.

Things do seem different this time around, though. Given the sheer volume of heroes assembled in Avengers: Doomsday, it stands to reason that some won't survive the film's entire runtime. It's hard enough fitting them all on screen at once if the many, many crammed group shots already shared online are anything to go by.

That's ok though, because Marvel has already lined up replacements for all the Avengers who have outstayed their welcome.

First up are the New Avengerz, Val Cooper's ragtag group of ex-villains who have banded together to redeem themselves. Sentry aside, it's hard to imagine any of them surviving for long against Doctor Doom – good luck to Yelena Belova and her pointy sticks – but survive they will. Or at least, most of them will.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

Thunderbolts* wasn't the biggest hit Marvel's ever had at the box office, but some of these characters clearly remain popular nonetheless, and their anti-hero outlook brings a fresh new perspective to the Avengers as a concept. While the team has enlisted heroes with a dodgy past before – think Black Widow, Winter Soldier – few took so much joy in wrongdoing as the majority of these former Thunderbolts did.

It's not just the New Avengerz gunning for a permanent seat at the table. After twenty years of comic book storytelling, it looks like the Young Avengers will officially form at last too, either in Doomsday or its follow-up, Avengers: Secret Wars.

Created by Allan Heinberg and Jim Cheung in 2005, the Young Avengers started out as a group of super-powered teenagers with various ties to longtime members of the main Avengers team. The same is true of their MCU counterparts, including a number of heroes who have been introduced in various films and shows before they (presumably) come together here.

At the end of 2023's The Marvels, Kamala Khan, aka Ms Marvel, explained to Kate Bishop that she's recruiting "kid" heroes to start a team of their own. There haven't been any updates since, aside from news that Kamala was "visiting friends" in California, according to her father during a guest appearance in Daredevil: Born Again. But with Hailee Steinfeld now confirmed to reprise her Hawkeye mentee role in Doomsday, the pieces are all coming together.

Given the sheer volume of heroes assembled in Avengers: Doomsday, it stands to reason that some won't survive the film's entire runtime

Kathryn Newton's Cassie Lang, who just so happens to reside in California, made her debut alongside Kate in the most recent Doomsday trailer, sowing the seeds for an imminent team-up between Ant-Man's daughter and Hawkeye's surrogate daughter.

Plus, it's surely no coincidence that founding members (and brothers) Wiccan and Speed were united at the end of VisionQuest just a few weeks back. The WandaVision spinoff also tied into Doomsday directly with Doctor Doom's surprise recruitment of their mother, Wanda Maximoff, in a post-credits scene. Family reunion, anyone?

Meanwhile, Iman Vellani's Ms Marvel isn't traditionally associated with the Young Avengers, but her plans for a team-up make this version of the character essential to the team's future onscreen. Don't be surprised if Vellani's involvement in the film is officially confirmed at the end of her Countdown to Doomsday podcast, which revisits every Marvel movie in the lead-up to Doomsday.

Three other Young Avengers have already appeared in previous MCU projects too: Kid Loki (Jack Veal) on Loki season 1, America Chavez (Xochitl Gomez) in Doctor Strange in the Multiverse of Madness, and Elijah Bradley/Patriot (Elijah Richardson) in The Falcon and the Winter Soldier.

Any one of them could make their return in the Young Avengers lineup, especially as multiple members showed up last year in concept art by designer Mushk Rizvi (that's been conveniently taken down since). Don't forget Hulk's son either, Wil Deusner's Skaar, who made a brief, silent cameo in the finale of She-Hulk: Attorney at Law.

Whatever happens, it's clear that we don't know the whole story just yet. After all, Marvel is notorious for holding back and even misleading us with fake-outs in their biggest trailers. Many fans even suspect that the final act for Doomsday has been kept out of promotional material entirely so far, and it's almost certain that various characters have been edited out of the shots we have seen.

As such, it's not much of a stretch to imagine that some of our faves might snuff it or retire to make way for a whole new slate of heroes ready to do some avenging on their behalf. And it's about time too.

The longer Marvel remains fixated on replaying their greatest hits, the more desperate and tired the MCU will feel. Thankfully, change is most definitely in the air. Marvel head Kevin Feige says (via EW) that both Doomsday and Avengers: Secret Wars are "passing the torch from one universe to another," describing the latter as "a launching pad for our films in 2028 – the 20th anniversary of the MCU," which will launch "a very new era at that point."

While we know that "new era" will kick off with a new X-Men movie, followed by a third Black Panther film and a new Ghost Rider, three other unspecified Marvel movies remain on the slate. How much do you want to bet a Young Avengers film will be among them?

Thor might not know who these kids are, and casual Marvel fans might not either. But that's going to change real quick once Doomsday and Secret Wars reshuffle the MCU entirely, paving the way for a whole new generation of heroes we can rally behind again.

Avengers: Doomsday arrives in theaters on December 18 in the US and December 16 in the UK. For more, check out our guide to upcoming Marvel movies and shows.

10 Games That Will Feel Better To Play After GTA 6

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ronald is a video game journalist with more than 10 years of experience in the industry, working on several of the most important Spanish websites.

If you're like 32.5 million other people around the world, you've probably been dedicating a huge amount of time to GTA 6 lately, and you can't imagine what your life will be like when the experience is over.

All good things must come to an end, and eventually, your time in Leonida will reach a conclusion. However, crossing that frontier doesn't necessarily have to be a bittersweet farewell but rather an opportunity to re-evaluate everything that comes next.

Titles like this one help you appreciate the craft of video game development more than ever, as they fully exploit many of the aspects that make the interactive industry the most special of all those focused on entertainment.

Therefore, if these hypermodern times have already gotten to you and are making you nervous about what will come after Jason and Lucia's story, calm your anxiety by checking out this list of ten games that will feel better to play after GTA 6.

If you're wondering how on earth this list is going to start with one of the games most diametrically opposed to the philosophy of GTA 6, like GRIS, the answer lies precisely in that statement.

After an adventure brimming with stimuli, endless possibilities, lengthy explanations, and violence that ranges from the personal to the systemic, what better way to unwind than with one of the most focused and introspective titles ever conceived?

In just three hours, without a single word and in a straightforward manner, GRIS takes you on a soothing path of reflection, making peace with the circumstances, and reveling in an audiovisual experience that engulfs you to the point of baptizing you with its art.

Without bullets or socioeconomic problems that dictate your relationship with the law, but with platforms, elegance, and a creative intent so distinct that you won't be able to help but fall in love by the time the credits roll on the Sunday afternoon it takes to complete it.

The cliché of "best game ever" versus "best game today" strikes me as both amusing and illogical, but I do believe there's some value in considering The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time Remake in light of GTA 6.

Both titles excel in their focus on player freedom, yet while GTA achieves this within a stunning range of action that opens up numerous possibilities, Ocarina of Time Remake does so in a much more concentrated and whimsical way.

The charm lies not in the immeasurable variety of options, but in the immeasurable creativity of the single alternative they offer for each sequence, demonstrating in the process how the notion of agency has evolved over the last 30 years.

They are two ends of the same spectrum, and experiencing the same sense of enjoyment through discovery in such different ways is what makes them complement each other far better than you might initially imagine.

Having spent its entire existence in the shadow of Rockstar's work, returning to Saints Row 2 now that GTA has taken a 180-degree turn in its approach has become more fitting than ever.

Jason and Lucia face a more realistic, weighty world with consequences, masterfully encouraging restraint and attention to detail, breaking the connection that pitted the two franchises against each other for so long.

Hence, whether you're among those who didn't fully embrace the change in philosophy or those who did, Saints Row 2's humor and arcade style are a perfect fit to reduce the pleasurable cortisol rush produced by GTA 6.

Don't get me wrong: I greatly appreciate Rockstar's courage in going against its own trend, though that doesn't mean I'm not much more eager to play a more relaxed game once I decide to stop wandering around Leonida, postponing saying goodbye.

Grand Theft Auto 6 puts a large emphasis on keeping your relationship sound, so you need to know what ways are the best to raise the romance between Jason and Lucia.

After GTA 6, it's quite clear the developer has mastered every imaginable variation of the driving genre, which includes the adrenaline-fueled and spectacular style they employed during the Midnight Club 3: DUB Edition era.

The fact that the vehicles in their latest product had to prioritize accuracy and composure was necessary to fit the rest of the game's structure, yet the primal thrill of getting behind the wheel and flying at 300 kilometers per hour in the most improbable way possible was sacrificed.

Consequently, even considering that the genre as a whole is today much closer to realistic simulation than arcade driving, dusting off your copy of Midnight Club 3: DUB Edition after GTA 6 would be in the instruction manual if GTA 6 had one.

Neither approach is inherently better than the other, evidently, but the desire to truly revel in the speed instead of worrying about the cost of a crash will grow stronger on you with each passing second.

Years go by, and it's incredible how no third-person shooter has understood that they are morally obligated to look to Vanquish to understand how to keep the genre from perpetually stagnating.

Gunfights in GTA 6 are technically impressive and an absolute marvel in terms of action sequences, and yet there isn't a second that goes by that I don't think of Sam Gideon and the thunderous perfection of his combat performance.

Instead of being a turn-based confrontation where both sides take turns behind a wall to exchange bullets, Vanquish is a spiral of destruction that doesn't even stop to reload, presenting a striking contrast to the game we're discussing here.

Would being able to slide at full speed and defeat giant robots in GTA 6 make sense? No. Am I dying to play through Vanquish for the thirtieth time for every occasion I have to stay put in cover? Absolutely.

While this might sound utterly personal, few things please me as much as revisiting a developer's older games after a long time, which brings us to Grand Theft Auto: San Andreas.

Being the franchise's installment I've sunk the most hours into and the reason I love open-world games, it creates an intrinsic incentive for GTA 6 because it allows you to appreciate the passage of time.

Many of the gameplay concepts we enjoy in the franchise today, such as character customization, routines, and minigames, were pioneered by CJ, and taking a step back to appreciate what San Andreas did over 20 years ago is magical.

This recommendation might be harder to justify theoretically because, generally speaking, GTA 6 is a vastly superior version, though the magic of nostalgia is something it won't acquire for another couple of decades, while San Andreas has plenty of that.

This is a brief guide that entails the location of the Fast and Furious green Eclipse in GTA 6.

Rockstar did an exceptional job, like few developers in history, of truly discouraging lethal attacks through a bloody, sensitive, and visceral presentation, though it's ironic that these same qualities are what elevate DOOM Eternal.

Being careful with how you drive or which body parts you shoot is excellent, but I'd be lying if I didn't admit that a part of me misses the raw brutality with which you can manipulate the Doom Guy to commit hellish extermination.

The two titles contrast sharply in both their approach to combat and the ease of movement that enables it, so just thinking about DOOM Eternal's levels, flying all over the place while leaping from execution to execution, makes my mouth water.

Furthermore, being able to shoot an enemy entity in the face with a shotgun without being completely dismayed by my own lack of humanity is a significant bonus, because I do miss being able to disconnect from the consequences of my characters' actions.

GTA 6 is a game of distance, in the sense that its experience focuses heavily on trajectories, the separation between spaces, and the distinct biomes, doing everything Yakuza 0 refuses to do despite also being an open world.

Kamurocho and Sotenbori occupy a tiny fraction of Leonida's map, and there's no vehicle other than your own two feet to traverse them, though this allows you to connect with every back alley and building in a way that no other title has been able to instill in me.

That familiarity and closeness with which Yakuza 0 relates to its inhabitants, locations, and activities is invaluable, and although GTA 6 manages to generate identical emotions, the difference in scale creates an impressive sense of belonging to Kiryu and Majima's journeys.

There are many points of convergence, but the size breaks down the comparison, allowing their divergences to become apparent as the hours pass, thus highlighting the asymmetries that each offers to fans of the genre.

It's clear after just a couple of hours of gameplay that Red Dead Redemption 2 is the primary influence on GTA 6, so we can all agree that we wouldn't be here if it weren't for Red Dead Redemption.

Replacing the noise of neon lights and social media with horses and forests is something Arthur Morgan's game could easily do, but what even the legendary outlaw can't achieve is to also hark back to a time when even design was more contemplative.

Unlike its sequel, Red Dead Redemption is a prototype whose full expression was prevented by technological limitations, resulting in a much quieter, more poetic, and simpler journey, but with the hints of grandeur that its direct and spiritual successor would achieve in 2018 and 2026, respectively.

Therefore, riding back through its empty landscapes, conversing with its few interactive characters, and firing its limited guns is the handbrake for the Pegassi Vitello inside which we find ourselves in GTA 6, letting us breathe fresh air and appreciate the beauty of slower times.

Some players may think they should register a hot new sports car as their starter car. There's actually a better choice. Here's the best starter car to register in GTA VI.

If at any point between 2013 and now you had told me that GTA 6 would be much more like Shenmue than any other video game, I would have thought you needed a psychiatrist, but here we are.

If you're surprised by the attention to detail in drinking a beverage, observing the routines of dozens of NPCs, having to stay fit and healthy, or enjoying complex minigames, let me inform you that Yu Suzuki was already doing it all back in 1999.

While Shenmue is more similar to Yakuza 0 in scale, it also resembles GTA 6 in that both embrace a life simulation design, where walking, talking, playing, interacting, and other such actions aren't driven by the sheer volume of fun but rather by the experience of placing yourself in someone else's shoes.

That talent for immersing you in a reactive, living world that transcends your existence is reserved for the legends, and if you can tolerate the difference in technological advancement and size, Shenmue will easily show you why it feels like a great-great-grandfather of GTA 6.

Like many games, Grand Theft Auto 6 is full of easter eggs for you to discover. These are some of the best Leonida has to offer.

Tapo’s new indoor security camera includes a touchscreen for video calls

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The C825 can keep an eye on your home or help you keep in touch with your family.

The C825 can keep an eye on your home or help you keep in touch with your family.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

TP-Link’s Tapo smart home brand announced a new indoor security camera that can help keep an eye on your house while you’re away or connect you to family and friends while you’re at home. The C825 upgrades a 2K pan-and-tilt camera with a 4-inch touchscreen, so when it’s not watching for intruders or misbehaving pets, it can be used to make hands-free video calls.

The Tapo C825 is available starting today for $149.99. That’s a little expensive for a 2K camera, but its tracking capabilities could make up for it not being 4K. Using a rotating base the C825 can pan 360 degrees while its camera module on top can tilt up and down up to 156 degrees. When paired with the camera’s 114.5-degree field of view every corner of a room should be covered, including in the middle of the night using its infrared night vision capabilities.

When used as a security camera the C825 leverages AI to differentiate between subjects including pets and different people through facial recognition. It can also recognize sounds and send alerts when a crying baby or barking dog is heard. The detection processing happens on-device, and videos of events are stored locally on a microSD card up to 512GB in size. No subscription is required, but you can upgrade to Tapo Care for a monthly fee, which adds cloud storage and features like video summaries and weekly reports.

While the C825’s two-way audio communication isn’t unique, expanding that to include two-way video is. Calls can be made to the C825 using the Tapo mobile app, but two of them can call each other directly by tapping a preconfigured onscreen contact. The device feels like another way for aging parents and grandparents to keep in touch with family without having to familiarize themselves with apps, phones, or tablets, but it’s also a useful hands-free video calling solution that can track and keep people in frame as they move around a room.

The C825’s touchscreen can be used as a digital photo frame, weather display, or a clock when you’re not in a video call. And for those times you don’t want the camera watching, you can configure privacy zones through the Tapo app or turn on a privacy mode that points the camera at the ceiling while pausing all monitoring.

The real price of Jensen Huang’s return to Nvidia: Higher regulatory costs for the company — and the entire AI industry

Companies selling robot-training video used to call about privacy once a month. Now it’s several times a week

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Simon Randall is the founder and CEO of Pimloc, which makes video-anonymization software.

Earlier this month, India’s technology ministry probed home-services platforms about cleaners wearing head-mounted cameras inside customers’ homes. The footage trains robots, and officials wanted to know how it was collected, what consent was obtained and whether it was going to AI developers overseas. They won’t be the last to ask.

Training robots this way is new (ish), but the questions aren’t. Over a decade ago, I helped build Autographer, one of the first wearable cameras. Back then, the focus was: “how do I know if I am being filmed?” so we designed privacy into the product. Today, because we’re filmed all the time, people (and their governments) ask: “Where is that footage, who has access and what are you doing with it?”

Today I run Pimloc, which sells software to anonymize sensitive video. Much of our work involves police body-camera footage: shaky, often low-light and full of people who aren’t part of the incident. It’s remarkably similar to what robotics firms now collect. We’ve spent years developing technology to protect people in footage where they move quickly, turn away or appear at unusual angles.

A year ago we were approached by companies collecting or preparing footage for physical AI training roughly once a month. Now it’s several times a week – businesses working across autonomous vehicles, delivery robots, factories and drones.

AI chatbots learned to communicate by reading the internet. A robot has as much chance of learning to fold clothes by trawling search engines as I have of becoming a Michelin-starred chef by reading Julia Child’s Mastering the Art of French Cooking.

So developers are recording real life instead. One startup recently told Fortune it had collected more than a million hours of first-person footage in its first month, and we’re hearing similar figures. Robotics and physical-AI companies had raised nearly $48 billion by early October, according to PitchBook data.

Many of the companies that first approached us were using open-source tools to try and redact footage – far from perfect, but it got them through non-public development and testing.

Now things are different in two ways. First, many of those companies are preparing to sell their footage to large AI developers with exacting standards. A buyer spending serious money on a dataset wants assurances about how it was collected, whether permissions cover its intended use and whether the people in it are protected. Suppliers know they will have to stand behind those claims.

Second, governments are paying attention. India’s inquiry is one example in one territory, of growing scrutiny over how AI training footage is collected and used. Big tech companies and labs looking to develop physical AI products and services have little choice but to push those same questions down to the organizations selling them the footage they need.

One company that approached us recently put it simply: “We need to solve this problem. Redaction is important to us.”

Some companies were created specifically to collect training footage. They can build consent and anonymization into their processes from day one. As India shows, that doesn’t settle every concern for workers or the people they film, but it gives them a headstart

Others have recorded video for years for a different purpose, and are only now discovering its new value. But having captured the footage isn’t the same as having the right to sell it.

Recently, an established business that helps people with everyday tasks asked us about anonymizing years of footage captured by their customers for AI training. We had two immediate questions: could permissions realistically be established, years later, for a use nobody – not least the consumer – had envisaged when the footage was recorded? And could every identifying detail – from people in shot to letters on the fridge – be reliably found and protected?

That discussion is still on-going, but it shows how difficult it can be to turn an archive into an asset after the fact.

For any business sitting on a digital archive, my advice is to start by asking: did the people you meant to film agree to the footage being sold? And is everyone else in it protected? You can’t assume the value without identifying those gaps and addressing them.

Buyers are best placed to make those checks standard. Privacy rules and expectations differ from country to country, as India’s scrutiny of overseas transfers shows, and footage is already crossing borders. If the AI companies paying for it insisted on the same proof of permission and protection wherever it was recorded, every supplier would have to meet that bar.

I’m optimistic about what physical AI could do. But the footage that is increasingly being used to teach it captures far more than the task – people nearby, inside homes, documents, voices and screens in the background. Autographer taught me that what a camera picks up by accident matters as much as what it’s pointed at. Physical AI is learning that lesson at a far greater scale, and buyers have both the power and the incentive to act on it.

Urban Outfitters stock fell 11% after hours following record sales

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Net income rose to $134.1 million from $116.4 million as comparable retail sales grew 4.4% in the quarter

Urban Outfitters reported record third-quarter net sales of $1.67 billion, up 8.9% from a year earlier, and its stock fell 11% in after-hours trading on Tuesday. The stock traded at $75.98 during that session.

Net income was $134.1 million, or $1.54 per diluted share, compared with $116.4 million, or $1.28 per share, in the prior-year quarter. Gross profit rose 9.6% to $617.6 million. For the nine months ended Oct. 31, net sales rose 10.2% to a record $4.81 billion.

Retail segment net sales rose 6.1%, and comparable retail net sales rose 4.4%, with mid single-digit growth in both store and digital sales. Comparable sales increased 8.5% at FP Group, 5.8% at the Urban Outfitters brand and 1.7% at Anthropologie Group. Subscription segment sales climbed 28.0%, and wholesale segment sales increased 18.8%.

Comparable retail sales growth was 8% in the year-earlier quarter.

Earnings per share topped the $1.52 that analysts polled by FactSet expected, while revenue matched Wall Street expectations, according to The Wall Street Journal.

Total inventory rose 10.1% from a year earlier. The company attributed the increase to higher net sales and the timing of inventory receipts.

"Our teams delivered another standout quarter, achieving record third-quarter sales and earnings per share," Chief Executive Officer Richard A. Hayne said in a statement. "Every brand and segment contributed positively to our third-quarter performance, underscoring the strength of our portfolio and disciplined execution across the organization," Hayne said.

In August, the company reported record second-quarter net sales of $1.66 billion, up 10% from a year earlier. It described that period as its eighth consecutive quarter of record sales and profits. Hayne said after first-quarter results in May that the company's largely higher-income customer base had remained engaged despite economic uncertainty.

Debian wakes up wearing Windows XP's actual skin

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Win4Wine runs the genuine Explorer shell atop Linux

Win4Wine is one of the more remarkable and creative hacks we've read about in recent years. It runs Windows XP's genuine Explorer shell through patched Wine on Debian 13, with a custom window manager integrating Linux applications into the desktop.

As its GitHub repository explains: "The taskbar, Start menu, desktop and folder windows are XP's real ones, and Linux applications get XP's frames and taskbar buttons."

This impressive, possibly demented project has English installation instructions and a README, although its design documents are in Japanese. We found Mozilla's built-in translation handled those pretty well.

Its developer goes by miKuta0407 on GitHub, their homepage, and X.

Also note, you're going to need some Windows installation media for the full experience. The README says: "No Microsoft files are included. Only the files that are needed are extracted locally from your own ISO."

It supports Windows XP Professional SP3 installation images in 32-bit Japanese or English editions, with an experimental option for Windows 7 SP1 x64. Without either Windows image, it can download and use ReactOS's free software shell instead, for what it terms "a Windows 2000-like classic look." In our view, that sounds much better anyway.

Before any former Windows users cry hurray and reach for an optical drive, this is not some ready-to-run project. The top of the README warns: "This is a personal, experimental project, not a finished product for daily use." The developer has tested it only on a fresh Debian 13 installation and recommends a VM or spare machine, rather than an existing working desktop, which sounds like an excellent precaution to us.

Also, before you ask, yes, it was vibe-coded with Claude.

We found ourselves reminded of another crazy-but-is-it project that we wrote about back in January: What if Linux ran Windows... and meant it? Meet Loss32. The Loss32 proposal was even more extreme. It suggested replacing essentially the entire Linux userland, so it would be a Linux kernel running Wine with a Windows desktop and Windows apps.

However, Mikuta-san was way ahead of us – lower down, the README mentions and even links to Loss32. Saying that, since it launched at the end of 2025, the bottom of the Loss32 web page shows a couple of updates, reporting on delays in getting it working. That, to be honest, did not shock us, but we still admire the let's-do-this spirit. The developer also seems to have got slightly distracted by a previous project, touchHLE: high-level emulator for early iOS apps.

Loss32, in turn, reminded us of Neptune OS, an attempt to give seL4 a Windows personality transplant. We wrote about that back in 2022, and work on it has continued: the project page shows updates in recent months. The team has also pushed out preview releases in 2022, 2024, 2025, and most recently in September.

This OS face transplant also reminded us of our article, Classic 7 is Windows 10 LTSC cosplaying as Windows 7. Some people really miss the look and feel of vintage Windows. Can someone do Windows NT 4 next, please? ®

A premium peripheral at a reasonable price

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

If you want to enjoy 8K polling gaming accessories on your PC, you can usually have to pay a bit of a premium, even with some comparatively affordable options hitting the market this year. But thanks to Amazon's Black Friday deals, you can save a lot of money on one of our go-to picks.

The GameSir G7 Pro 8K PC is down to $63.99 at Amazon, saving you 20% on its asking price, and marking the first serious discount for this controller since its launch earlier in the year. Some of the controller's various special editions are also reduced, but it's the standard model which is enjoying the best discount. If you've been tempted by an 8K polling controller, this deal is the one to jump on to experience what it's like.

The GameSir G7 Pro 8K hasn't seen a seriously good price cut just yet, since it was launched at the beginning of the year, and this non-Special-Edition model later still. So this is almost undoubtedly the best price you'll get on this 8K polling controller for a while.

There are several models of the GameSir G7 Pro 8K, and the standard black or white model, like the one above, is the cheapest. There are some alternative versions though: the Phantom Blade Zero edition, Aimlabs Edition, and Nioh 3 edition are all down to $71.99. There's also a Royal2 model down to $67.49. Other than the themes changing the color options, though, there's no difference in how they work.

Now, if you're looking for a new controller for a console, let me stop you right here: until the GameSir G7 Pro, the 8K model only works on PC. This ain't gonna play GTA 6 any time soon, that's for sure.

The point of 8K polling is that the controller picks up your inputs more times per second, leading to more accurate gameplay. This is useful if you value your precision. For example, if you play lots of online multiplayer games, GameSir is pitching it as a controller for Esports. When I tested the G7 Pro 8K, it spent a lot of time on Arc Raiders; that's the kind of title it's best suited to.

The flip side is that 8K peripherals are going to really suit you and your gameplay if you play at higher frame rates. Moreover, you're going to need a motherboard and USB port that can make the most of those higher speeds; otherwise, this kind of controller might not be worth it for you. If you've been putting off an internals upgrade for a few too many years, maybe check that your motherboard and USB ports are compatible with an 8K controller.

That doesn't mean you need the latest RTX model, though. The graphics card isn't going to have a say in how your controller functions, so no need to worry about looking for GPU discounts that don't exist right now. I used the controller loads on my road to 100%ing Nioh 3, and it mostly worked totally fine on my RTX 3060 Ti.

If you're looking to use the regular 1,000Hz polling rate, or you want to play on Xbox, the GameSir G7 Pro is reduced down to $63.99, and might be a better buy.

BitMEX co-founder warned the AI sector will implode, pushing talent back to crypto

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Maelstrom Fund's Arthur Hayes said bitcoin could test $50,000 if the Nasdaq keeps falling after seven straight weeks of declines

Arthur Hayes, co-founder of crypto exchange BitMEX, expects startup talent concentrated in artificial intelligence to return to crypto "when AI implodes," he told CNBC in an online interview on Tuesday.

Hayes, chief investment officer at Maelstrom Fund, said bitcoin could retest $50,000 should the Nasdaq $NDAQ extend its slide.

"A lot of talent has gone into the AI space, so I definitely think there'll be a resurgence in good founder talent coming back to crypto," Hayes said, according to CNBC. He also suggested that certain founders might head back to crypto after they "recognize" that Anthropic or OpenAI could "usurp" what they have built.

Hayes said the biggest frontier AI labs are so large and capable that it is hard to "build a real AI business." In his view, "most" founders "raise a bunch of money and then light it on fire."

He expects the AI trade to run into "serious headwinds" during the latter half of 2027, as leading labs start to owe payments on leases for computing capacity and similar commitments that cannot be canceled. Anthropic projects around $451.6 billion in obligations for compute and infrastructure through 2030, and about $70.7 billion of that falls due by the close of September 2027, according to its prospectus.

"Do they have the money? We're going to find out," Hayes said.

Bitcoin slid to a low of $55,142.16 last Saturday and has barely bounced back since. It has dropped for seven weeks in a row, a run that has happened only twice before in its history, according to CNBC. The cryptocurrency peaked at a record $126,279.63 in October 2025.

Hayes called the current market moves a "correlation-led liquidation." Bitcoin and gold have both stayed tied to the Nasdaq so far, he said. "Stuff goes down, you sell crypto first because it's easy, and then you sell everything else," he said, according to CNBC.

Hayes said his preferred scenario is a bitcoin bounce from roughly $55,000 even as the Nasdaq continues to slide. That split would indicate markets are drifting away from a general sell-off and toward pricing in "reflation," as the Federal Reserve supplies additional liquidity, he said.

Once that pattern shifts, Hayes said, Zcash would draw his interest, a token he characterized as a "high beta bitcoin" that places more emphasis on privacy. He named ENA, the token of Ethereum-based platform Ethena, as the other holding he is watching. A bitcoin rally could strengthen returns from funding rates and the futures basis, which would swell Ethena's income and give its USDe synthetic dollar broader appeal, he said.

Hayes had predicted in October that the AI data center buildout would overshoot and crash, with bitcoin rising afterward. He said then that data center providers would seek payment when new capacity is delivered, a point he placed in late 2027 or 2028.

Sterling given suspended sentence for dangerous driving

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Watch: Moment Raheem Sterling arrested after car crash

Former England footballer Raheem Sterling has been given a suspended sentence after admitting dangerously driving his Lamborghini while inhaling nitrous oxide from a balloon on a motorway.

The 31-year-old, from Berkshire, who later crashed into a metal gate during rush hour, gave no reaction as he was handed a 12-month jail term, suspended for 18 months, at Basingstoke Magistrates' Court.

Sterling had pleaded guilty to dangerous driving of a Lamborghini Urus and possessing nitrous oxide on the M25, the M3, the A327 and Minley Road on 28 May

The judge said Sterling's use of the Class C drug had became a "maladaptive coping mechanism during a difficult period" in his career.

Sterling, who left the court without making any comment, was ordered to complete 100 hours unpaid work and was banned from driving for 24 months.

He was arrested after crashing near the M3 in Hampshire.

Raheem Sterling - from national icon to suspended jail sentence

District judge Kirsty Allman told the court on Wednesday that Sterling made a "deliberate and entirely selfish decision" to drive his vehicle "without adequate sleep" and whilst consuming nitrous oxide, commonly known as laughing gas.

She said this exposed a "large number of road users to a significant risk of serious harm by the manner of your driving on two motorways and in peak travel time that morning".

During the September hearing, prosecutor Simon Jones said a number of calls were made between 07:45 and 08:52 BST about a black sports car and the manner it was being driven.

Footage was released of the footballer driving dangerously

Witness Christopher Jennings called 999 after driving alongside Sterling's car on the M25, and "he was shocked by what he saw", Jones said, and made clear "he sighted the defendant inhaling from the balloon".

Fiona Jennings â driving on the southbound M3 â described the driving as "absolutely horrendous" and "swerving in and out of lanes", the court heard.

The court heard Sterling strayed into the central reservation of the M3, exited at junction 4A, and veered off the A327, crashing into a metal gate near a layby.

His vehicle sustained "substantial damage to the tyres and bodywork and was no longer capable of being driven", the court heard.

Sterling was taken to Basingstoke police investigation centre by officers, where he disclosed he had a "problem with nitrous oxide", said Jones.

He complied with an impairment test, but for a blood sample, Sterling asked to obtain legal advice before making a decision.

In the police sergeant's view "he was deliberately failing to provide a clear answer".

Sterling was later interviewed by police with legal representation present where he accepted he had been the driver and provided no comment answers about the driving.

Defending Sterling on Wednesday, Jason Bartfeld KC said there was no dispute that this was a "top category case" because of prolonged driving and "at times" serious impairment which would have been short periods within the driving.

But the defence did not accept the damage to the car was serious and said there was "confusion" over the failure to provide a specimen.

Sterling last played for England at the 2022 World Cup in Qatar

District Judge Kirsty Allman said: "I accept that your character has been positively exemplary.

"You enjoyed a highly successful professional football career and were widely regarded as a role model for many young people through your achievements and public profile."

He was given credit for his guilty pleas.

Sterling most recently played for Feyenoord in the Dutch Eredivisie, having left Chelsea in January, but made just eight appearances in the Netherlands.

In four years at Chelsea - including a season-long loan at Arsenal - he made 59 league appearances after signing from Manchester City in 2022.

With City he won four Premier League titles, having started his senior career with Liverpool before moving to Manchester in 2015.

In the 2021 Queen's Birthday Honours, Sterling was made an MBE for services to racial equality in sport.

Nick Cave, Indian Pavilion Honored in 2026 Venice Biennale Awards, Smithsonian Secretary Responds to Leaked Plans, Marianne Boesky to Open Paris Gallery, and More: Morning Links for November 25, 2026

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

To receive Morning Links in your inbox every weekday, sign up for our Breakfast with ARTnews newsletter.

VENICE VICTORS ANNOUNCED. After a Venice Biennale encumbered by bitter controversy, the exhibition has at last revealed the winners of its top honors, which were chosen this year by public vote instead of a jury, reports ARTnews’s Alex Greenberger. They went to the American artist Nick Cave for his participation in Koyo Kouoh’s main exhibition and India’s national pavilion. The so-called Visitors’ Lions replaced the traditional Golden Lions because the five-person jury for the latter award resigned in May, ahead of the show opening, after stating they would not consider pavilions for the award, which represented countries whose leaders are being charged with crimes against humanity by the International Criminal Court. Read on to learn more about the winners. SMITHSONIAN TAKES A STAND. Lonnie Bunch, the secretary of the Smithsonian Institution, is among the leading figures who responded to a leaked document outlining the Trump Administration’s plans to fire top personnel at the Smithsonian, including the director of the institution’s National Museum of American History, Anthea M. Hartig, reports the New York Times. Though it’s unclear whether the White House intends to carry out the leaked plans, in a letter to staff, Bunch insisted on the Smithsonian’s autonomy. “What remains clear is that the Smithsonian is an independent institution with the responsibility and privilege to present accurate information to millions …” he stated. The memo about the Smithsonian overhaul was photographed on the Washington, D.C. Metro by a fellow traveler who spotted it on the laptop of an aide, who has since been identified by ProPublica as an associate policy adviser within the White House’s Domestic Policy Council. The White House has not responded to requests for comment.

Thorsten Sadowsky has been named director of the Munch Museum in Oslo, following his previous experience as director of the Schleswig-Holstein State Museums Foundation at Gottorf Castle. [Monopol]

The UK government has banned the export of what is believed to be the earliest landscape painting of a British country estate, made by an unknown artist in 1662, and depicting Llannerch Park in Denbighshire, northeast Wales. [The Art Newspaper]

A painting by Austrian Expressionist Max Oppenheimer that was thought lost for nearly a century, sold for $3.91 million, over six times its high estimate at a Dorotheum auction house’s sale in Vienna on November 17. [Artnet News]

The FBI has formally requested a warrant to search devices belonging to a deceased man who was accused of stealing art from Western Michigan University’s Richmond Center for the Visual Art, and the Grand Rapids Art Museum over the summer. [News Channel 3]

After two decades, the fully restored Old Acropolis Museum in Athens has reopened this week with exhibitions featuring archaeology, contemporary art and digital technology, as well as newly accessible areas along the eastern esplanade of the Acropolis. [Finestre sull’Arte]

SORRY, NOT SORRY. In this descriptive profile for the Financial Times, New York dealer Marianne Boesky looks back as her eponymous gallery turns 30. For one, we learn that she plans to open a gallery in Paris next year, her first in Europe. She also reflects on how mothers have had to struggle to make their way in the art world over the years. One memory in particular still stings: In 2006, when Takashi Murakami left Boesky for Gagosian, she recalls that after her daughter Ellie was born, he told her he couldn’t be represented by someone who was “lactating.” “It was devastating for me because it was still so early in my career,” she said. “He didn’t honor his commitments that we had made to each other. That’s when it went south.” Murakami’s studio said the artist had no recollection of saying the comment about breastfeeding. But things were also different then, said Boesky. “At that time, the idea of being a woman in business and feeling fully liberated and autonomous meant that you just kept your kids hidden,” she said. “The next generation, they are present and unapologetic.”

French Senate Rejects Plan to Make Crypto Holders Declare Self-Custody Wallets

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

French Senate Rejects Plan to Make Crypto Holders Declare Self-Custody Wallets

France's Senate has rejected a proposal that would have required crypto holders to declare self-custodied wallets worth more than €100,000 ($110,000) to tax authorities, according to the official record of Tuesday's debate on the 2027 budget.

Two identical amendments, one from the Communist-led CRCE-K group and one from a group of senators led by Anne-Sophie Romagny, were voted down after both the government and the Senate's finance committee opposed them. The vote was earlier reported by Cointelegraph.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short BTC with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

The amendments would have obliged holders to declare self-custodied crypto above the threshold. French taxpayers already have to declare crypto accounts held on platforms, but not crypto held in their own wallets.

"The shop window is illuminated, but the back room remains in the dark," CRCE-K senator Alexandre Basquin said, in comments translated from French. The more closely platforms are monitored, the greater the incentive for large holders to keep their crypto themselves, he argued.

Minister for Public Action and Accounts David Amiel said the EU's DAC8 directive, which requires crypto service providers to report users' transactions to tax authorities, had already brought transparency. A purely declarative obligation would not let authorities track how crypto is used in money laundering or tax fraud cases, he said.

The proposal had already passed through France's lower house. The National Assembly's finance committee backed it in October, prompting a backlash from the crypto industry.

The budget was later rejected in the lower house, so the Senate is debating the government's original bill, without the crypto amendments that deputies had added.

On Tuesday, senators also rejected an amendment that would have made swaps from crypto into stablecoins a taxable event. Amiel argued that stablecoins are not money, as they have no legal tender status and are often unstable. A proposal to extend France's exit tax to crypto holdings worth more than €800,000 ($877,000) was also defeated.

Critics of the reporting plan had warned that a register of large crypto holders could become a target in France, which has become the global epicenter of "wrench attacks," in which criminals use violence to force victims to hand over crypto.

France's national anti-organized crime prosecutor's office recorded more than 70 crypto-linked kidnappings and unlawful detentions in the first eight months of 2026, Decrypt reported in September. Authorities charged 88 people, including minors, in a crackdown in April.

Leaks of personal data have added to those fears. A breach of France's tax authority in August exposed records tied to more than 678,000 people and businesses. In January, a tax agent was accused of looking up crypto investors and prison guards in tax databases for an unidentified buyer, Le Parisien reported, while a June attack in Nancy followed a data breach at French crypto tax platform Waltio.

Ripple Partner SBI Launches $2.3 Trillion-Backed Solana Push in Japan: Main Crypto News This Morning

📅 2026-11-25  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin failed to hold above $64,000 earlier this week, fell below $60,000 and was trading at around $58,580 on Wednesday morning, down 1.97% over 24 hours, according to TradingView data. The Crypto Fear and Greed Index stood at 13 on Wednesday.

Ethereum lost 3.18% to $1,641, Solana dropped 3.72% to $67.86, and XRP fell 2.23% to $0.908 over the same period, according to CoinMarketCap data. The TOTAL3 index, which tracks the market value of altcoins without Bitcoin and Ethereum, was at $621.3 billion. Among large assets, Tron was the exception and traded at $0.3077, up 0.10%.

The combined share of USDT and USDC in the crypto market rose 1.73% to 12.4%. On Tuesday, the Nasdaq 100 closed down 1.99%, and the S&P 500 lost 0.50%. Nasdaq 100 futures were down 2.93% on Wednesday morning, and the VIX volatility index jumped 16.61% to 30.75, according to TradingView data. The yields on 2-year and 10-year U.S. Treasuries fell to 4.604% and 4.848%, respectively.

According to SoSoValue data, U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs saw $231.56 million in net outflows on Tuesday, and U.S. spot Ether ETFs lost another $64.02 million. It was the sixth trading day in a row of outflows for Bitcoin ETFs. Besides, the Coinbase Premium Index has stayed negative for 63 days in a row, according to CryptoQuant data, and Bitcoin has been trading cheaper in the U.S. than on global exchanges.

On Wednesday, SBI Holdings, Sumitomo Mitsui Financial Group (SMFG) and the Solana Foundation announced that SBI Solana Global had commenced full-scale operations. According to the announcement, the three parties completed a series of agreements for a new joint venture structure, and SBI R3 Japan now operates under the new name.

The plans for the venture were first announced on July 13, when the Solana Foundation agreed to take a stake in SBI R3 Japan. Solana said on X that SBI Holdings and SMFG bring "$2.3T in combined assets and 80M+ customers" to the venture.

According to SBI, the company will start with use cases in Japan and then expand across Asia and connect with global on-chain markets. SBI listed four business lines for the venture:

SBI Holdings is one of Ripple's oldest partners in Asia. SBI owns around 9% of Ripple Labs and holds 60% of SBI Ripple Asia, its joint venture with Ripple, and SBI Remit uses XRP for money transfers from Japan to the Philippines. Earlier in November, SBI chief Yoshitaka Kitao publicly confirmed his support for XRP.

Bitwise CIO Matt Hougan also spoke about Solana in an interview with Real Vision. According to Hougan, Solana could become "a $300 billion chain" in five years if it captures 10% of global stock trading through tokenization. Hougan called it "a completely reasonable valuation."

On Wednesday, Binance also launched spot trading of five tokenized exchange-traded funds, called bStocks, against USDT. One of them, IBITB, is linked to BlackRock's iShares Bitcoin Trust, and the others include SOXXB, IGVB, ARTYB and FRHCB.

Ripple also announced a partnership with Yapı Kredi, one of the largest private banks in Türkiye. According to Ripple, Yapı Kredi uses Ripple Custody to power its crypto platform Yapı Kredi Kripto. In June, the Turkish Capital Markets Board (CMB) approved the bank's application to operate as a crypto asset custodian, according to Yapı Kredi's filing on the KAP disclosure platform.

"With XRP among the most widely held assets in one of the world's largest retail crypto markets, banks are rapidly embracing enterprise-grade custody," Ripple executive Reece Merrick wrote on X.

Ripple also posted all sessions from its Swell conference, held on Oct. 27–29 in New York, on YouTube. At Swell, Ripple said that 1,700 institutions use its solutions. On Tuesday, AI project Heurist said on X that AI agents had made more than 10 million inference payments on the XRP Ledger, settled in RLUSD through t54's x402 facilitator.

On Wednesday, charts shared on X showed Bitcoin completely below the lowest band of the Rainbow Chart, a long-term model of the Bitcoin price. According to Glassnode data, the Bitcoin hashrate is down 8% year over year, and it is the first annual decline since China banned mining in 2021. Hashprice also fell to a record low, according to Glassnode. The market capitalization of Strategy (MSTR) fell to $35.7 billion, and the Bitcoin on the company's balance sheet is worth about $51.3 billion.

Joao Wedson, CEO of the analytics platform Alphractal, said on X on Wednesday that retail traders were buying Ethereum and whales and sharks were selling. According to the cumulative volume delta (CVD) data shared by Wedson, orders below $1,000 added $132 million since the Sept. 21 peak. Sharks, with orders from $100,000 to $1 million, sold $4.29 billion, and whales, with orders above $1 million, sold $2.5 billion.

"In my view, a sustainable Ethereum bull market will only return when sharks and whales start buying again," Wedson wrote.

The Ethereum Foundation also published the PQAI #1 roadmap on protecting the network from quantum computers. According to the roadmap, the developers picked the Keccak-256 hash function over faster ZK-friendly options such as Poseidon2 and BLAKE3.

According to CoinGlass data, $362 million worth of positions were liquidated over the past 24 hours, and 81% of them were longs. The U.S. dollar index (DXY) stood at 103.92 and USD/JPY at 159.25 on Wednesday morning, according to TradingView.

BitMEX co-founder Arthur Hayes posted a Bitcoin chart on X when BTC was at $58,773. Hayes wrote that Bitcoin "will retest $55k, hold on to your butts!" On Saturday, Nov. 21, Bitcoin fell as low as $55,150, its lowest price since September 2024.

Some traders took the other side. One of them opened a $500,000 long with 75x leverage and called prices below $60,000 "pure heaven to buy" on X.
