After testing dozens of headphones and earbuds over the past year, I've realized my favorite audio gear has to be open earbuds. Whether heading out for a run or working from home, I reach for open earbuds like the Bose Ultra Open almost every day. Maybe that's why I test pretty much every pair of open earbuds here at Tom's Guide. (In fact, I've personally tested or used every set of earbuds on this list, save the Tozos.)

Open earbuds have quickly become the gold standard for runners and gym-goers who need motivational audio without having to sacrifice situational awareness. And lucky for anyone preparing for their New Year's resolutions, some of the best open earbuds have had their prices slashed for Black Friday.

The latest Bose Ultra Open earbuds are currently $50 off at Amazon. And my favorite open earbuds, the Beyerdynamic Amiron Zero are reduced from $249 to $119 at World Wide Stereo.

But that's far from all. I've put together a collection of the best deals worth grabbing before stocks run out, with over 50% off on some products.

The Tozo OpenEarRing open earbuds are ultra-light for all day long usage. They have a flexible ear cuff for a snug fit, and an impressive 40 hours of battery life. The compact charging case makes it easy to take them anywhere.

The JLab Flex open earbuds are an ultra-budget option at just $29 in this year's Black Friday sales. They offer an ear cuff design that allows ambient sound to enter, keeping you aware of your surroundings. There is a total of 21 hours of battery life with the charging case, so for the price point, this is a great deal. They also offer multipoint connectivity and have JLab’s signature bass capabilities that can be customized in the JLab companion app.

The JLab Epic Open Sport earbuds are super comfortable to wear even for long periods and don’t move around during activities. They’re sweatproof with an IP55 rating and offer a balanced sound profile to keep you motivated with your favorite tunes. The JLab app gives EQ customization settings for personalized sound. At just $51, this is one of the best deals around right now.

The EarFun OpenJump are a good option if you want a pair of workout earbuds, and with 42 hours of battery life, they're reliable for all-day listening. A custom sound profile is useful for fine-tuning your favorite music, and the budget price makes the sound even better.

The EarFun Clip 2 open-ear earbuds are budget-friendly, comfortable, and promote awareness. They boast an excellent 40-hour battery life and reliable call quality, plus a handy AI real-time translation feature. I enjoy listening to my music with these earbuds, and regularly reach for them when working in the office.

The Beyerdynamic Amiron Zero are easily my favorite open earbuds I've ever tested. They have excellent sound quality with impressive detail, and the simple app gave me complete control. They’re comfortable to wear all day and have an IP54 durability rating. The battery life is 20 hours (less than many other models on the market), but this isn’t a deal breaker for me since the sound is so good.

The Beyerdynamic Amiron 200 open earbuds provide sound quality comparable to top in-ear models. The companion app allows for excellent EQ customization, and the 36-hour battery is impressive. The buds lack wear detection, which is slightly annoying. The touch controls are very sensitive, but you can turn them off in the app. I highly recommend them for both workouts and office use.

The Sony LinkBuds Clip earbuds offer the crisp Sony sound I know and love with the convenience of ambient sound. The sound can also be personalized in the Sound Connect app. Call quality is great, even when moving around, and the touch controls are responsive and easy to use. I find them exceptionally comfortable, and this sale price makes them even more attractive.

The Bose Ultra Open Earbuds (2nd Gen) are a welcome upgrade to my favorite headphones, and seeing them discounted already is a real treat. These earbuds are stylish without compromising on audio at all. They are lightweight for a barely-there feel and deliver great audio for music fans who still need some awareness. With up to 28 hours of listening, they're a good option for all-day listeners.

AI debt fears return as CoreWeave’s bond yield tops 17% and futures slide

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

CoreWeave’s bond yield topped 17% as the cost of insuring AI debt rose for Oracle and Nvidia. Man Group says Big Tech is on track to spend more than it makes in cash.

A candlestick chart on a dark screen shows prices sliding lower, mostly in red candles.

The sell-off in artificial intelligence stocks returned on Wednesday. It came two days after Nvidia’s rally on Jensen Huang’s return as CEO. Nasdaq 100 futures slid 3% and S&P 500 futures lost 2% before the open, CNBC reported. Dow Jones futures dropped 682 points, or 1.4%.

Nvidia fell more than 5% in premarket trading. Anthropic dropped about 4.6%, further below its $83 IPO price. Oracle lost about 5%, Alphabet 3% and Dell about 6%, despite Dell’s strong quarterly results, according to CNBC.

Asian markets fell first. South Korea’s Kospi closed 6.16% lower. Japan’s Nikkei 225 lost 2.78%, and Hong Kong’s Hang Seng fell 2.56%. SoftBank Group dropped 9.55%, chip-testing firm Advantest 12.54% and Tokyo Electron more than 10%. Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix each fell about 7%.

In Europe, ASML fell more than 3%, Jason Karaian reported for The New York Times. The wider Stoxx 600 index edged higher, CNBC reported.

Investors in AI debt grew more nervous on Tuesday, Michelle Fox reported for CNBC. CoreWeave’s shares sank 13%, and the yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 rose above 17%.

Credit default swaps insure against a default. The spread on CoreWeave’s five-year swaps jumped 8.5% to more than 1,200 basis points. A wider spread means investors see the debt as riskier. Oracle’s spread rose 7% to 355.68, and Nvidia’s widened by 3.4%. Meta’s and Amazon’s rose by more than 2%, according to CNBC.

Ben Emons, chief investment officer at FedWatch Advisors, called it a waning of the “Jensen effect”.

“Markets see government intervention as ‘moral suasion’ that carries credit and interest-rate risks,” Emons told CNBC.

Huang is considered one of the AI industry’s top financiers, CNBC reported. Nvidia has struck deals with neoclouds such as CoreWeave to buy its chips, some backed by Nvidia guarantees. Nvidia said on its earnings call that it had $118bn in guarantees for AI projects as of 25 October. It also spent $58.1bn on stakes in other companies in nine months.

During Huang’s absence, chief financial officer Colette Kress told analysts that Nvidia would be careful about new investments. That suggested Nvidia would rein in its financing, Paul Meeks of Freedom Capital Markets told CNBC. He said Huang’s return should put that concern to rest.

“He’ll double down on the circular financing,” investor Steve Eisman said on CNBC.

Stifel analyst Ruben Roy took the opposite view in a note on Monday.

“Given scrutiny of disclosure around financing and guarantees … we do not expect Mr. Huang’s return to bring a quick reversion to broader customer financing,” Roy wrote.

John Lloyd of Janus Henderson Investors holds CoreWeave in the portfolios he manages. He told CNBC the company does not need the high-yield market for now.

Microsoft, Amazon, Meta and Alphabet are on track to spend more on capital projects than they generate in cash, in both 2026 and 2027. That is according to Man Group analysis cited by CNBC.

Heavy spenders and debt issuers fell 25% to 45% from their June peaks to Friday’s close, Man Group found. They include Oracle, Alphabet and some chipmakers. Apple, which has stayed out of the AI spending race, rose almost 20%.

PIMCO expects the biggest AI companies to raise about $250bn through bond sales this year, citing Goldman Sachs data. Borrowing across the wider AI industry could reach $300bn to $570bn.

“There are vast capital needs in this AI story. The only problem is there’s just not enough money to go around,” Cole Smead, chief executive of Smead Capital Management, told CNBC.

Dan Taylor, deputy chief investment officer at Man Group, said Big Tech needs spending discipline to rebuild its cash flow. Chipmakers, he said, need those same companies to keep spending heavily.

“It is hard to see how both sides get what they need at the same time,” Taylor said.

The Federal Reserve is widely expected to raise interest rates next month, the Times reported. That would make debt more expensive for companies building data centres. Monday’s rally put December and January hikes back on the table, Steve Liesman reported for CNBC. Traders are also watching reports of new US tariffs and October’s inflation data, due on Wednesday. US markets close on Thursday for Thanksgiving.

Some analysts see a correction rather than a bubble bursting.

“We see the retreat as a broadening, sentiment-driven correction rather than a deterioration in fundamentals or a popping of the A.I. bubble,” Allianz wrote, according to the Times.

So far, the moves do not look like the bear markets of recent decades, Deutsche Bank’s Jim Reid wrote in a note cited by CNBC. Liz Ann Sonders, chief investment strategist at Schwab, told CNBC that the sell-off in AI stocks began before the news about Huang.

The former Megaspeed employee's husband has ties to the Chinese intelligence service, officials have warned.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Just a day after Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as its CEO, U.S. intelligence officials met to deliver a briefing to Congress about one of his love interests and her alleged ties to China's Ministry of State Security, or MSS. Alice Huang has been accused of being a Chinese spy, and her affair with Jensen Huang saw the Nvidia founder resign. But even with Jensen Huang now back in the saddle at Nvidia, attention has turned to whether she posed, or poses, a security threat to the United States as a whole, and its place in the global AI food chain. A New York Times report details the meeting for staff members of the House and Senate intelligence committees and aides to the leaders of both chambers, including its bombshell news that Alice Huang's husband has a much more significant intelligence connection.

The intelligence officials told those in attendance that they still don't fully understand what role Alice Huang played in the saga, but that there is no evidence that she initiated the affair at the behest of Chinese authorities. That being said, they also refused to dispute that she passed intelligence back to the country's top intelligence agency while the affair was underway. Further, the officials told those at the meeting that Alice Huang's husband, Kang Wei, was a more significant figure with respect to any connection to the MSS. In fact, he had at times been an MSS asset with deeper contacts within the agency than his wife. It's been reported that Alice Huang was in contact with the Chinese spy agency from the spring of 2025 until the fall of 2026, while Kang Wei was also in contact with the same agency during that time.

Alice Huang was a Megaspeed employee when it was a customer of Nvidia. The data center company was buying Nvidia's AI chips and is now under a criminal investigation over claims that it was bypassing export controls in an effort to get those advanced chips into China. The company has since been placed on a U.S. blocklist.

Jensen Huang's return to Nvidia came after days of campaigning for his reinstatement, including by U.S. President Trump. Trump later pressured Nvidia to rehire him, going so far as to call into a board meeting to do so. Despite Trump's backing, Jensen Huang must still face Congressional and Senate committees to discuss Nvidia, export controls, and any national security threats brought about by his infidelity.

Donald Trump may have been embroiled in controversy this week for his self-confessed role in getting Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang his job back, but at least he's handling the situation with the grace and dignity you'd expect from a world leader — oh no, hang on, he posted an AI meme.

Bitcoin’s crash protection could make its next selloff worse as US inflation data looms

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Options dealers could amplify price swings between $58,000 and $64,000, even after much of Bitcoin's vulnerable futures leverage has been cleared.

A Bitcoin put can cushion losses for its buyer, while the trades a dealer makes to hedge the same option can make a fresh selloff harder to stop.

In its Nov. 24 report, Glassnode estimated that options dealers could reinforce declines by selling and rallies by buying when Bitcoin traded between $58,000 and $64,000. The US inflation report due Nov. 25 gives that setup a near-term test.

On a seven-day average, 1.7 Bitcoin puts traded for every call, the highest ratio since June 2022, according to Glassnode’s Nov. 24 report.

The put-to-call ratio for outstanding contracts separately reached its highest since May 2021. Put trading includes sellers and positions that serve purposes beyond crash protection, so the volume ratio shows where activity shifted without revealing who took which side.

A put gives its buyer the right to sell at a fixed price, or receive the equivalent cash payoff. The lower Bitcoin finishes below that price, the larger the payoff before the option's cost.

A dealer who sells the put takes the other side of that exposure and can hedge by selling Bitcoin or futures. As Bitcoin falls, the put becomes more sensitive to the decline, and the dealer may need to sell more to keep the hedge balanced.

Gamma measures how quickly an option’s price sensitivity changes. If prices reverse, a dealer in short gamma can buy back part of the hedge, adding to the rebound.

The feedback can be stronger when few buyers or sellers stand ready to take the other side, a pattern that researchers studying equity options found in less liquid stocks.

Glassnode estimated positive dealer gamma in the adjoining $56,000-$58,000 and $64,000-$66,000 bands. Dealers hedging positive gamma can buy weakness and sell strength, helping absorb a move. Those were modeled zones for one snapshot, and new trades or expiries can shift them.

A separate estimate from GEX Monitor from Nov. 24 also showed negative net gamma near $60,000, with the largest concentration by absolute exposure at that strike.

Glassnode’s Nov. 24 estimate put short gamma between $58,000 and $64,000. Bitcoin was near $59,700 later that day.

Within the estimated short-gamma band, dealer hedges can reinforce both a selloff and a rally.

The bands describe an estimate at one point in time. Dealer positions, liquidity and the influence of hedging can change as prices move, trades arrive or contracts expire.

Range widths are proportional to the reported dollar spans. Glassnode’s estimates use Nov. 24 data at 16:00 UTC. The later CryptoSlate observation at 21:12 UTC is rounded to $59,700. The paths illustrate short-gamma dealers hedging a move.

The bands describe an estimate at one point in time. Dealer positions, liquidity and the influence of hedging can change as prices move, trades arrive or contracts expire.

Glassnode estimated that nearly 90% of the leveraged futures longs it had flagged between the prior week’s Bitcoin price and its realized price had cleared. Realized price is the average price at which coins last moved.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The selloff had already pushed into a $55,000-$62,000 cluster of positions at risk of liquidation.

Futures liquidations occur when traders can no longer meet their margin requirements, so options dealers rebalance as prices move even if nobody faces a margin call.

Glassnode reported $4.3 billion of net outflows from US spot Bitcoin ETFs over the 20 trading days covered in the report.

The Bureau of Economic Analysis scheduled its October inflation report and revised third-quarter GDP for Nov. 25 at 8:30 a.m. ET. The inflation report includes the personal consumption expenditures price index (PCE).

Trading Economics listed a 0.3% monthly consensus for core PCE, which excludes food and energy. The Cleveland Fed’s model estimated 0.27% separately.

Investors are watching what the data means for rates after the Fed’s October minutes showed broad support for more tightening if the economy evolved as officials expected.

At derivatives exchange Deribit, Wednesday’s daily Bitcoin options are scheduled to expire at 3 a.m. ET, five and a half hours before the release.

They had about 6,172 BTC of open interest, around 1.4% of outstanding coin-settled Bitcoin options on Deribit, in Deribit’s Nov. 24 options data, against 111,797 BTC in the Nov. 27 monthly expiry.

Deribit’s Nov. 24 coin-settled Bitcoin options book showed 6,172 BTC of gross open interest in the daily expiry before PCE and 111,797 BTC in the monthly expiry after it. The exchange publishes open interest with an eight-hour lag.

Source: Deribit options data · Deribit expiry policy · Deribit public API changelog · BEA release schedule

CryptoSlate estimated the effect of removing Wednesday’s expiry using coin-settled contracts expiring within 30 days.

Assuming dealers held the long side of every call and the short side of every put, contracts set to outlast the release accounted for about 93% of the Nov. 24 model’s net negative gamma.

The Nov. 27 expiry alone accounted for about 49% of the original total, leaving about 7% in Wednesday’s expiry under the same assumptions.

The calculation holds prices and positions at their Nov. 24 readings, so actual gamma can shift as Bitcoin moves and expiry approaches, and the public exchange data counts contracts without identifying dealer positions.

The Nov. 27 put open interest was spread across several strikes, including $60,000, $63,000 and $64,000. At the same $60,000 strike, annualized implied volatility was 50.4% for Wednesday’s expiry, 56.1% for Thursday’s and 54.6% for Friday’s.

The higher Thursday and Friday marks cover the inflation release and other risks over longer periods.

A hot print could lift rate expectations and start a selloff that short-gamma dealers reinforce with hedge sales.

A cool print could ease rate pressure and prompt a rebound that the same dealers reinforce by buying. If inflation comes in close to forecasts, the GDP figures and demand for Bitcoin could matter more.

For the rest of the week, watch whether Friday’s expiry changes positioning and whether spot buyers return. The NYSE closes for Thanksgiving, ending Friday’s core equity session at 1 p.m. ET.

If dealers turn long gamma or liquidity improves enough to absorb their trades, the same catalyst could produce a smaller swing.

Plus: Hackers reportedly used an AI tool to breach US spy agencies.

This is today's edition of The Download, our weekday newsletter that provides a daily dose of what's going on in the world of technology.

AI has intensified investors' interest in public records. Over the last few years, venture-backed startups have raised upwards of $100 million to collect data from thousands of agencies and turn it into AI-powered "signals" that help businesses sell to governments. At the same time, chatbots are making it easier for individuals to file requests.

As agencies struggle to tell whether they’re dealing with a citizen, a chatbot, or a sales pipeline, some states are passing legislation that makes it easier to deny requests. That could potentially make public offices less transparent.

In July 2021, a wildfire burned through a forest on the University of Alaska, Fairbanks campus, stripping away the moss and soil insulating the permafrost below. Five years later, we followed researchers into the forest to investigate how fighting fires could help keep that carbon locked in the ground.

Their probe revealed an escalating problem: As climate change fuels more frequent and severe fires, they’re accelerating permafrost thaw and releasing greenhouse gases. Now, researchers are trying to break that cycle. But how much should we spend, and what risks are worth taking?

Teaching a computer to use human language requires an inhuman amount of data. An LLM can easily churn through a hundred thousand times more words than a person will experience in the process of mastering their mother tongue.

This yawning divide between children and machines is called the data efficiency gap. It raises a tantalizing question for researchers: How can children learn so much with so little?

By reverse-engineering how they learn, scientists hope to create more efficient AI models—and perhaps settle enduring questions about language and children's developing minds. Here’s how they’re trying to close the data gap.

The three fastest cars are Andrew Node's Thrax Truffade, the Seaspray Emerus and the Honeycomb Emerus

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The fastest cars in GTA 6 tend to be incredibly expensive, but you can actually get some for free if you're strategic and know where to look. The fastest car is the Thrax Truffade owned by Andrew Node, a modified version of the standard supercar, but there's other options out there that are a little more reliable. Below I'll lay out all the fastest cars in Grand Theft Auto 6 and how you can get them for yourself.

The top 5 fastest cars in GTA 6 are as follows:

Keep in mind that there's basically no real speed difference between the Progen Emerus GTA 6 cars, they all come in joint equal for the silver medal, and the only real variation is how they look for the most part.

To get the fastest car in GTA 6 you need to get Andrew Node’s Thrax Truffade, a modified version of the standard Truffade that goes even faster. You can get this one for free at any point in the game by doing a certain glitch, though keep in mind that it's much easier after you've obtained the Key Cloner in GTA 6.

Andrew Node's Thrax Truffade is actually unregistered, meaning you can add it to your list of GTA 6 Personal Vehicles at any point. Though it will cost you $70,000 to do so, so make sure you've either built up your personal wealth, or have a garage to keep it in until that point.

You can find the Honeycomb Emerus in Vice City, in a parking lot outside a dealership and car rental spot across from the Hospital. This one is very easy to access – it's basically just outside – but it does require a Key Cloner, and it has Advanced Security and Trackers. This is something you can't really make best use of until the mid-to-late game.

This one is found inside a shipping container at the location in the Vice City County Dockyard. Keep in mind that not only is it illegal for you to be here, you'll need to have unlocked the cut-off tool in GTA 6.

However, if you can reach the area, use Focus mode to identify the one shipping container you can open, evade or KO the nearby security, and use the cut-off tool to get inside. This car is actually unlocked and deactivated – though registering it will cost you $29,900. You'll also need to quickly drive it out of the dockyard to avoid getting arrested and shot to pieces.

You can find the Seaspray inside a garage in Northwest Capri. You can unlock the garage with the Snap Gun in GTA 6, and again, it has all the most advanced car security features. Still, it's very easy to physically get to the car, to one to consider.

The baseline Thrax Truffade is usually found on the back of a trailer at the entrance to the Boone Raceway in Southwest Hamlet, just outside of the entrance. Like the Seaspray and Honeycomb, it's dripping with security features, so you won't be able to keep it until the later stages of the game.

To find aliens in GTA 6 you need to be abducted, then wait for dream to tell you what to do next

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The GTA 6 alien mission starts with an abduction that leads to some weird dreams, and eventually sends you off to literally find what's out there. Spoilers obviously abound for everything I'm about to tell you below, but if you want to see what the latest instalment of Grand Theft Auto's alien stuff is, read on.

To start everything off you first need to be abducted. There's an element of luck here but what you're looking is to see a car by the side of the road with all its doors open and lights going crazy. It seems like there might be a few locations it can happen, and it can only happen at night, but for me it was here in Port Gellhorn:

Follow the cars headlights and you should see some clothes scattered around. Using your flashlight and Focus you'll see more clothes making a trail you can follow. Eventually you'll see a light, and pick up something... else with your Focus. I won't spoil it too much:

Once this sequence plays out you'll get the objective to go back to the woman's car. This is what triggers your own abduction experience. Once that's done you'll wake up by the side of the road apparently unharmed.

To continue the GTA 6 alien quest you'll need to go to sleep to trigger some dreams. It might take a day or two but keep sleeping and you should eventually have some strange dreams. It needs to be the same character that was abducted, obviously, in case you're not seeing anything happen.

This dream is actually an explainer on how to rescue an alien, although there's a tricky puzzle to get past first.

Leaving the room you woke up in will take you to a large dark room full of eggs with a bright light at the center. The eggs will explode if you get too close, and once a few have popped you'll be grabbed by a tentacle. This will restart the dream with the option to 'give up', but don't. If you're having trouble all you need to do is sneak past slowly. If you crouch and keep your speed as low as possible you shouldn't pop any eggs, no matter how angry they look.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

You'll need to find a path through the eggs as it's a bit of a maze, but as long you move low and slow you should be able to reach the light in the middle easily, which will end this sequence.

Once past the alien eggs you'll reach a room with a model solar system in it. The Earth is actually a picture of a location, so examine that and move on.

Next you'll see a keypad with a series of lines over it. These lines denote the alien key pad code and are randomised. For me it was 3214 but you might have something different.

Don't worry about memorising it, as it will be noted like the GTA 6 safe codes you can find elsewhere. Once you're through you'll find a control panel to use. Switch it on and you'll see four buttons you'll need to turn green:

Pushing any button will cycle the light either side of it through off, orange and then green. Using that info you'll need to make all the lights green and then press another button to reveal three doors ahead of you.

Through to door on the left you'l find some milk to fetch, and through the door on the right you'll find a machine you can put the milk in. The door in the middle has a burning sun inside that will kill you. We don't open the middle door.

Get the milk and take it to the machine. Placing it in the slot will then end this whole sequence and you'll wake up.

The radio tower you saw in your dream is at the location shown on the map above - just above Shoeless Keys. Drive there and use your car to encourage the gates to open, as there's barbed wire everywhere. Go around the building to this door and head inside:

Immediately inside you'll see some stairs down to a door locked with a keypad. Use the code you got in your dream to open it. This will then take you down via lift to a control room where you'll find a tape you can play on a table, and then repeat what you learned in the dream:

Putting the milk in the machine will open the door and reveal the alien. However, make sure you have full health before you pick it up. The second you grab the ET poison gas will start flooding the place, and you'll need to get to the lift as fast as possible while your health drains.

Head outside and let the events play out. When it's all over you'll have your health, stamina and focus all permanently increased. For reasons your car will probably be impounded and end up at the police station nearest your current home.

If you are miles from home at this point, simply switch to the other character and then call up and arrange to meet. This will teleport whoever was stranded immediately back to you.

Flipkart CEO Kalyan Krishnamurthy to step down next week amid IPO uncertainty

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Kalyan Krishnamurthy (pictured above), who steered Flipkart through Walmart’s acquisition in 2018 and helped build it into one of India’s biggest e-commerce companies, is stepping down as chief executive next week after more than 11 years with the company.

On Wednesday, Flipkart said it would announce Krishnamurthy’s successor in the coming weeks, and chief product and technology officer, Balaji Thiagarajan, will lead the company until a replacement is found.

A Walmart spokesperson told TechCrunch that Krishnamurthy’s last day at Flipkart will be December 4.

Krishnamurthy joined Flipkart in 2015 from Tiger Global, one of the company’s early investors, and became its CEO in 2017. He remained at the helm after Walmart acquired a controlling stake in the company for $16 billion in 2018.

Under Krishnamurthy, Flipkart said its business grew more than five-fold. The company has more than 500 million registered users and over 1.4 million sellers, including those on Shopsy, its low-cost shopping platform. The company’s marketplace offers over 150 million products across more than 80 categories.

Flipkart has of late moved into newer segments, including quick-commerce through Flipkart Minutes and, more recently, food delivery through a venture called Eat-in by Minutes. The expansion is targeted at Indian consumers who are increasingly turning to quick-delivery services for everything from groceries to electronics.

Thiagarajan joined Flipkart in September last year to lead its product and technology teams. Before that, he had founded Kasu.ai, a Bay Area startup working on AI assistants for corporate finance. He also previously worked at Google, Microsoft, and Uber in senior engineering positions, per his LinkedIn profile.

Krishnamurthy’s exit comes amid uncertainty around Flipkart’s IPO. In March, the company moved its headquarters from Singapore to India as part of its preparations to go public, but in May, Walmart reportedly told Flipkart to delay its IPO and focus on becoming profitable first.

Walmart declined to comment beyond its announcement when asked what prompted Krishnamurthy’s departure, and whether the leadership change would affect Flipkart’s IPO plans. Flipkart did not respond to a request for comment.

Founded in 2007 by Sachin and Binny Bansal as an online bookstore, much like its bigger rival Amazon, Flipkart eventually expanded into other categories. Under Krishnamurthy, it has grown into India’s largest online retailer by gross merchandise value, ahead of Amazon.

“Both Flipkart and Walmart are better because of Kalyan. We are grateful for his leadership, the governance he instilled and his many contributions to the company,” Chris Nicholas, president and CEO, Walmart International, said in a statement.

Huang Is Worth Up to $10 Trillion to the Market, Ives Says

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Dan Ives, senior managing director at Yorkville Ives & Co., discusses Jensen Huang's return as Nvidia Corp. Chief Executive Officer and his value to the AI growth cycle and overall market. "We continue to be bullish, not just on Nvidia, but the overall tech trade," Ives tells Bloomberg Television. "AI is not stopping here despite maybe scarcity of capital," he adds. (Source: Bloomberg)

Solana to $300B? Bitwise CIO Says Tokenized Stocks Could Drive 7.5X Growth

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bitwise CIO Matt Hougan says Solana could reach a $300 billion valuation in five years if it captures 10% of global stock trading through tokenization.

Bitwise Chief Investment Officer Matt Hougan is betting that tokenized stock trading will make Solana one of the largest networks in crypto. In a Real Vision interview published Tuesday, he said Solana could be worth $300 billion within five years if it handles 10% of global stock trading.

He called that “a completely reasonable valuation.” Solana’s market value today is about $40 billion, so his target is roughly 7.5 times higher.

LATEST: ⚡️ Bitwise CIO Matt Hougan says Solana could become “a $300 billion chain” in five years if it captures 10% of global stock trading via tokenization, telling Real Vision it’s “a completely reasonable valuation. pic.twitter.com/ws7EFgBTk2

— CoinMarketCap (@CoinMarketCap) November 25, 2026

Solana’s own fees show how far the network still has to go. It collected $229 million in transaction fees over the past 12 months, about 1/33 of the revenue in Hougan’s scenario.

SOL trades near $67.81, down 77% from its January 2025 record and 2.7% lower on the day.

Hougan spoke to Real Vision at Solana’s Breakpoint conference in London. CoinMarketCap’s report of the interview lays out his math:

A tokenized stock is a blockchain token that tracks a share such as Nvidia or Tesla. It can trade around the clock and settle in seconds.

Hougan was less upbeat on Ethereum, which still holds more tokenized assets overall. He said some ETH holders are “probably going to step out of the asset,” adding: “We’ll see which ends up being more valuable.”

Market Intelligence: Crypto Analyst Predicts Top Solana Meme Coins to Bid

For Hougan’s forecast, the size of today’s market matters. It is growing, but it starts small. The rwa.xyz stocks dashboard counted $3.57 billion of tokenized shares in investors’ hands on Wednesday, held across 5 million wallets.

That last number puts Hougan’s goal in perspective. Ten percent of global stock trading comes to about $2.5 trillion a month. Even counting every token transfer as a trade, tokenized shares on all chains are at about 4% of that.

The key test for Hougan’s case is fees, because his math rests on them. We added up the base and priority fees users paid on the network, using DefiLlama’s Solana fee data.

January 2025 alone, the month of the TRUMP memecoin launch, brought in $241 million, and that memecoin frenzy has faded. Trading in tokenized shares would need to replace it many times over for Hougan’s math to work.

EXPLORE: Elon Musk’s Grok AI Predicts an Explosive Move for Solana Crypto

SOL trades at $67.81, below its 50-day exponential moving average (EMA), an average that gives recent prices more weight, at $87.50, and its 200-day EMA at $94.09.

Market Intelligence: Crypto Analyst Reveals Upcoming Coinbase Listings

Hougan’s tokenized stock forecast gives SOL a long-term story. For the price, the signal to watch is a daily close back above $75.70.

Tate Liverpool to reopen with Surrealism survey after two-year delay

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Concept art for Tate Liverpool redevelopment

Tate Liverpool will reopen next autumn following an extensive four-year redevelopment costing around £30m. The relaunch programme includes a major show dedicated to Surrealism along with the first major retrospective for the Liverpool-based artist Chila Kumari Singh Burman. The key regional UK gallery, located on the city’s Royal Albert Dock, closed in October 2023 and was initially due to reopen in 2025.

Chila Kumari Singh Burman in front of her Winter Commission covering the façade of Tate Britain in London in 2020

Surrealism: Staging Dream Worlds, which will run until spring 2028, will include work by Salvador Dalí, Eileen Agar and Man Ray, among others. “The show will reveal the important role of Surrealism in the history of theatre and dance, from experiments with masquerade, character and role play to major commissions for stage sets and costumes,” says a Tate statement. Works featured include Claude Cahun's photograph of Sheila Legge’s 1936 performance Surrealist Phantom of Sex Appeal, when the artist wore a Dalí-inspired costume in London's Trafalgar Square.

Claude Cahun, Sheila Legge as Surrealist ‘Phantom’, Trafalgar Square (1936)

© Claude Cahun; Image courtesy Jersey Heritage Collections

For the overhaul, Tate Liverpool received £18.6m from the Public Bodies Infrastructure Fund, which is awarded by the UK Department for Digital, Culture, Media and Sport. It also received philanthropic donations from the Garfield Weston Foundation (£3m), the Wolfson Foundation (£1.25m) and the Ross Warburton Charitable Trust, alongside other trusts and individuals.

The UK Ministry of Housing, Communities and Local Government also awarded £10m to the gallery, which it secured through a combined £20m bid with National Museums Liverpool. “We have raised more [than £30m] because there are other costs associated with reinstalling and reopening the museum,” a Tate spokesperson tells The Art Newspaper.

According to a Tate statement, the designs for the new gallery, drawn up by 6a architects, “open up spaces to display the incredible variety of Tate’s collection and host world-class exhibitions. These galleries will sit alongside new public spaces for play, relaxation and learning with views across the Mersey, helping to connect the gallery with the city and the communities that surround it.” The New York and Berlin-based artist Asad Raza will create the first major commission for the gallery’s Art Hall.

Tate Liverpool, which opened in 1988, was designed by James Stirling who converted the northwest corner of a seven-story warehouse standing on the Royal Albert Dock into the five-storey gallery. It received 405,000 visitors in 2022, its last full year, and 660,000 in 2019, before the Covid-19 pandemic.

Helen Legg stepped down as Tate Liverpool director earlier this year, with Jemima Pyne taking over in the interim; a new director will be recruited after the new director of Tate, Jessica Morgan, is in post next year, the spokesperson confirmed. Tate comprises four galleries: Tate Modern, Tate Britain, Tate Liverpool and Tate St Ives.

Solo shows dedicated to Isaac Julien and Sarah Lucas, a survey of contemporary African photography and the controversial "Philip Guston Now" are also on the slate

The £10m public grant is part of a new initiative aimed at equalising quality of living between the north and south

The first stage of splitting the Tate Gallery into a museum of British art and a museum of international modern art takes place this month

SNL Fans Just Made The Thanksgiving Comment About 'Sister' I Was Already Thinking

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I Rewatched SNL's Sister Sketch, And Caught A Few Funny Details

SNL Fans Are Talking About Shane Gillis’ F-Bomb, But How About Mikey Day’s Improv Response?

SNL Finally Released The Second Half Of Its Weirdest Recent Sketch, Ending The Conspiracy Theories

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Bring on the awkward dinner table conversations.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I can’t remember the last time an SNL sketch went as viral as “Sister” did a week ago. While impressions, “Weekend Update” and host and musical guest moments are topics of conversation at Studio 8h every week, everyone and their mother saw the “Sister” sketch, and now all those people are going to be in a confined space with one another over Thanksgiving weekend. The comment section is all over this awkwardness.

I’d already been thinking about “Sister” ahead of my own Thanksgiving celebration. Caretaking has hit my family hard while my mom’s battled cancer twice, and while I have another sister who has helped me with those dynamics, SNL’s John Mulaney sketch really spoke to me, and I just already know it’s going to come up at Thanksgiving for many families dealing with caring for an older family member. For us, it should be a humorous conversation. For many? Not so much.

Written by Chase Rosenberg, Mikey Day, Streeter Seidell, and Simon Rich, the sketch follows brothers who have implemented their “sister” for elder care, and have then stepped back to let her handle everything that comes with that label. It’s really funny, but there are also many rings of truth to it, and it has sparked a lot of conversation from angry siblings who feel burnt out and tasked with doing too much. It's led to so much discourse in the days after its release, I'd imagine it will land on the list of Saturday Night Live sketches we'll talk about for years.

Now, one Internet user thinks it’s going to be a big topic of conversation over the holidays, and many SNL viewers agree the timing was probably intentional for a sketch that was this biting and led to so many personal feelings.

Streeter Seidell dedicated the sketch to his own sister Heidi! I wonder how many awkward moments this will inspire next Thursday. 😂

In fact, lots of people think the timing of the SNL sketch was perfect or awful, depending on what you think about it. I don't want to harp too hard

One viral tweet also noted the release of the sketch just before family get togethers had to be “deliberate." (Again, it's definitely interesting.)

SNL gets a bad rap, often deservedly so. But this is a sketch we'll be talking about for decades. Look at the replies and quote-tweets: person after person after person saying "this is my life", or "I'm Sister." Dropping this right before Thanksgiving had to be deliberate, right?

To note, I don't personally agree with the bent that SNL deserves a bad rap. The show has had ups and downs as long as I've watched it, and silly bits can sometimes drive home harder than some of the political or Update stuff. While not on topic to Thanksgiving like the Adele-themed "Thanksgiving Miracle" sketch with Aidy Bryant, I do think this is one we will continue to watch and discuss this week.

I'd just recommend people do so before people are too hyped up on tryptophan, alcohol and sugar.

She oversees news content, hiring and training for the site, and her areas of expertise include theme parks, rom-coms, Hallmark (particularly Christmas movie season), reality TV, celebrity interviews and primetime. She loves a good animated movie. Jessica has a Masters in Library Science degree from Indiana University, and used to be found behind a reference desk most definitely not shushing people. She now uses those skills in researching and tracking down information in very different ways.

DSA Is Right: Congressman-Elect Mocked For Refusing TSA Facial Scan—But He Brought A Passport Card, And The Photo Is Optional

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A newly elected congressman is getting mocked for refusing a TSA face scan, with commenters telling him to take the bus or train instead. The post sharing his video accuses him of refusing to have identification. But Chris Rabb says he brought a U.S. passport card, which is accepted ID. And the checkpoint photograph he declined is optional. The democratic socialist gets some of the explanation wrong. However, the people demanding his arrest appear to have a bigger problem with the rules than he does. Rabb, a Pennsylvania state representative, was elected to represent Philadelphia in Congress and takes office in January. In the video, he says he’s returning to Philadelphia from Boston. He says he doesn’t have a REAL ID card. He got a passport card so he wouldn’t need to carry his actual passport. He says TSA’s equipment wouldn’t process his identification, employees asked for another ID, and he didn’t have one with him. He also says he’d told them he didn’t consent to the photograph. DSA Congressman Chris Rabb already working for the people by refusing to let TSA take his photo and also refusing to have an ID card. So brave. https://t.co/DnZSKeKZ7e — Lee Kuan Yimby (@LeeKuanYimby) November 23, 2026 He Brought A Type Of ID TSA Accepts The State Department says a U.S. passport card can be used for domestic air travel instead of a REAL ID driver’s license. It fits in a wallet. It doesn’t work for international air travel, but Boston to Philadelphia isn’t international. REAL ID enforcement doesn’t mean every passenger needs a state license with a star. A passport, passport card, and other documents on TSA’s accepted identification list are acceptable. Ye replies include “Arrest his ass,” “poor Comrade,” “Bro just ride the train,” and sarcastic declarations of bravery. I have plenty of disagreements with democratic socialists. I’ve criticized Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez’s intervention in American Airlines’ maintenance dispute and Bernie Sanders getting airline taxes wrong. That doesn’t make a socialist’s privacy objection wrong. And disliking someone’s glasses isn’t a reason to demand he surrender a choice even the government says he has. You Can Decline The Photo. You Still Have To Complete Screening. Rabb’s most useful advice is simple: Just say, “I don’t consent to being photographed.” That’s TSA’s published policy. Travelers can decline facial recognition and use the standard ID verification process instead. Rabb Wants To Avoid Being Put In A Database Rabb says he’s trying to avoid being put into another database. Too late! The standard checkpoint camera compares your face to the photograph on the ID you present. TSA says the image isn’t saved after a positive match, apart from limited testing used to evaluate the technology. You don’t necessarily have to believe them. Touchless programs can work differently. Identifying an enrolled traveler using a gallery of existing government photographs doesn’t add you to the database, you’re already there. Opting out doesn’t remove your travel records. The government already receives passenger information for Secure Flight screening. And obtaining a passport card already means you supplied a photograph. There are cameras everywhere, but for the most part there’s so much data that at least we benefit from security through obscurity. At least we did before AI made it all so much more easily searchable. I’ve covered passenger information collected for aviation security being used to help immigration agents intercept travelers. Law enforcement has access to passenger data even without this additional photo. Positive ID Matches Don’t Contribute Much To Security Positive identification is supposed to make sure you can’t simply fly under somebody else’s name. That’s necessary for the government’s database of people they want to bar from flying or ensure receiving extra screenig works. Of course, the database itself is corrupted. The terrorist screening database has over two million people in it. I’ve written about the sprawling watchlist system and agents using the no fly list to retaliate against people who wouldn’t become informants. Identity checks also serve a separate economic purpose for airlines. Matching the traveler to the name on a nontransferable ticket helps enforce price discrimination. If you could buy a cheap ticket months ahead and freely sell it to someone traveling tomorrow, you’d compete with the airline’s expensive last-minute fare. Preventing that resale helps airlines separate passengers willing to plan ahead from those willing to pay more. It doesn’t create a real aviation security benefit. I Made My Own Compromise Long Ago I’m an old line civil libertarian on this. I was late signing up for Global Entry in 2013 because I didn’t like volunteering fingerprints and a photograph to the government in exchange for easier travel. When I finally joined, before the Edward Snowden revelations, I was already writing about government collection of cellphone geolocation data, license plate readers, and other surveillance. The government already had the information, so I might as well get the benefits. At the same time, I think we’ve largely lost this argument in practice. I’ve written about the spread of checkpoint photography and biometric boarding gates becoming normal. The surveillance state has won. Rabb himself concedes that his refusal won’t make much practical difference. He sees value in reminding people they can object and in resisting normalization. I have sympathy for this. What To Do If You Want To Opt Out Bring an accepted physical ID and make the request before the camera takes your picture. “I’m opting out of the facial photograph. Please use the standard ID check.” Hand over the document and cooperate with that check. Separate a camera objection from a document problem. If the reader won’t process a passport card, ask for a supervisor and an alternative document check. Carrying a second accepted ID can save time if something fails. With millions of passengers processed per day small failure rates mean actual failures happen regularly. Escalate a refusal of the opt out calmly. TSA says you can request a supervisor or passenger support specialist. Record the airport, checkpoint, date, time, and what was said, and submit a complaint. They’ll go ahead and ignore or dismiss it. Don’t confuse any of this with arriving without allowable identification. Since February, TSA’s ConfirmID requirement costs $45. The payment covers a ten day travel period and doesn’t guarantee identity verification. Meanwhile, at the border, U.S. citizens can request an alternative inspection. Noncitizens, including permanent residents, generally cannot claim the same photo opt-out. Rabb will shortly have a better tool than an argument at a document podium: a seat in Congress. He can press for enforceable limits on retention and sharing, a meaningful manual alternative, and a way to challenge bad database entries. It won’t matter, because the security state is far more powerful than he is. But I’d be happy to see a democratic socialist spend some of his time constraining government power over travelers. The democratic socialist gets some of the explanation wrong. However, the people demanding his arrest appear to have a bigger problem with the rules than he does.

Rabb, a Pennsylvania state representative, was elected to represent Philadelphia in Congress and takes office in January.

In the video, he says he’s returning to Philadelphia from Boston. He says he doesn’t have a REAL ID card. He got a passport card so he wouldn’t need to carry his actual passport. He says TSA’s equipment wouldn’t process his identification, employees asked for another ID, and he didn’t have one with him. He also says he’d told them he didn’t consent to the photograph.

It doesn’t work for international air travel, but Boston to Philadelphia isn’t international. REAL ID enforcement doesn’t mean every passenger needs a state license with a star. A passport, passport card, and other documents on TSA’s accepted identification list are acceptable. Positive ID Matches Don’t Contribute Much To Security Positive identification is supposed to make sure you can’t simply fly under somebody else’s name. That’s necessary for the government’s database of people they want to bar from flying or ensure receiving extra screenig works. Of course, the database itself is corrupted. The terrorist screening database has over two million people in it. I’ve written about the sprawling watchlist system and agents using the no fly list to retaliate against people who wouldn’t become informants. Identity checks also serve a separate economic purpose for airlines. Matching the traveler to the name on a nontransferable ticket helps enforce price discrimination. If you could buy a cheap ticket months ahead and freely sell it to someone traveling tomorrow, you’d compete with the airline’s expensive last-minute fare. Preventing that resale helps airlines separate passengers willing to plan ahead from those willing to pay more. It doesn’t create a real aviation security benefit. I Made My Own Compromise Long Ago I’m an old line civil libertarian on this. I was late signing up for Global Entry in 2013 because I didn’t like volunteering fingerprints and a photograph to the government in exchange for easier travel. When I finally joined, before the Edward Snowden revelations, I was already writing about government collection of cellphone geolocation data, license plate readers, and other surveillance. The government already had the information, so I might as well get the benefits. At the same time, I think we’ve largely lost this argument in practice. I’ve written about the spread of checkpoint photography and biometric boarding gates becoming normal. The surveillance state has won. Rabb himself concedes that his refusal won’t make much practical difference. He sees value in reminding people they can object and in resisting normalization. I have sympathy for this. What To Do If You Want To Opt Out Bring an accepted physical ID and make the request before the camera takes your picture. “I’m opting out of the facial photograph. Please use the standard ID check.” Hand over the document and cooperate with that check. Separate a camera objection from a document problem. If the reader won’t process a passport card, ask for a supervisor and an alternative document check. Carrying a second accepted ID can save time if something fails. With millions of passengers processed per day small failure rates mean actual failures happen regularly. Escalate a refusal of the opt out calmly. TSA says you can request a supervisor or passenger support specialist. Record the airport, checkpoint, date, time, and what was said, and submit a complaint. They’ll go ahead and ignore or dismiss it. Don’t confuse any of this with arriving without allowable identification. Since February, TSA’s ConfirmID requirement costs $45. The payment covers a ten day travel period and doesn’t guarantee identity verification. Meanwhile, at the border, U.S. citizens can request an alternative inspection. Noncitizens, including permanent residents, generally cannot claim the same photo opt-out. Rabb will shortly have a better tool than an argument at a document podium: a seat in Congress. He can press for enforceable limits on retention and sharing, a meaningful manual alternative, and a way to challenge bad database entries. It won’t matter, because the security state is far more powerful than he is. But I’d be happy to see a democratic socialist spend some of his time constraining government power over travelers. Rabb says he’s trying to avoid being put into another database. Too late!

Touchless programs can work differently. Identifying an enrolled traveler using a gallery of existing government photographs doesn’t add you to the database, you’re already there.

Opting out doesn’t remove your travel records. The government already receives passenger information for Secure Flight screening. And obtaining a passport card already means you supplied a photograph.

There are cameras everywhere, but for the most part there’s so much data that at least we benefit from security through obscurity. At least we did before AI made it all so much more easily searchable.

Separate a camera objection from a document problem. If the reader won’t process a passport card, ask for a supervisor and an alternative document check. Carrying a second accepted ID can save time if something fails. With millions of passengers processed per day small failure rates mean actual failures happen regularly.

Escalate a refusal of the opt out calmly. TSA says you can request a supervisor or passenger support specialist. Record the airport, checkpoint, date, time, and what was said, and submit a complaint. They’ll go ahead and ignore or dismiss it.

Don’t confuse any of this with arriving without allowable identification. Since February, TSA’s ConfirmID requirement costs $45. The payment covers a ten day travel period and doesn’t guarantee identity verification.

Meanwhile, at the border, U.S. citizens can request an alternative inspection. Noncitizens, including permanent residents, generally cannot claim the same photo opt-out.

Rabb will shortly have a better tool than an argument at a document podium: a seat in Congress. He can press for enforceable limits on retention and sharing, a meaningful manual alternative, and a way to challenge bad database entries. It won’t matter, because the security state is far more powerful than he is. But I’d be happy to see a democratic socialist spend some of his time constraining government power over travelers.

Ukraine passes bill to legalize crypto and tax profits at 23%

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ukraine’s parliament passed a crypto regulation bill after three years of legislative work, with the new rules set to take effect in July 2027.

Ukraine’s parliament passed a bill Wednesday to regulate the cryptocurrency market and tax individuals’ profits from digital asset transactions.

The bill secured 271 votes in its final reading. Its main provisions would take effect on July 1, 2027, pending presidential signature.

Individuals would generally pay an 18% personal income tax and a 5% military levy on their net annual crypto profits. Crypto-to-crypto swaps would be exempt, and investors could carry forward losses to offset future crypto gains, Ukraine’s state news agency Ukrinform reported.

Investors who bought crypto before the rules take effect could opt for a lower income tax rate if they sell those holdings for fiat currency by the end of 2028. Under that option, the 5% income tax and 5% military levy would both apply to the full sale proceeds.

The National Bank of Ukraine would share oversight of the crypto market with the country’s National Securities and Stock Market Commission. The central bank would oversee issuers of stablecoins pegged to a single official currency.

The bill would also set rules for admitting foreign tokens that can function as electronic money to the Ukrainian market, central bank Governor Andriy Pyshnyy said.

Fifth Circuit Dismisses Lawsuit Over Facebook Removal, Supposedly Connected to Stanford Internet Observatory, of COVID-Related Post

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mostly law professors | Sometimes contrarian | Often libertarian | Always independent

From Hines v. Stamos, decided yesterday by Judges W. Eugene Davis, Leslie Southwick, and Stuart Kyle Duncan:

Jill Hines sued Stanford University, Alex Stamos, and others, alleging they conspired with the government to censor her online speech about COVID-19. The district court dismissed her suit without prejudice for lack of subject-matter and personal jurisdiction. Because Hines has not shown that her injury is fairly traceable to Stanford or Stamos, she lacks standing. We therefore AFFIRM without reaching personal jurisdiction….

This case is closely related to Murthy v. Missouri, which guides our resolution of this appeal. In Murthy, Hines (along with others) sued federal officials and agencies, alleging the government censored her online speech against COVID-19 restrictions in violation of the First Amendment. Here, Hines challenges Defendants' participation in an academic project named the Election Integrity Partnership (EIP). She alleges the EIP and its participants aided the government's scheme to have Facebook suppress her online speech, also in violation of the First Amendment. {Our review is limited to Hines's claims against Stanford and Alex Stamos, the former director of the Stanford Internet Observatory, which was an EIP member.}

Facebook began moderating COVID-19 content early in the pandemic, deleting or demoting posts it deemed false. In April 2020 alone, it labeled roughly 50 million pieces of content. That same month, Hines launched a grassroots campaign to challenge COVID-19 government restrictions and used Facebook to communicate with her supporters. She was aware of Facebook's moderation and took deliberate measures to circumvent it.

The EIP, a consortium of researchers and institutions, was formed in July 2020. It sought to identify misinformation about elections and voting. For about three months in 2020, it monitored elections-related content on social media. EIP participants sometimes recommended actions to social-media platforms and logged their communications when they did. In most instances, the platforms took no detectable action on those recommendations….

To have standing, a "plaintiff must have (1) suffered an injury in fact, (2) that is fairly traceable to the challenged conduct of the defendant, and (3) that is likely to be redressed by a favorable judicial decision." For the claims before us, the second inquiry—traceability of Hines's past injuries—is "the whole ball game." …

There is one injury that Hines claims is traceable to Defendants: on September 9, 2020, Facebook removed a post from Hines's page that attached an article entitled Masks Don't Work. At the time, Facebook explained the post went "against our Community Standards." It also reduced distribution of Hines's page on COVID-19 restrictions for "repeated" violations of its standards on "Inauthentic Behavior" and for "repeated sharing of false news." Hines does not dispute that these restrictions fit Facebook's own content moderation…

Hines's traceability theory rests on an inference drawn from timing. On September 8, 2020, the EIP received a massive submission of over 200 webpages and social-media posts from the Democratic National Committee. Among those 200 webpages was a single post by Hines where she encouraged her followers to lobby their representatives to end the COVID-19 public-health emergency. In Hines's view, the one-day timing between the DNC's submission and Facebook's moderation of her page is evidence that EIP participants called Facebook's attention to her single post in the 200-webpage DNC submission.

We are not persuaded by her argument. Discovery revealed no communication between the EIP and Facebook about Hines, and Facebook did not remove or moderate the post that the EIP received. {Hines argues there may be evidence of Defendants' communications with Facebook in undiscovered Slack or Zoom messages. But Stanford produced "communications and other documents" mentioning Hines or her account, and Hines neither challenged that production nor moved to compel additional discovery, although the scheduling order allowed for that. The mere possibility of undiscovered communications that were not sought in the district court is not evidence.}

A notation in the EIP's records even says Hines's COVID-19 posts were beyond the scope of the EIP's elections-oriented work. We conclude as a matter of law that timing alone is too attenuated to trace Hines's injury to Defendants.

Murthy also requires us to consider Facebook's own incentives to moderate content. Facebook gave its own reasons for acting when it removed Hines's post and demoted her page, and Hines does not dispute that that moderation aligned with Facebook's COVID-19 policies. We agree with the district court that there's no indication that Facebook, in moderating Hines's post, did anything other than follow its own moderation policy….

I am not at all involved with Stanford's defense of this lawsuit.

Elisabeth S. Theodore, John B. Bellinger, III, R. Stanton Jones, and Stephen K. Wirth (Arnold & Porter Kaye Scholer, L.L.P.) and James A. Brown (Liskow & Lewis, A.P.L.C.) represent defendants.

NEXT: Today in Supreme Court History: November 25, 1757

Eugene Volokh is the Thomas M. Siebel Senior Fellow at the Hoover Institution at Stanford, and the Gary T. Schwartz Distinguished Professor of Law Emeritus and Distinguished Research Professor at UCLA School of Law. Naturally, his posts here (like the opinions of the other bloggers) are his own, and not endorsed by any institution. He is also the co-host of the Free Speech Unmuted podcast.

Bobbi Althoff on Tradwives, Reply Guys, and Sharing a Bed With Drake

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bobbi Althoff has a baby face and a vocal fry. Her tenor––which you’ve likely encountered in your TikTok feed, whether or not you know her name––is vaguely baritone and sharply deadpan, an oddly melodic gravel on the eardrum. Depending on who you ask, it’s either endearing, or lazy, or simply slutty. Despite rising to fame on a skittish gaze, the 29-year-old MomTokker turned podcaster turned non-Mormon cast member of Secret Lives of Mormon Wives is now holding eye contact.

We’ve known each other for 20 minutes. She puts down her fork and palms her phone. She wants to read me something, she says, her gemstone-encrusted ring (reading “MOM”) catching the sunlight streaming into The Dominick hotel, where Althoff is staying during the unseasonably warm week of the Mormon Wives premiere.

It’s an essay entitled “I Hate Myself,” written by Althoff when she was a teenager mired in a bout of depression and anxiety made worse by bullies at school. I’d asked what she would title her memoir, if she were to write one, and how she handles online hate, from being accused of sleeping with Drake after interviewing him for her podcast to rumors that her marriage ended due to infidelity to scrutiny about her appearance. “Can a girl gain some weight in her face without you guys fucking saying she has cheek filler?” she wrote on Instagram. “Can a girl be insecure about her upper lip and get some fucking lip filler without you guys fucking commenting on it?” Althoff has been at the center of myriad social media firestorms, some motored by straightforward, internet-enabled misogyny and others by exasperation with her indirect, vaguely hostile approach to engaging with her podcast guests.

She takes a breath, and begins reading. “I want so badly to be able to show everyone the real me, the person that is hurt and is scared to trust anyone, the little girl that is funny, loud, and doesn't care what other people think. Instead of being myself, I create a different personality to satisfy everyone's needs. I hide the real me behind fake smiles and high-pitched laughs.” She pauses, then chuckles (at a normal pitch).

“I was 14 when I wrote that,” she continues. “And that was before ChatGPT. I wrote that myself.”

A smattering of the many awkward—or awkwardly flirtatious—and reliably fury-baiting encounters that have gone down on Althoff’s The Really Good Podcast since its inception in 2023: She and Jason DeRulo LARP a date that gets interrupted by an attempted larceny. While next to him in bed, she informs Drake that Aubrey is a girl’s name. She accuses Michael Cera of founding Cerave, then argues with him about whether his wife needs to know that their feet touched. Lil Yachty threatens to teach her to dance. An oral mishap cum Freudian slip—did she say magician or musician?—in a conversation with Sukihana appears to spark a feud, but instead jumpstarts a friendship and another podcast, this year’s surprisingly raw and affectionate That’s BS with Bobbi and Suki.

Despite her reputation as an invulnerable, even defensive interlocutor, Althoff is strikingly invested in self-exposure. She has spoken openly about her severe struggles with mental illness, insecurities around beauty, her plans to get breast implants, and her painful experience with botched Botox injections. Her penchant for oversharing, she told me, startles her interview subjects, inspiring their own surprising and frank disclosures in return.

“I’m like, hi, nice to meet you, I tried to kill myself,” she tells me. From a young age, she has used comedy to cope with the indignities of womanhood, and she comes by her wry, bluntly charming humor honestly. “Mom, by the time you read this, I’m going to be chilling with Satan,” she remembers writing in a note she left for her mother as a teenager when, she tells me, she attempted suicide. “Keep in mind, I literally was so religious that I accepted that I was going to go to hell for killing myself.” Althoff grew up in a hyper-religious Christian household in California, where she was taught to believe in a classic set piece of Hell that involved “being burned alive forever.”

Althoff has since left the Church, and with it the “conservative worldview” that characterized her adolescence and early adulthood, leading her to get married at the age of 22. Now, she says, she is effortfully de-centering men after “being told that my purpose in life was to be a wife.” Which brings us to her latest venture: divorced, un-religious, founding cast member on a reality show that purports to follow a cabal of Mormon wives living in Orange County. Althoff says her friendships with fellow cast members have been formative and healing, her first experiences with genuine connection among a group of girls after a childhood defined by bullying and a young adulthood spent slaloming down social media slopes riddled with misogynistic vitriol that came from women just as often as it did men.

With her star turn as one of two non-Mormon, unmarried cast members in Mormon Wives, Althoff says she is she surrendering the control over her image that comes with having her own editing team for her podcast, which entails being “completely myself” to a “terrifying” degree. Where the original iteration of Mormon Wives, set in Salt Lake City, centered a pre-existing friend group, the OC spinoff opens on a cast of highly competitive loose acquaintances largely connected by a rabid desire to build their individual audiences. Entering the arena as the most random cast member makes watching Althoff build actual friendships in real-time bizarrely affecting. Instead of sitting safely in the interviewer’s chair, coquettishly crossing her legs and batting her eyelashes, this time those eyelashes are drenched in tears, and she’s not hiding the streaks of mascara dripping down her cheeks.

Those cheeks were once pumped full of Botox, which caused serious facial paralysis (a relatively rare side effect). But enduring a period in which she “couldn’t smile” inspired a shift in her approach to beauty standards and an appreciation for human foibles over cosmetic perfection. “I have been trying to find beauty in the fact that I’m not perfect,” she tells me. “Do I not like my cellulite? Yes, but, I can show it and it’s fine because I’m human. My boobs are saggy: I literally breastfed both of my kids.” Doing reality television also led to an epiphany: she’s been too hard on herself about her appearance. This occurred to her watching the show back with her new castmates-turned-friends, women who she finds beautiful. “I actually don’t need all this stuff,” she says. “I don’t need to have a perfectly chiseled nose and perfect boobs.”

Some might wonder whether joining the Mormon Wives cast, comprised largely of buxom, blonde, traditionally attractive women who in previous iterations of the show have attended group Botox appointments as a form of socializing, might be a counterintuitive way of deprogramming conservative aesthetic norms. But Mormon Wives runs on the tension inherent in its premise: these are women famous for being religious tradwives, who repeatedly slip and fall into subverting tradition: they are breadwinners, they are in bisexual throuples, they talk about sex on camera (even if they insist they only engage in it in the marriage bed). Perhaps most poignantly, they seem to find more emotional sustenance in their relationships with each other than any of their largely deadbeat, cheating, whining, soda-chugging boyfriends and husbands.

In the show’s premiere, she corrects an offscreen producer who calls her an influencer––she’s an internet personality. Online, she is disembodied, cut into clips, context-free and content-forward. And like many of today’s hyper-contemporary internet personalities, Althoff is paradoxically very famous and utterly unknown. As in: she has almost four million followers on Instagram, and every straight man I mentioned this profile to immediately had multiple, highly specific inquiries about interactions she’s had with male celebrities. (“What was it like to share a bed with the 6 God?”) Meanwhile the majority of girls, gays and professional podcasters I brought Althoff up to started at me blankly, evincing not even a dreg of familiarity with her work.

Her legions of followers cannot, in large part, be called “fans,” but do include avid viewers, reply guys, bewildered women, judgmental tradwives, hate-watchers, and pop culture fanatics. She collects connoisseurs more than disciples. As such, Althoff is a cipher, and a harbinger of a novel vector of celebrity that’s potentially liberating for women who’ve been trained to seek validation, adoration, and eternal “relevance,” as she puts it. This makes her an intriguingly modern economic actor. When she is recognized in public, she doesn’t tell her children that they just met a few of Mommy’s fans. “I say, these are all Mommy’s coworkers,” she tells me, seeming sincere as she emphasizes that she wouldn’t want her children to think she was traditionally “famous.” But she clearly can’t resist a facetious acknowledgment of how successful her campaign for relevance has been: “They think I have coworkers everywhere.”

She “judges relevance by incoming brand deals,” which means she’s willing to maneuver to maintain your attention. “My audience has a lot of men who are just wishing they could see me naked,” she says, a reality she enthusiastically “manipulates a little bit.” She knows her combative approach to flirtation sparks outrage among her male viewers. She’s also aware that many of those men resort to infantile insults, which is where her deadpan self-deprecation comes in: “I want to beat people to the punch,” she claims. “You can't call me ugly and have it hurt me because I called myself ugly first. You can't say I'm not funny: I've already said it.”

When I relay to Althoff some of the questions my straight male friends had for her, she admits the rabid speculation around her sex life is frustrating but also flattering. “They obviously think I had a chance with these guests,” she deadpans. “There’s no interaction I can have with a man where people aren’t like, Oh, you guys fucked.” Her haters, she’s observed, fall into two camps: Trump voting white men “who think they would never have a chance with me” and believe unmarried mothers to be morally corrupt. On the flip side are “women who are jealous.”

Her political leanings––which have veered far to the left since her childhood––periodically foment some turmoil among her new girl squad, but Althoff says she also finds much to admire in her conservative cast members lifestyles. “Do I agree with everything in the Church? No. But I see the beauty in the tradwife life.” The relationship between McCall DaPron, a fellow cast member and frenemy turned pseudo sister of Althoff’s, inspired her to break up with her ex, because she saw a healthy dynamic missing from her own relationship. She is committed to maintaining her conviction around political issues her cast members might rather not address: “Does it bother me when I’m the only one in the cast posting about certain things happening in the world? Yes. Fuck ICE, Free Palestine. Free Congo. Free Sudan.”

She hopes her onscreen proto-sister wives, and viewers like them, might allow her to “burst that bubble a little bit”—to widen their perspectives, open their eyes, and inspire re-evaluations of issues close to Althoff’s heart. Admittedly, as the show illuminates, sometimes that means earnestly defending a political position, and other times it means (antifascistly?) refusing to follow the dress code one of the other women attempts to enforce at a party. Observing that male guests on her podcast “always rank themselves a ten out of ten, she notes that “men could be a little more realistic.” She’s not attacking all men, she clarifies, but she would like to alert them to the following: “I don't need to fuck every man that hits on me.”

She adds, with the sprightly sarcasm that’s become her trademark, one final message to readers of this magazine: “Men who read GQ: be nicer to me. I’m not mean, I don’t hate you, don’t hate me.”
