Is GEO Visibility Delivering Business Value, Or Are We Measuring The Wrong Outcome? via @sejournal, @billhunt

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Learn how to prioritize page fixes, check agent access and measure readiness for your 2027 SEO plan.

Where search leaders are putting 2027 budget, plus the AI visibility scorecard that replaces session reporting.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Regain Control Of Google Ads: Better Tracking, Clearer Proof Of ROI

GEO visibility can hide gaps that keep buyers from shortlisting your brand, while Decision Coverage helps identify the evidence needed to earn recommendations.

Recent research is raising questions about the commercial value of AI visibility. It also exposes a problem with what we have chosen to measure.

Rand Fishkin recently asked whether AI visibility is becoming a marketing boondoggle. In a Whiteboard video shared on LinkedIn, he discussed several studies questioning the commercial returns from visibility in standalone AI assistants, while distinguishing those platforms from Google’s AI-powered search experiences.

I understand the skepticism. We have seen an extraordinary amount of enthusiasm for GEO measurement, accompanied by new tools, new metrics, and a growing expectation that brands should monitor how often they appear in AI-generated answers. Much of that work is useful. I want to know whether a company appears when prospective customers search for its category, how its capabilities are presented, and which competitors are recommended.

For some time, I have been concerned that we are investing heavily in tracking mentions, citations, and share of voice without giving enough attention to the decisions customers are asking AI systems to help them make. We seem to have accepted that being mentioned is inherently valuable, even when we cannot demonstrate that the mention helped a customer evaluate the company or move toward a purchase.

I’m also concerned about this given the executive attention SEO, GEO, and AEO are receiving. Search professionals have spent years trying to secure the resources and cross-functional cooperation needed to make meaningful changes. Now, in some organizations, we have senior leadership listening, budgets for tools and content, and an unusual willingness to bring marketing, product, sales, and technical teams together.

We risk squandering that opportunity if the principal outcome we report is more AI visibility. Executives are investing because they believe AI is changing how customers discover and choose vendors. We need to show that our work is addressing that change, not simply producing another set of exposure metrics.

The starting point is the customer’s motivation. People generally aren’t going to ChatGPT, Perplexity, or Google’s AI-powered search experiences for another page of links. They want help understanding something, comparing alternatives, or reaching a decision. Traditional search left much of that work to the user, who had to visit multiple sites and reconcile the information. AI systems are attractive partly because they reduce that effort.

Yet we still talk about GEO as though the expected journey is a brand mention, followed by a click, followed by a conversion. That sequence may describe some purchases, but it is a poor model for many complex B2B decisions.

Fishkin’s argument drew on research from Marie Haynes, The SEO Works, and Kevin Indig. Although they approached the issue differently, their findings make it difficult to assume that increasing visibility in standalone AI assistants will necessarily produce meaningful traffic or conversions.

In GEO Is a Distraction, Haynes examined analytics data from 100 websites representing more than 145 million sessions. ChatGPT referrals accounted for approximately 0.62% of Google organic sessions, rising to 0.73% when other tracked language models were included. Even when ChatGPT visitors converted at higher rates, the difference in traffic volume meant that Google organic traffic remained far more important to measured conversions.

Her concern is partly one of priorities. Given the changes taking place within Google Search, how much effort should businesses devote to improving visibility in standalone assistants that currently contribute relatively little measurable traffic?

The SEO Works’ AI Referral Traffic Benchmark examined 95 websites and found that organic search generated 39 times as many sessions as AI assistants. For every 100 conversions attributed to organic search, AI referrals generated 3.2. The study also found considerable variation in conversion performance. Although AI referrals converted approximately 18% better overall, the rate for B2B product companies was only 0.62 times that of organic traffic.

That last finding caught my attention because B2B buying behavior is often discussed as though AI-assisted discovery will naturally produce exceptionally valuable visits. I agree it may, but only if the information is available, and I note that it might not be universally applicable.

Kevin Indig explored another possibility in Does AI Drive More Direct Traffic?. Perhaps AI influences buyers who subsequently visit a website directly or search for a brand, leaving the original influence unattributed. In an analysis of 12 analytics properties, he found that some apparent direct-traffic growth was due to bot activity. Additional testing across four brands and 632 tests did not establish a reliable relationship between AI visibility and subsequent direct or branded search activity.

Indig did not argue that AI has no influence on purchasing behavior. Rather, his work demonstrated how difficult it is to establish that influence using the signals available in conventional analytics.

I think these findings deserve more than a debate about whether GEO is worth the investment. They should prompt us to reconsider what we are asking visibility metrics to prove.

If a customer uses AI to compare vendors, rule out unsuitable products, or validate a shortlist, there may be no referral click. The customer could make substantial progress toward a decision without visiting any of the cited sources. That does not mean the AI interaction had no value, but it also does not mean every brand mentioned in the response benefited commercially.

The question I want to answer is what happened during the evaluation, particularly whether the company had enough supporting evidence to be considered a suitable choice.

See also: AI Visibility Measurement: What To Track & What To Ignore

A recent conversation with an executive brought this into focus for me. He was evaluating outside consulting support and explained that his organization had been disappointed by consultants and vendors three times in the previous year. He was not interested in another polished sales pitch or a broad claim of expertise. Having been burned repeatedly, he intended to make the next selection process considerably more demanding.

What stood out was his sense of responsibility for the decision. He needed to be confident that the next consultant could deliver, but he also needed to justify that confidence to the organization.

We sometimes underestimate how much of a complex purchasing decision is about avoiding risk. Buyers are trying to establish that a product will work in their environment, meet the requirements of different stakeholders, and avoid creating problems they will later have to explain. When budgets are tight, the consequences of getting that decision wrong become even more significant.

A company can be well-known, highly visible, and frequently mentioned without addressing those concerns.

Consider a buyer evaluating specialized software. Knowing that a vendor is a recognized provider may be useful at the beginning of the process. Once the buyer introduces requirements involving integrations, compliance, implementation capacity, or operational constraints, general category visibility becomes much less informative. The buyer needs evidence that the vendor can satisfy those requirements.

This is where I believe the distinction between appearing in an answer and earning a recommendation becomes commercially important.

See also: 3 Steps To Win B2B Brand Consideration, Day Zero Decides The Shortlist

We saw this problem in an engagement with a specialized B2B software company. Across the AI responses we observed, the brand was cited 98% of the time. By most visibility measures, that would be considered an excellent result. The company was clearly being recognized within its category.

At the same time, it was receiving little observable AI referral traffic, and its lead volume was declining. We could not establish that AI had caused the decline, but the situation prompted us to look more closely at what happened when buyers moved beyond broad-category questions.

We began testing more specific decision scenarios, introducing the requirements that a prospective customer might use to determine which vendors to include on a shortlist. Under those conditions, the company’s recommendation rate was 17%. In a separate testing condition where supporting evidence was absent, the rate was 8%.

As we examined the responses, we found that important capabilities were not always supported by information the AI systems could retrieve. In a workshop reviewing those gaps, someone on the client team said, “We have all of that. We just don’t have it on the website.”

I have heard variations of that statement throughout my career, but it takes on a different significance when customers can use AI to evaluate vendors before speaking with sales.

There are often legitimate reasons information is not publicly available. Some details are commercially sensitive. Some capabilities are complex enough that sales or technical teams prefer to explain them directly. Other information simply never makes it onto the website because nobody has recognized its importance to the buying decision.

Historically, a website could generate interest and leave the lead nurturing to sales. As research has shown, especially in B2B, AI-assisted evaluation often takes place before the buyer makes contact, creating a significant disadvantage. The company may satisfy the requirement, yet the available evidence may not be sufficient to establish that suitability.

The answer is not to just publish everything but to clearly identify the requirements customers are likely to evaluate and determine what credible, appropriately disclosed evidence can support those assessments.

See also: B2B Buyers Choose A Vendor Before They Reach Out – 3 Ways To Be Visible When It Counts

This is the thinking behind the Decision Coverage framework I introduced in Decision Coverage: Why AI Recommends Some Brands And Not Others.

In an earlier article, When AI Takes The Click, Click Worthiness Should Guide Your Strategy, I argued that marketers should focus more attention on interactions where direct engagement still adds meaningful value after AI has answered an informational question. Decision Coverage approaches the problem from the other side: before a customer engages directly, have we made enough evidence available for our company to be evaluated accurately?

One useful concept is the eligibility gate, a requirement that a solution must meet to be considered suitable for a particular buyer. This is a way to model purchasing decisions, not a claim that language models use a fixed internal checklist.

A buyer might regard some capabilities as desirable and others as mandatory. If a vendor cannot satisfy a critical requirement, it may not belong on the shortlist, regardless of how visible it is. But there is another possibility that marketers need to recognize: the vendor meets the requirement, yet the supporting evidence is missing or difficult to retrieve.

Traditional content-gap analysis does not always uncover this problem. We may find that a competitor has written about a topic we have not covered, or that our pages need stronger explanations of a product category. Those findings can improve discoverability, but they may have little bearing on whether a buyer can establish that we meet a mandatory requirement.

Decision Coverage starts with the decision itself. What is the customer trying to accomplish? Who is involved? Which requirements are essential, which are negotiable, and what trade-offs are likely to influence the choice? We can then examine whether the information available about the company is sufficient to support an assessment against those criteria.

This requires significantly more cooperation across the business than many traditional SEO activities. Marketing may understand the buyer persona, but product teams often hold the evidence needed to answer them, and most importantly, sales understands the prospects’ objections and evaluation criteria.

That is another reason I am concerned about wasting the current level of executive attention. We finally have an opportunity to bring these groups together around a problem that matters to customers. I would rather use that access to close important evidence gaps than simply produce more category content.

Our research into AI fan-out questions gave us another reason to focus on decision criteria. In one study beginning with a prompt asking for the best solution in a software category, 62% of the observed questions concerned understanding the category, developing evaluation criteria, or comparing vendors.

Those observations do not reveal a model’s complete internal reasoning, and I would not suggest that every AI system follows the same process. What they show is that the observable research supporting a recommendation can involve considerably more than retrieving a list of vendors.

We also found that recommendations sometimes changed when we altered the buyer’s perspective. A prompt framed around an individual’s professional responsibilities did not always produce the same recommendations as one written from the perspective of a department or organization.

In another test, a vendor appeared as the leading recommendation until we changed the emphasis and ordering of the requirements. That vendor then disappeared from the recommended set, replaced by a company that had not previously appeared but was particularly strong on the newly emphasized criterion.

I cannot attribute that change solely to the order of the requirements, as we saw other cases where retests days later prompted different interpretations, putting focus on different criteria. Nevertheless, the results made it difficult to defend the idea that recommendation performance could be represented adequately by a single broad prompt or a static collection of category queries.

That is why understanding decision context matters. A company may be the strongest option for one combination of requirements and less suitable when another constraint takes priority. Unfortunately, we don’t have unlimited budgets for every conceivable prompt, but we can maybe use larger diagnostic prompts that capture multiple prompt turns.

Accordingly, for measurement and alignment purposes, I would rather test a representative set of buyer decisions than simply increase the number of prompts being tracked. The objective is to understand when the company qualifies, where evidence is missing, and which competing vendors are better supported for particular requirements. Those are the critical insights we need to understand to truly close gaps.

Mike King also raises a related measurement issue in How Do We Move Forward if the SERP Is Generative?. As search results become dynamically generated, he argues that conventional assumptions about stable rankings are increasingly difficult to maintain. Measuring visibility requires repeated observations and a probabilistic approach rather than relying on a single result.

I agree, and I think recommendation measurement needs to account for another source of variation: the customer’s requirements.

A company might appear in nearly every broad category response but be recommended infrequently for the decision scenarios that matter most to its business. Another company might have lower overall visibility but be consistently recommended when its particular strengths align with the buyer’s needs. Aggregated mention counts cannot adequately explain either situation.

For our B2B software client, the evidence gaps suggested a testable hypothesis. If the company genuinely satisfies important buyer requirements but has not made that information retrievable, improving the available evidence should increase its recommendation frequency under comparable decision scenarios.

We can establish a baseline, address specific gaps, and repeat the testing. Because generated responses vary, that work needs multiple observations and consistent scenarios rather than a few favorable examples.

I want to be careful about what an improvement would mean, however. A higher recommendation rate would suggest that the available evidence is better supporting the company’s suitability. It would not prove that buyers are choosing the company more often or that the work has increased revenue.

That distinction matters especially for this client, whose sales cycle typically lasts 9 to 14 months. Even if better evidence starts influencing evaluations now, we may not see the corresponding effect on qualified opportunities or pipeline for several quarters. Some buyers may already have made their decisions while important information was missing.

We also have to recognize that the decline in leads could have several causes. Market demand, product competitiveness, sales execution, and other marketing activity may all be influencing performance. The combination of declining leads and weak recommendation performance is a reason to investigate, not evidence of causation.

I would therefore measure progress in stages. First, determine whether the gaps in decision-support evidence have actually been closed. Then test whether recommendation performance changes in the scenarios where those gaps mattered. Over time, examine whether qualified opportunities, pipeline, and eventually revenue show corresponding improvements, while considering other factors that could explain the results.

Customer interviews and self-reported discovery information may help fill some of the attribution gaps. They will not provide perfect answers, but they can help us understand whether AI-assisted research played a role in the buying process. This is consistent with Indig’s broader finding that neither referral traffic nor changes in direct traffic can explain AI’s commercial influence on their own.

I would not make AI referral traffic the primary KPI for this work. If the customer can reach an evaluation or shortlist decision without clicking, referral counts may tell us relatively little about whether our information helped or hindered that decision.

I think Fishkin is right to challenge the industry on business value. The findings from Haynes, The SEO Works, and Indig should make marketers cautious about presenting increased AI visibility as evidence of commercial success. King’s work also reminds us that the visibility we are trying to measure is becoming less stable and more dependent on context.

My concern is that we will respond by building increasingly sophisticated visibility dashboards without addressing what happens when a buyer asks whether a company is actually suitable.

For complex purchases, that question is often more important than whether the brand appeared in an earlier answer. Customers are trying to reduce risk, compare alternatives, and make defensible decisions. If AI systems are helping them do that work, our responsibility is to make sure the information available about our companies supports an accurate evaluation.

This is also an opportunity for search professionals to expand their contribution. The attention GEO is receiving has created access to people and resources that were often difficult to engage through conventional SEO initiatives. We can use that access to work with product, sales, technical, and legal teams to identify what customers need to establish and make appropriate evidence available.

I do need to know whether a brand is being discovered and represented accurately, but more importantly, I want to know whether the brand is recommended when it genuinely meets a buyer’s requirements, what evidence supports that recommendation, and where missing information may be limiting consideration.

If we can improve those leading indicators and then establish, over time, that they contribute to higher-quality opportunities and better commercial outcomes, we will have a much stronger argument for GEO investment than mention counts alone can provide.

The question is not whether AI visibility has value. It is whether we are confusing being present in an answer with being considered in a decision.

Inside Retro Reset, the "really inspirational" father and son project teaching kids about classic games hardware from aboard a converted bus

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"When they have written some code, nearly all of them have a big smile on their face"

At first the kids jostle to get to the games that they know. "Welcome to the Retro Reset Bus," says Luke Stoner as the students push to get to the screen that’s caught their eye. Sonic on the Sega Mega Drive is the first they see, and the quickest student to get on board the bus looks triumphant as he nabs the stool in front of the console. Jason, Luke’s dad, asks him what he thinks about the graphics – slightly grainy and slightly pixelated – but he’s not at all bothered and soon engrossed. After a few minutes, though, his eyes keep moving to the back of the bus where "Jackson is a wally" is now emblazoned across a flickering screen in seven different colours. Jackson then gives out a chuffed yell when his code finally works after a couple of restarts. His retort to his mate appears on the 1981 BBC Micro Model B‘s screen as the other students crane to have a look. This is, incidentally, the machine that turned a national broadcaster into a computer retailer (with help from Acorn), says Jason.

Luke’s knowledge is beautiful but the students also find him approachable. As Jason shares: "It’s really inspirational for kids coming onto a bus that they can talk to someone younger like Luke, who knows his stuff and has made something incredible happen." One student asks where Minecraft is and Luke explains that it doesn’t fit the parameter of being more than twenty years old yet, sharing details of when it was published and by who. Roblox could be included, but Luke isn’t keen because of recent controversies around safety. When this group are reluctantly herded off the bus by even more reluctant teachers, another heads in with just a little gap for resetting the machines and cramming a biscuit.

The bleeps and blips start from the machines as the students start playing. One teacher stops at the Atari 2600 and shares that her family has just bought her one. She is loving the daily dose of nostalgia. Luke and Jason move up and down the bus answering questions, resetting machines and showing the students their collection of old phones. One student winces at he is passed the bag that would have carried the battery for a Panasonic EB-3600 "brick" phone from 1989. When Jason says his dad had one, they give that look of pity reserved for anyone over 30 but are also incredulous that anyone would lug that around, especially when the phone could only do calls. When Jason asks if anyone would like to break an iPhone and have a look inside, all of the hands shoot up. He then talks about memory, RAM and chips, brings out a floppy disk; and tells the tale of where the expression "computer bug" came from. Still, it's the game on a tape - Jungle Story for the Commodore 64 - which they all turn away from screens to see. Perhaps we have Stranger Things to thank, but the tape has retro currency and they all think it’s sick.

The event is being run by East Sussex Careers Hub and is specifically aimed at students with SEND (Special Educational Needs and Disabilities). More than twenty-five schools turn up and most teachers leave with a flyer, chatting to colleagues about where the bus could park onsite. Jason says that the response to the bus, since Retro Reset's launch as a registered charity in August 2025, has been phenomenal, and they could easily fill a full week with visits. For now, he is juggling it with work commitments.

The bus was Luke’s idea and inspired by a visit to The Centre for Computing History in Cambridge, where visitors can get their hands on home computers and consoles from decades gone by. The family started thinking about what was possible and finally, after Luke finished college, began kitting out the bus: buying or accepting donations of hardware, researching which monitors would work with the ancient systems, and installing it all within the cabin.

On a basic level, the charity is telling the story of how technology has iterated. But by allowing visitors to take apart their machines, and giving brief lessons in coding with Basic, it also pulls the veil back on the work that goes into making a game - while opening students' eyes to what they could create themselves. "I guess it’s bridging the gap between the polished and finished latest game," Jason says, "which could be perceived as not achievable for them, to seeing how all that coding came about. Other than making sure they are really accurate with their typing; they realise that coding can be quite simple and something that they can actually do. I would go further and say that when they have written some code, nearly all of them have a big smile on their face." This, he hopes, might be the spark for some students to get into coding, especially for PC games, where under-18s are an underrepresented demographic.

It also hit the road as the popularity of retro gaming continued to surge. According to a Consumer Reports survey published last February, 14% of US gamers regularly play games made before 2000. This is reflected in the staggering stat that the retro gaming market is estimated to be worth $3.8 billion globally, with a projected rise to $8 billion by 2033. Retro-focused videos on YouTube and TikTok rack up millions of views of views, and communities formed on reddit or Discord - some with hundreds of thousands of members - discuss nothing else.

Nostalgia is playing a huge part. Atari’s Implicit Conversations is built on just this, creating retro games for modern consoles like PlayStation 5 or the Nintendo Switch. For Luke, though, this misses the point. He likes the click when you put a cartridge in a machine; and he likes that he is just him and the game. The parents of visiting students appreciate this too, and often share how overwhelming it feels to keep their kids safe in the online gaming environment. "Parents of younger children [tell us that] they hate that Minecraft and Roblox can allow their kids to play against perceived strangers. Even the more technical parents say they still miss safety measures and would prefer the games not be online at all," Jason explains.

Last month, Ofcom published its Growing Up Gaming summary report, which found that almost two-thirds (64%) of parents of three to 17 year olds who game online are concerned about their child talking to strangers through games or their chat functions. And, while the vast majority of young players go online without incident, it is true that those who use chat functions will likely interact with strangers. The same research found that more than half (55%) of children who game online say they play with people they have never met offline at least sometimes, while one in five (20%) do so all or most of the time they play.

By contrast, Jason claims that not one student has asked if they can get online, and he’s also never seen one get out their phone when they’re on the bus. Perhaps it is a welcomed break. "We offer a completely safe environment to come and experience these games and machines. That's really what we're here for," he states.

Luke shares too that he became nervous of the data he was sharing with gaming companies, and conscious that this was all being stored, thus making it vulnerable to breaches. He also baulked at what he felt was a constant money grab from gaming companies, including moves that render games unsupported or completely obsolete. Retro gaming is cheap in comparison and therefore more accessible, especially for younger players. "When you tell them that they can get an old console for less than £100 and often games come free when you buy them second hand, their ears really prick up," Jason says. "For a lot of the youngsters, the cost of modern gaming equipment is unattainable. And of course, we explain that they could take old computers and laptops and then load free operating software onto them. This gets them online, often for free. Once they are there, they can game."

John W. Champeau is founder of Champ Games, a Connecticut-based venture, which develops retro games system primarily for the Atari 2600. He has Scramble, Galagon, Gorf Arcade and Elevator Agent on his line-up. He agrees that price point is a huge part of the appeal of retro gaming. "Atari has released a line of "+" systems that are modern reinterpretations of the class Atari 2600 and 7800 hardware, but at a lower price point but with modern conveniences such as HDMI support, firmware updates, and new controllers," he notes. These systems can also play older cartridges that were released back in the day. "This means that the games themselves can be acquired at a low cost as well, sometimes even free if someone is lucky enough to know someone with a box of old Atari carts in their basement or attic."

But there may also be something intrinsically different about how these games are designed to be played that is driving their renaissance. In December 2025, Newsweek shared the views of two mental health professionals that "90s Video Games Were Better for Kids’ Brains." In what was sure to be a contentious piece (and perhaps that was the point), mental health counsellor Veronica Lichtenstein, and Melissa Gallagher, a licensed clinical social worker, argued that the very nature of gaming has changed and is now damaging. Retro games had a start and an end – and with the latter came a big hit of dopamine for achieving victory. Modern games are designed as "loops for addiction" with no real end; and constant prompts to buy. Satisfaction is constantly deferred. Lichtenstein also says that modern games leave little room for critical thinking, as a quick search online will delivers solutions if a player ever gets stuck.

Champeau agrees. "Our games are not designed to be addictive 'time stealers' like some modern video games that encourage players to 'stay online' for hours on end to acquire additional resources needed to complete a game or advance to a new level, etc," he says, adding: "All of our games are designed to be short burst distractions that don't rely on long gaming sessions to feel a sense of accomplishment.” He says that the lower graphics resolution and primitive sounds are actually a relief for many after years of hyper-realistic gaming. "Retro games distance the gap between game and reality, which was the intention of video games in the first place," he argues.

Back on the bus, Sonic may have attracted the initial crowds but it is now Pacman and the Binatone that the students are huddling around. Jason has given up one control for the tennis game to a student and a noisy competition is now taking place. The graphics are basic as is the premise, but scoring is tricky and the battle to get to 15 points hard-fought. The teacher laughs. "The bleeps from that game are bringing back memories," she says as she tries to encourage her students to finish and hand over the controllers. But with faces lit up by the screen and set in concentration, they don’t hear her. This is play for play’s sake, with no distractions, and could be why the games of the past might be just the future these kids need.

Sign in and join us on our journey to discover strange and compelling PC games.

Alex Prager Has Hollywood Dreams

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

With a feature film and major solo shows, the artist is experiencing a moment like no other.

Alex Prager was in her cubicle at an internet startup, staring at the endless rows of other desks, when she realized that something was terribly wrong. “The rest of my life flashed in my mind,” she says. She began to cry. “It was like that Talking Heads song: And you may find yourself in a beautiful house / With a beautiful wife / And you may ask yourself, / ‘Well…how did I get here?’” she says. “This was not my life.” Soon after, she attended a show of William Eggleston’s work and left certain that her future was in photography. Within a week, she’d bought a used Nikon N90s camera and darkroom equipment. “I just knew, the way that you know you have a face and you know who you love. I knew from that moment forward what my path was,” she says.

It didn’t take long for Prager to start getting noticed in art circles. And this winter will be one of the most significant seasons of her career, with both a major solo museum show at the Crystal Bridges Museum of American Art in Bentonville, Arkansas, and an exhibition at the New York gallery Lehmann Maupin. DreamQuil, her first feature film, was released in theaters this fall and will be available to stream in December.

Prager’s color-saturated works capture cinematic scenes that bring to mind classic Hitchcock films. She creates full worlds for her photos, with sets, casts, and impeccable period costumes. Her pieces gained notice with her 2007 show “Polyester” at L.A.’s Robert Berman Gallery. The series showed women, perfectly attired in ’60s-era looks, posed in evocative ways. A heavily made-up girl clutches a Big Gulp, a flaxen braid thrown over one shoulder; four young women huddle together in swimsuits, a plane flying across the perfect blue sky above. An ominous feeling pervades.

Her work sparkles on the surface but explores something darker underneath. “The first color photograph I took was staged and a play on The Wizard of Oz and death, but it was very colorful and beautiful. I was interested in the way to explore something that felt layered. Underneath it, you find there are very uncomfortable things or darker stories and emotions,” Prager explains.

Prager steadily built up a following through a series of gallery shows, but things changed markedly in 2010 when her work was selected to be shown in the elite “New Photography” exhibit at MoMA. She had her first solo show at the Corcoran Gallery of Art in Washington, D.C., three years later. She’s also done work for magazines and brands like Cartier, Disney, and Apple.

“My work has always been about my personal challenges and things that I’m not really understanding in the world, but I think more and more it becomes less about me and my emotions. That’s probably also me becoming a mother and growing up, but the world has changed,” says Prager, whose son Francis is 9. The scale of her work has grown—bigger audiences, more ambitious concepts, a greater ability to stage elaborate photography—but the approach hasn’t. It’s still about the questions she needs answers to.

The idea for DreamQuil came to Prager during the pandemic (she was inspired by a meme that advertised YearQuil, a fictitious product that would allow people to sleep through the misery of 2020), and she wrote the script with her sister, Vanessa Prager. Elizabeth Banks stars as a woman who’s living in a depressing future world where people rarely leave their homes and finds herself dealing with her robot clone.

Opening two major shows within months of the release of her film wasn’t something Prager anticipated—each alone would have been a high point in her career—but she often does her best work amid a frenzy of activity. “I thrive in a bit of chaos,” she says. “I like to try to mold it and wrestle with it and attempt to control it and bend it to my will. It doesn’t always happen exactly the way I want, but it infuses me with inspiration and energy.”

This story appears in the December 2026/January 2027 issue of ELLE.

Getting Ready With Jared McCain

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The chronically online NBA player’s routine is all about preserving his mental health. And yes, he has read your comments about his nails.

Jared McCain loves a routine—several routines, actually, which is why there’s a whiteboard in his bathroom, covered with a bulleted list of his daily to-dos (journaling, affirmations, bone broth, and lymphatic jumps, among them). That’s all separate from the 22-year-old’s bedtime and wake-up regimens (silk eye mask, silk pillowcase, silk bonnet, magnesium, and mouth tape, then a multistep skincare process, respectively). And this doesn’t even include many of the things his 7.7 million social media followers have come to love: Jared hanging out in de-puffing eye patches. Jared wandering around wearing Gen Z’s favorite pimple patches. Jared flashing his latest bright-colored manicure.

As he moves through his Oklahoma City home, where he lives with three of his best friends, it’s clear that none of the above is purely performative, as is so often the case with internet celebrities. “I do a lot of skincare because I sweat a lot,” he says as he works his way through what looks like the entire Hailey Bieber–founded Rhode skincare line. “I really like to take care of my face.”

Click through the videos on his TikTok or Instagram page and you’ll likely see that face illuminated by LED lights. “One of my favorite parts about my house is my red light showerhead,” he says. “Red light just creates this vibe and gives me health benefits. It’s number one in my life.” It’s also on his whiteboard three times.

Jared first broke into virality as a starting guard on Duke University’s basketball team. And while his talent on the court would lead him to the NBA—he now plays for the Oklahoma City Thunder—it was something else that captured the wider world’s attention. “I started painting my nails during COVID, and that’s when I also started on TikTok,” he explains. “I played with the polish on and I had a really good game, so I thought, Hold on, this might be the new thing.” (He now has brand partnerships with Sally Hansen and Ulta Beauty.)

“I didn’t really care what other people thought about it,” he says of any negative feedback he got at the time. He now keeps manicure inspiration cataloged on a Pinterest board—“most of the time, I’m feeling colorful; I love bright, bright colors”—and his Oklahoma City–based nail artist Yvie McCrary often sends him ideas to prep for his next appointment.

“In the NBA, there can be a lot of scrutiny, so I try to take care of my mental health,” he adds. In his downstairs “energy room,” he flicks on an LED panel (of course) for his daily yoga flow. “I love finding peace,” he says. “Meditation allows me to assess what’s going on in my life.” It’s yet another habit he uses to work through any unfavorable comments or haters he comes across: “It’s funny at this point. I’ve gotten used to it by now. It’s a blessing to even be talked about.”

Not that everything Jared does is 100 percent zen. When he’s not indulging in self-betterment practices, he’s posting TikTok dances and lip syncs from the Thunder’s locker room, sometimes with special appearances from his teammates.

“Entertaining has always been so fun to me,” he says. “That’s kind of what basketball is, too. Growing up, I thought I was going to be on the Disney Channel or a pop star. I always end up singing some Olivia Dean.” His plea for the singer to bring him onstage during her Art of Loving Tour stop at Madison Square Garden in New York City got nearly 2 million views (sadly, she did not invite him up onstage).

He launches into a rendition of Olivia’s “A Couple Minutes” with surprisingly impressive vocals. “The song could make me cry right now,” he confesses. One of his roommates later jokes, “We need a mute button”—something Jared’s fans have never asked for and likely never will.

<i><em>Blade Runner 2099</em></i> Is a Beautiful Slog Through a Grim Future

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The new Prime Video series picks up way, way after Denis Villeneuve’s sequel. Not that it matters.

Crowded streets. Long, cool coats. Flying cars darting through smog and neon skyscrapers. An abundance of Japanese aesthetics that imply America’s lost superpower status. Ridley Scott’s seminal 1982 masterwork Blade Runner (itself adapted from Philip K. Dick’s Do Androids Dream of Electric Sheep?) laid the foundation for almost all of cyberpunk science fiction, so much so that everything else feels undeservedly derivative. Even a new Blade Runner. The new series Blade Runner 2099 looks the part, but it lacks the soul of its predecessors, leaving its perspectives on what it means to be more human than human hollow, like a Replicant without memories of its own.

Streaming now on Prime Video (and just in time for a long holiday weekend) is Blade Runner 2099. A stand-alone continuation of both Ridley Scott’s towering original and Denis Villeneuve’s remarkable legacy sequel, the series takes place in 2099 (decades after Villeneuve’s Blade Runner 2049). An unseen revolution has elevated Replicants to the ruling class of Los Angeles, rendering flesh-and-bone meat-bag humans as the new discriminated minority.

Hunter Schafer flexes some of her budding leading-lady chops as Cora, a real human posing as a Replicant to smuggle herself and her brother out of Los Angeles. Her ruse leads her to Olwen (Oscar-winner Michelle Yeoh), a Replicant Blade Runner spending the final days of her artificial life span seeking out someone from her past. A humorless buddy-cop mystery, Blade Runner 2099 is mired in a plot-centric narrative that cares less about the why of all things—an underrated and beautiful feature of its predecessors—than the what and how.

In other words, it’s the first time a piece of Blade Runner media actually zeroes in on the plot, instead of anything else more interesting. Try not to think of this as an eight-hour Blade Runner series but rather an eight-hour series stretching at the seams to feel worthy of its branded title.

Is it because streaming TV in 2026 has become so plastic and hollow that even Blade Runner 2099 feels like an unengaging slog? Or were the direction of both Scott and Villeneuve so immaculate that we were distracted from how turgid they might have been all this time? Regardless, 2099’s lack of auteurship is its most deafening shortcoming. The series has been steered to the screen by executive producer Silka Luisa, whose warmer color palettes—based on her choice of more sunshine, which glistens off surfaces—lend 2099 some identity. Some.

But beyond even that minor distinction, Blade Runner 2099 is still just a plot-y crime drama that checks off boxes and goes through the motions of its genre, with nary a real thing to say. About us, about our fragile humanity, about the demise of all we’ve built through artificial intelligence. Blade Runner 2099 is a dystopian sci-fi in all the ways you expect it, but it’s hardly a good one. Sure, the original movies were oppressive, dreary, and morbid too. But they possessed a knowing glare into our fears about the future and our imminent collapse as a civilized species. The new series lacks this same insight, or indeed any of its own, all in the name of goosing up engagement numbers for the closest thing to a real-life Tyrell Corp. (Do shareholders dream of electric quarterly reports?)

Despite surface-level similarities to the movies, plus a moody noir-like motif with a sexy saxophone that, admittedly, completely and unapologetically rips, the plodding Blade Runner 2099 is a needless walkabout through a world that never begged for one. Needless is a good word, actually. The show’s strong visual execution is negated by its inability to rise to the occasion as a worthwhile or enlightening probe of the human experience, or even just as an entertaining cop show. It may be smart, but what good is intelligence if there isn’t an ounce of grace?

Astronomers discover the most distant and ancient supermassive black hole-powered quasar in the universe

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Latest space missions, discoveries and exploration updates.

Stargazing tips, cosmic events and expert insights on the universe

Sign up to our monthly entertainment newsletter to keep up with all our coverage of the latest sci-fi and space movies, tv shows, games and books.

Discover this week's must-see night sky events, moon phases, and stunning astrophotos. Sign up for our skywatching newsletter and explore the universe with us!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The supermassive black hole, known as Dún Aonghasa, is unlike any other seen at a similar period of the early universe.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Euclid space telescope has spotted the most distant and ancient quasar ever seen, a bright cosmic event powered by a supermassive black hole with 360 million times the mass of the sun.

The supermassive black hole is seen as it was just 630 million years after the Big Bang. 40 million years further back in time than the previous record holder for the most ancient quasar.

The discovery further deepens the mystery of how supermassive black holes grew so massive so soon into the history of the universe. That's because previously, scientists had theorized it should take 1 billion years of feeding and merging for black holes to reach supermassive status of millions or billions of times as massive as the sun.

Quasars occur when supermassive black holes are surrounded by vast amounts of gas and dust on which they feed. The immense gravitational influence of the black hole causes this material, in the form of an accretion disk, to glow brightly and to blast out bright jets of plasma. That means quasars at the heart of galaxies in regions called active galactic nuclei (AGNs) like this one can often be brighter than the combined light of every star in the entire surrounding galaxy, but only briefly.

The newly discovered quasar, designated J1504+6252 and nicknamed Dún Aonghasa, is in this brief phase of its existence. It was discovered among millions of objects in 18 months of data from Euclid.

Euclid's initial observation of Dún Aonghasa was followed up by an array of ground-based and space-based telescopes, including the W.M. Keck Observatory, the Large Binocular Telescope Observatory, the Hubble Space Telescope, and ultimately the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST). It was the JWST that determined the mass of this supermassive black hole.

What is interesting about Dún Aonghasa is that it is unlike other supermassive black holes seen at a similar period of the early universe. That's because this black hole is feeding relatively slowly. That means if it engaged in rapid feeding to reach its massive size, that period of gorging on gas and dust has either ended or it has paused.

That is something that further complicates our picture of supermassive black hole growth.

By staring back 13.1 billion years in time to see Dún Aonghasa, Euclid is also observing a crucial time in cosmic history when the first stars and galaxies were taking shape, and the clearing of cosmic gas was ending the dark ages of our universe by allowing light to travel freely.

Euclid's primary mission is improving our understanding of dark energy, the mysterious force accelerating the expansion of the universe, and dark matter, the most mysterious "stuff" in the cosmos. Collectively, these aspects of the universe account for around 95% of its matter-energy budget and have come to be collectively referred to as the dark universe. Hence Euclid's nickname as the "dark universe detective."

This research, along with Euclid's discovery of at least 12 that date back around 13 billion years, shows that this pioneering space telescope launched in 2023 could also make a major contribution to our understanding of black holes and their growth.

The team's research was published on Tuesday (Nov. 24) in the journal Astronomy & Astrophysics.

Robert Lea is a science journalist in the U.K. whose articles have been published in Physics World, New Scientist, Astronomy Magazine, All About Space, Newsweek and ZME Science. He also writes about science communication for Elsevier and the European Journal of Physics. Rob holds a bachelor of science degree in physics and astronomy from the U.K.’s Open University. Follow him on Twitter @sciencef1rst.

Manchester City started brightly under Enzo Maresca, but after terrible form of late, the club are considering the Italian's future

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Manchester City are considering the future of manager Enzo Maresca, just 19 games into the Italian's stint.

After Pep Guardiola led the Citizens to 20 trophies in a decade-long tenure, Manchester City turned to Maresca to take charge of the club, with the former Leicester City and Chelsea boss having worked at the Etihad as both the under-23s manager and as one of Guardiola's assistants in the 2022/23 Treble-winning campaign.

But with uncertainty shrouding the club – following an independent commission finding City guilty of over 100 breaches of serious financial rules across a nine-year period – things may be about to become even more unstable, should the six-time Premier League champions pull the trigger on their manager already.

Maresca started fantastically after taking over from his former mentor, winning his first seven Premier League games – including a defiant 3-0 thumping of Liverpool at Anfield following the news that City had been found guilty and were facing Premier League sanctions.

But the Eastlands outfit went on to lose the following three games in the league, drew against Fulham at the weekend, and had to rely on a late Rayan Cherki equaliser against Napoli last night in order to scrape a point in the Champions League.

Fichajes in Spain are reporting that Maresca “will be fighting for his job” if he doesn't beat Arsenal this weekend, with the Gunners having already outclassed his side 3-0 in the Community Shield earlier this season.

A defeat at the Emirates Stadium would widen the gap between the pair to a seemingly unassailable nine points, with Liverpool and surprise package Brighton & Hove Albion shaping up to be the champions' biggest threats in completing back-to-back league championships for the first time in almost a century.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

While City started the season emphatically, fans have questioned several tactical decisions – and after netting seven in his first seven fixtures, talismanic centre-forward Erling Haaland has cut a frustrated figure while leading the line.

Record signing Enzo Fernandez has blown hot and cold this season and kept the mercurial Cherki out of the side at times, Marc Guehi has started the season slowly with shaky performances from centre-back, and Phil Foden has been in disappointing form in the league, though he netted a hat-trick against AEK Athens in the Champions League.

Several of Guardiola's generals have been linked with exits, with Haaland's future in doubt, while Barcelona have interest in Josko Gvardiol to fill a problem position at left-back.

Despite the autumnal wobble, however, FourFourTwo understands that there are no plans at present to part with Maresca.

The Italian was handed a three-year deal over the summer to succeed Guardiola, with BBC Sport reporting that City paid around £17 million to Maresca's former club Chelsea as a compensation fee after he left the Blues in January 2026, following run of just one win in seven league games.

City's clash with Arsenal is set for 4.30pm this Sunday as Premier League action resumes.

China's memory giants have a record year as Huawei ships HBM without a named source

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

A record year for Chinese memory, while Huawei ships its own HBM without a named source.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

China’s homegrown memory comes largely from two companies, CXMT for DRAM and YMTC for 3D NAND, and both are booming. CXMT’s Oct. 30 quarterly report shows a nine-month net profit of ¥132.0 billion ($19.6 billion), against a ¥5.28 billion ($785 million) loss a year earlier, on revenue more than eight times as high, at ¥269.0 billion ($40.0 billion). YMTC’s numbers, just for Q1, are at ¥47.04 billion ($7.0 billion) in revenue and ¥33.38 billion ($5.0 billion) in profit attributable to shareholders, more than double 2025.

Both are expanding and adding fabs, and each is edging into the other’s products. No Chinese manufacturer can yet produce high-bandwidth memory (HBM) in volume, though. HBM is the stacked DRAM that AI accelerators depend on, and currently the U.S. bars Samsung, SK hynix, and Micron from selling it into China. CXMT’s prospectus lists no HBM project, though its HBM3E is reportedly in risk production, an early run before the process is fully qualified. Huawei designs its own HBM but doesn’t say who makes it.

Memory is a commodity, so the story is in the wafers. We cover the companies running memory fabs in China: CXMT, YMTC, a handful of smaller Chinese fabs, and Samsung and SK hynix, which run some of their biggest plants there. Huawei makes the cut for its HBM program, though it has no memory fab. Fabless designers, module makers, and NOR flash producers are left out, except XMC for its HBM line. Fab counts, products, and financials come from filings and company statements; capacities, planned fabs, and the HBM progress that remains the big question mark rest largely on anonymous reporting. Let's dive into the current state of the Chinese DRAM industry.

CXMT, short for ChangXin Memory Technologies, is China’s biggest homegrown DRAM maker. It operates two 12-inch fabs in Hefei and one in Beijing, each able to produce around 100,000 wafers a month. It is also building fabs in Shanghai and Hefei and is in funding talks for a second Beijing fab, which together could take it past 600,000 wafers a month. The Shanghai fab is expected to have two to three times the capacity of the Hefei base, with tools going in during the second half of 2026 and volume in 2027.

CXMT is now the fourth-largest DRAM maker worldwide, with 9.5% of DRAM revenue in Q2, according to TrendForce. CXMT says LPDDR6 is in mass production for Xiaomi, and its president says the first 4F² product, a server DDR5 RDIMM, is due by year-end. This comes after record first-half revenue of ¥150.3 billion ($22.4 billion), net profit of ¥77.6 billion ($11.5 billion), an 84.84% gross margin on its main business, and a listing on Shanghai’s STAR Market on July 27. Third-quarter profit alone was ¥54.4 billion ($8.1 billion).

The primary products are DDR5 rated up to 8,000 MT/s with 24Gb dies, and LPDDR5X rated up to 10,667 MT/s. CXMT says its fifth-generation G5 platform reaches 11.95nm active-area half-pitch with quadruple patterning for 24Gb LPDDR5X, and gets at least 50% more dies out of each wafer than G4, counted as 8Gb dies. CXMT says a DRAM product on its sixth-generation platform has also completed engineering-sample validation. Jeongdong Choe, Senior Technology Fellow at TechInsights, told Tom’s Hardware Premium that the firm measured the smallest active half-pitch on CXMT’s G4 at 14.8nm and hasn’t analyzed G5 yet.

CXMT lists no GDDR or 3D NAND among its products and is limited to nodes made without extreme ultraviolet (EUV) lithography as it lacks the tooling. CXMT’s second Beijing fab, however, is planned to include a 3D NAND R&D line, which could shake things up. The growth picture for now is capacity and price, with the tests of scaling without EUV lying with the RDIMM and Shanghai fab.

YMTC, short for Yangtze Memory Technologies Co., has two fabs of its own in Wuhan, which can produce about 200,000 wafers a month together. A third fab in the Xinzhou district, registered in September last year, is expected to produce about 50,000 wafers a month by 2027 and eventually 100,000 at full capacity. While YMTC is known for its NAND flash, roughly half of Fab 3’s output is earmarked for DRAM. The company also makes enterprise SSDs and UFS and eMMC parts, and sells consumer SSDs under its ZhiTai brand. YMTC filed its own STAR Market prospectus, to match CXMT’s, on Aug. 21.

NAND flash was, until recently, the star of the show for Chinese memory, and YMTC’s Xtacking technology is one reason why. Its fifth-generation Xtacking 4.0 die, used in 1Tb TLC and 2Tb QLC parts, has 294 gates, and Choe told us 267 of them are active word lines. Most of what YMTC ships, however, is still 160- and 232-layer NAND. Samsung licensed YMTC’s Xtacking bonding patents in February 2025, so the technology could be considered both proven and valuable.

Omdia data put YMTC at 38.5% of China’s NAND market in Q2, ahead of Samsung’s 33.5%. Globally, YMTC ranked third in NAND shipments in Q2, according to Counterpoint Research, and its share of NAND revenue rose from 9% to 14% between Q1 2025 and Q1 2026. YMTC’s stated long-term targets are about 20% of global NAND supply and about 500,000 wafers a month, DRAM included.

For DRAM, YMTC’s Fab 2 is running about 5,000 wafers a month of 16Gb LPDDR5, still in R&D and testing, with no plan for mass production. However, a 9,600 MT/s part on a vertical channel transistor (VCT) 4F² cell is slated for mass production at Fab 3 if it passes qualification. XMC, YMTC’s former foundry unit, left its control in July.

Two smaller fabs worthy of note are Fujian Jinhua and SwaySure. The former has a 12-inch fab in Jinjiang, which produces custom DDR4 on 25nm, at 40,000–50,000 wafers a month, with about 90% going to Huawei. DDR4 prices have surged this year, by as much as 50% in the first quarter alone, as the big suppliers wind DDR4 down in favor of server and HBM parts and enterprise buyers stock up.

SwaySure, a Shenzhen company founded in March 2022 with state-owned investment, is building a 28nm 12-inch DRAM line and is reportedly backed by Huawei. Analyst Jim Handy of Objective Analysis doubts the 28nm line could make HBM. Neither company responded to our requests for additional information.

Hefei Phase 1 and Phase 2 (Anhui); Beijing (Yizhuang)

Producing; about 100,000 wafers a month each

DRAM for servers, PCs, and cars, plus HBM lines

Under construction; due by the end of 2026

Hefei under construction; Beijing in funding talks

Producing; about 200,000 wafers a month combined

3D NAND; DRAM planned (9,600 MT/s LPDDR5 on a VCT 4F² cell)

Equipping; starts late 2026 or 2027; about 50,000 wafers a month by 2027

Wuhan, two 12-inch fabs and a reported HBM line

Specialty foundry: NOR flash, MCUs, 3D integration

HBM (a 3,000-wafer-a-month line under construction since February 2024)

Operating; out of YMTC’s control since July 2026

Producing; 40,000 to 50,000 wafers a month

Not every AI chip needs HBM. Inference hardware can get by on LPDDR5X, as Axera’s AX8860P does in Sipeed’s MaixStation, or on DRAM bonded straight onto the processor, the approach PieceMakers is betting on. Training-class accelerators like Huawei’s Ascend series still need HBM, though, and since the U.S. banned the export of HBM as of Dec. 2, 2024, China can’t buy it from the big three memory makers.

Even those who can get HBM are seeing heat and yield issues with larger memory stacks, specifically 12-high. SK hynix said on its Q3 2026 earnings call that some customers’ spec changes show “demand is indeed overwhelming supply,” and that lowering the stack is “a strategy to increase the number of cubes that can be obtained for the same capacity.” This mirrors other indicators, including potential Nvidia Rubin Ultra decisions, and it suggests that HBM is in short enough supply that even easier-to-build stacks are in high demand.

CXMT has sampled HBM with Tongfu, and HBM3 built on its 16nm-class G4 node to Huawei. Its HBM3E has now gone to several domestic AI chip designers as samples, with limited production due by the end of 2026, and an expansion expected in 2027. Yields are only about 20% to 30%, though, against about 70% at SK hynix, and in testing the chips performed closer to SK hynix’s HBM3 than to its HBM3E. An HBM packaging plant in Shanghai worth at least ¥17.1 billion ($2.5 billion) is targeted for year-end, with capacity for about 30,000 units a month.

Tongfu reportedly sampled HBM2, but its own filings mention only DRAM and flash packaging. Huawei said last year that “we will offer two proprietary HBMs.” It launched the Atlas 350 card in March, built on the Ascend 950PR, which carries 112GB of HBM. XMC has had a 3,000-wafer-a-month HBM line under construction since early 2024, but no production has been confirmed. At this time, YMTC, Jinhua, and SwaySure don’t appear to make HBM, though reports say YMTC is developing the through-silicon via (TSV) stacking it would need, and Jinhua was named in a Huawei-led HBM group in 2024.

Huawei said in 2025 that the 950PR would use HiBL 1.0, its own “low-cost HBM”; TechInsights told us it hasn’t analyzed HiBL 1.0 yet. However, Huawei’s 2025 roadmap specced out the 950PR's 128GB of HBM at 1.6TB/s. Huawei said that its 950PR is “optimized for the prefill stage of inference,” which is compute-heavy with “lower demand for memory access bandwidth,” and that using HiBL 1.0 is “more cost-effective than HBM3E and HBM4E” for that workload. The higher-end HiZQ 2.0, for the 950DT, is due in Q4 and, by Huawei’s figures, brings 144GB at 4TB/s. In both cases, Huawei’s supplier remains in doubt, and the company did not respond to our requests for comment.

HBM3 samples to Huawei; HBM3E sampling, with limited production by year-end at 20–30% yields

Under construction; due by the end of 2026

Shipping since March 2026 in the Atlas 350 (112GB); maker not disclosed

HiZQ 2.0, in the Ascend 950DT (144GB at 4TB/s)

Under construction since February 2024; production not confirmed

HBM samples with CXMT and HBM2 samples reported; filings show only DRAM and flash packaging

Doubtful on a 28nm line (analyst Jim Handy)

Of the companies from outside China, Samsung and SK hynix both run large memory fabs on Chinese soil, and both are now upgrading their NAND lines there. Samsung’s Xi’an handles about 40% of the company’s NAND, including 236-layer, or V8, in volume, with X2 converting to 286-layer, or V9, this year, slated to reach 5,000 V9 wafers a month in December and 40,000 by December 2027. A Samsung spokesperson told Tom’s Hardware Premium that the company “has been transitioning its existing manufacturing operations in Xi’an to 9th-generation V-NAND in compliance with U.S. export regulations and in close communication with the U.S. government.”

SK hynix’s Wuxi produces 30–40% of its DRAM and has moved from 1z to 1a, with the EUV layers done in Korea. Dalian turns out about 100,000 wafers a month of 192-layer floating-gate NAND and is moving to 240 layers, the most SK hynix is authorized to make there.

SK hynix, which already sells NAND with up to 321 layers elsewhere, told us it needs a U.S. license every year and declined to give product details or capacities for individual fabs. The company said that it intends for its 321-layer flash to be “over 50% of domestic (Korean) capacity in the fourth quarter,” which puts the Dalian lag into relief.

SK hynix also has “Mod B,” a second fab at its Dalian site that was completed in 2023. The company has reportedly tied a new U.S. NAND fab to Washington, letting it ship more U.S. tools there: Dalian’s 2026 license capped its equipment, no 2027 license had been issued as of mid-October, and its U.S.-based Solidigm unit has applied for an export license to buy more tools for Mod B. SK hynix told Tom’s Hardware that “no capacity expansion is being sought for the Dalian Mod B” and has been installing equipment at its Dalian factory.

Solidigm is now reportedly in talks with the U.S. Commerce Department over a $45 billion chip plant in the U.S., but wants Washington’s approval to ship equipment to upgrade the Dalian plant first, arguing that the upgrade would help finance the new site. However, SK hynix denied an earlier report of such a deal and says no specific plans have been finalized.

As for its Chongqing packaging plant, SK hynix told us it is “exploring various strategic options to enhance our packaging competitiveness,” though nothing is final. Elsewhere in China, Samsung’s Suzhou plant handles assembly and testing, and Micron’s Xi’an site handles packaging and testing.

All of this plays out inside a U.S.-China fight over chips that AI has only sped up. CXMT isn’t currently on the Commerce Department’s Entity List, which bars U.S. suppliers from shipping to listed companies without a license; YMTC, Jinhua, and SwaySure are. Jinhua has been on the list since 2018; SwaySure joined in December 2024 over its Huawei links, and YMTC was added in December 2022.

CXMT is, however, on the Pentagon’s 1260H list of Chinese military companies, which it is suing over. It’s been on the list since January 2025 and stayed on with the June update. For Samsung and SK hynix, the validated end-user status that let both firms ship in U.S. tools was revoked as of Dec. 31, 2025. Annual licenses are now required, and the Commerce Department has said it doesn’t intend to license capacity expansions or technology upgrades. Meanwhile, both companies say upgrades are under way, which sits uneasily with that policy.

China, for its part, has been weighing broader technology export controls and has now proposed adding 40 technologies, reportedly including some memory-chip design technologies, to its Catalogue of Technologies Prohibited and Restricted from Export. The draft, Announcement 48 from China’s Ministry of Commerce and Ministry of Science and Technology, is open for comment until Nov. 15, and anyone exporting a listed technology must register first.

About 40% of Samsung’s NAND (both Xi’an fabs)

Converting to V9; 5,000 V9 wafers a month in December 2026, 40,000 by December 2027

DRAM, mostly 1a; EUV layers done in Korea

Floating-gate 3D NAND, 192-layer, moving to 240

About 100,000 wafers a month; 30% to 40% of SK hynix’s NAND

Producing; upgrading, authorized up to 240 layers

Built in 2023; SK hynix says no expansion is sought; no 2027 license yet

Currently, China’s homegrown memory hinges on two manufacturers that are, admittedly, booming at the moment, although the memory industry worldwide has seen a massive surge since spring 2025. SK hynix said that “our DRAM and HBM volume for the next three years through 2029 are effectively sold out” with supply failing to keep up with demand as “a constraint projected to continue through 2030.” Its average selling prices rose from Q2 by mid-20% for NAND and high-20% for DRAM.

There may be some signs of at least temporary cooling in China’s consumer channel, though, as China Flash Market reports that SSD prices fell across the board this week, with most spot NAND wafers trading more than 30% below suppliers’ official prices. It looks like supply will remain a hot topic and China’s production capabilities are front and center.

CXMT and Huawei both have HBM milestones due by year-end: CXMT’s limited HBM3E production and Shanghai packaging plant, and Huawei’s 950DT with HiZQ 2.0. YMTC’s Fab 3 is due in late 2026 or 2027, and CXMT’s HBM3E expansion is expected in 2027. That leaves little time to ramp within 2027, especially at CXMT’s current yields, so we’re skeptical of volume HBM from a Chinese fab, whether measured against domestic demand or global supply, before 2028. Samsung and SK hynix, meanwhile, still need U.S. licenses for next year to keep tooling their Chinese fabs.

Shane Downing is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware, covering consumer storage, PC hardware, and AI.

The questions Jensen Huang and Nvidia will still have to answer about Megaspeed

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Jensen Huang’s return as CEO to Nvidia hasn’t eased the scrutiny the company confronts. The chipmaker still faces multiple inquiries into allegations that its advanced GPUs were smuggled into China by a customer, the Singapore-based data center operator Megaspeed, in violation of US export rules designed to slow China’s development of frontier AI models.

The central question for investigators is what Nvidia knew about where those chips ended up, and whether Huang’s affair with a Megaspeed executive gave the company reason to doubt Megaspeed’s assurances. Huang’s return doesn’t settle that. US Attorney General Todd Blanche has suggested Nvidia isn’t a target of an investigation, but California’s attorney general and Congress are pressing ahead, and the Securities & Exchange Commission is reportedly investigating, too.

The US government opened an investigation into Megaspeed in 2024 and requested information from Nvidia about its billions of dollars in sales to the company, concerned that some of the chips Megaspeed bought for data center projects in Malaysia were in fact being diverted to China. Singapore also began investigating similar allegations in 2025. Megaspeed has denied any wrongdoing.

Diversion of this kind would be a fairly routine issue for a company like Nvidia, whose in-demand chips are the targets of smugglers and re-sellers. When it comes to compliance and liability for export control violations, Nvidia and other chip sellers need assurances that the end users of their chips are obeying the rules. However, they also cannot ignore other information that might call the reliability of those assurances into question, export control attorneys told TechCrunch.

And that’s why Jensen Huang’s affair with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang was such a problem for Nvidia, likely even more so than her alleged role as a source for Chinese intelligence agencies. If Nvidia personnel, from lower-level employees up to the CEO, had reason to believe that statements from Megaspeed weren’t reliable, the attorneys said, that would increase the company’s legal risk.

“Government investigators likely would want to know, notwithstanding whatever formal assurances were exchanged between the parties, what anyone at Nvidia actually knew about the end use and end users of the chips,” Dan Fisher-Owens, an attorney at the firm Berliner Corcoran & Rowe, told TechCrunch last week.

The concern Nvidia’s board faced when it decided to oust Huang is that he knew more about what Megaspeed was doing with Nvidia’s products than was reflected in any of the compliance statements. The Wall Street Journal reported that Alice Huang and her husband, Kang Wei, were part of a network of businesses that funneled chips to a company in Shanghai. Kang also reportedly introduced his wife to Jensen Huang, whom he had known for a decade.

On Monday, the US government added Megaspeed and its affiliates to a list of companies restricted from US trade due to national security concerns. The US also issued new rules last week barring large data centers that purchase US chips from serving customers in China and other nations seen as problematic by the US government. Nvidia, meanwhile, said it would stop selling licensed chips to China, and laid out plans to hire its first compliance officer and create a new board committee focused on export controls.

Nvidia’s board said it reinstated Huang on Monday after receiving “input from senior levels of the United States government” that left it confident his return posed no national security risk. President Donald Trump later said he called into the meeting to give them that assurance.

In a news conference Tuesday, Blanche said that Nvidia was “a good corporate citizen” and suggested it is not the target of an investigation, but Blanche declined to answer questions about the government’s investigation into Megaspeed, which the Justice Department said was ongoing as recently as last week.

“When you say the export investigation is still ongoing, I don’t know what you’re talking about,” Blanche said.

Other authorities may be more demanding. Nvidia faces investigations by California Attorney General Rob Bonta and, reportedly, the SEC, both tied to its corporate disclosures. While many details of the investigation still aren’t known, short sellers complained about the company’s decision to stop breaking out its exports to Singapore in financial filings after journalists raised concerns about chip diversion.

Members of Congress from both parties say they want answers from Nvidia, with many citing alarm about China and some questioning the White House’s role in Huang’s return. Three committees in the House of Representatives will privately interview Huang and Nvidia’s top lawyer, Tim Teter, next week. Teter reportedly intervened in the Department of Commerce’s investigation of Megaspeed last year.

“We remain committed to getting answers on the mass smuggling of Nvidia products, their diversion for Chinese military purposes, and their key role in advancing our foremost adversary’s AI capabilities,” the Republican chairs of House committees focused on foreign affairs, intelligence, and China said in a joint statement.

Rep. Brian Mast, the chair of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, has also demanded that the Department of Commerce share extensive data regarding its investigation into chip smuggling, including by Megaspeed and its affiliates.

The Senate Banking Committee will also question Huang in a public hearing on Dec. 3.

“Mr. Huang, Nvidia executives, and the company’s board still owe Congress answers about the company’s compliance with U.S. export controls,” Senator Elizabeth Warren, the ranking Democratic member on the committee, said in a statement. “The Trump Administration also needs to explain to Congress what ‘input’ was given and by which senior officials in the United States government to support his reinstatement.”
